Learn English/Parts of speech/06-prepositions/catalog
On this pageABCDE–GH–IK–MO–PQ–RST–WVerbs that take no preposition at all

Dependent prepositions: verb patterns#

The prepositions you do not choose — the ones a particular verb simply requires, so that depend takes on, rely takes on, and discuss takes nothing at all.

← The grammar of prepositions · All categories

Every other file in this dictionary treats prepositions as words with meanings of their own — in is a container, under is a position, since is a starting point. This file treats them as grammar fixed by the word in front of them. Nothing about the meaning of on explains why English says depend on and not ✗ depend of, and nothing about the meaning of of explains why accuse takes it while charge takes with; these pairings are arbitrary, they differ from language to language, and they have to be learned one at a time the way irregular verbs are. What follows is the American English inventory of verb patterns, with the complement each one allows, the frames it appears in, and — the field to read first — what changes when you swap the preposition for another one.

One rule governs half the errors in this file. A preposition is followed by a noun, and when you want a verb in that slot it must become a gerund, the -ing form. That is why look forward to takes hearing and never ✗ hear, why object to takes being questioned, why resort to takes lying, and why succeed in takes raising rather than ✗ to raise. The test that settles every case: put a plain noun in the slot. If look forward to the meeting works, the to is a preposition, and any verb after it must end in -ing. Where a to-infinitive really is correct after one of these patterns — depend on him to be late, wait for her to finish, count on them to show up — it is licensed by the person sitting between the preposition and the verb, never by the preposition itself.

The gerund family, in full#

Any preposition in this file can be followed by a gerund. But the trap only bites where the preposition looks like the infinitive marker to, and those entries are worth learning as one block: look forward to, object to, resort to, confess to, admit to, commit to, dedicate to / devote to, get used to / get accustomed to, get around to, adapt to, react to, contribute to, lead to. Every one of them takes -ing after the to and never an infinitive: ✗ look forward to hear from you, ✗ object to be questioned, ✗ get used to work nights, ✗ resort to lie, ✗ committed to lower emissions.

Four more prepositions run the same trap without the disguise. In: succeed in takes raising, never ✗ to raise. Of: take care of takes getting it notarized, never ✗ to get it notarized. On: insist on and plan on take paying and staying, never ✗ to pay, ✗ to stay. From: prevent from, discourage from, and their whole family take -ing where their positive counterparts take an infinitive — encourage her to apply but discourage her from applying.

Two entries look like members and are not. Prefer to uses to for a comparison between two like things (prefer walking to driving) and separately takes a plain infinitive with no comparison at all (I'd prefer to walk). Subscribe to takes a noun and nothing else — ✗ subscribe to getting the paper. And hope for is the exact inverse of the whole family: it refuses the gerund, so you must switch frames and say hope to win.

The -ed words built on the same frame — accustomed to, used to, opposed to, committed to, dedicated to, devoted to — behave identically (accustomed to working nights, opposed to raising the fee) but are adjectives rather than verbs, so they are catalogued next door in dependent prepositions: adjective patterns rather than repeated here. The verbs that produce them are the entries above.

The sets worth learning first#

Nine of these entries are not single patterns but families, where changing the preposition changes what the sentence claims. Each carries a full contrast table.

hear about / from / of · agree with / to / on · look at / for / after / into · think about / of · talk to / with / about · wait for / on · care about / for · worry about / by · ask for / about

Three more meaning-changing sets belong to adjectives and live in the companion file: good at / for / with, sorry for / about, and bored with / of. The adjective different from / than / to is treated here, under differ from, because the verb and the adjective are constantly confused with each other.

And the last section of the file — verbs that take no preposition at all — is where the errors are most stubborn, because the noun almost always does take one: a discussion about it but ✗ discuss about it, an approach to it but ✗ approach to it, married to her but ✗ marry with her.

A#

abide by#

Pronunciation
/əˈbaɪd baɪ/ · uh-BYDE by · weak /əˈbaɪd bi/
Русский
соблюдать, подчиняться — принять правило или решение и следовать ему
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral with a legal tilt; contracts, court orders, club rules, and any promise to accept a decision
Complement
noun phrase naming a rule, decision, agreement, or outcome — the rules, the terms, the ruling, the results, our agreement. Never a person (✗ abide by the referee; you abide by his call), never a gerund (✗ abide by paying on time — say abide by the payment schedule), never a to-infinitive, and never a that-clause (✗ abide by that the vote was fair). The past tense is regular: abided by, never ✗ abode by.
Pattern
abide by [a rule / a decision / an agreement] · agree to abide by [the terms] · be required to abide by [something] · abide by [the results of a vote]
Contrast
Abide by is not the same verb as bare abide. With no preposition, abide means tolerate and lives almost entirely in the negative: I can't abide him — and there ✗ can't abide by him would be wrong. Against its neighbors, abide by accepts a decision or a promise that already exists, often with a note of good sportsmanship; comply with meets a requirement someone imposes; adhere to sticks to a standard or a plan over time; conform to matches a specification. Abide in, meaning dwell, is biblical and archaic.

Accept a rule or a decision and act accordingly, even if you did not want it.

  1. Every player signs a form promising to abide by the league's drug policy.
  2. Both parties agreed in advance to abide by the arbitrator's decision.
  3. If you rent here, you abide by the quiet hours like everyone else.
  4. The state said it would abide by the court's ruling while it prepared an appeal.
  5. She lost the primary and abided by the result without a word of complaint.
  6. Contractors must abide by the safety standards written into the bid.
See also
comply with · adhere to · conform to, conform with · agree to · by

absolve of#

Pronunciation
/əbˈzɑlv əv/ · ub-ZOLV uv · weak /əbˈzɑlv ə/
Русский
освобождать от (вины, обязанности) — снимать обвинение; юр./книжн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; law, official reports, and religion — an American meets it in a court opinion, in a newspaper sentence like the report absolved the mayor of any wrongdoing, or in a church, where a priest absolves a penitent
Complement
noun phrase naming blame, guilt, responsibility, liability, or a charge — of all blame, of responsibility for the collapse. A gerund is grammatical but rare (absolved of having signed it); never a to-infinitive (✗ absolved him of to pay) and never a that-clause (✗ absolved him that he was innocent). The passive carries most of the traffic: was absolved of. In the religious sense the preposition can drop entirely: the priest absolved her.
Pattern
absolve [someone] of [blame / responsibility] · be absolved of [wrongdoing] · absolve [someone] from [an obligation] · absolve [someone] — religious sense, no preposition
Variants
absolve from — the older form, still standard; American writing prefers of for blame and guilt (absolved of any wrongdoing) and keeps from for duties and obligations you are released from (absolved from further payments, absolved from her promise). Where the object is guilt, the two are interchangeable and of is the safer default.
Contrast
Four verbs clear a person, and they are not interchangeable. Acquit of is what a jury does when a formal charge fails; absolve of is broader and often moral, and needs no trial; clear of is the neutral journalistic word; exonerate stresses that the evidence proved innocence. And do not slide absolve of into relieve of: you are absolved of blame but relieved of a duty, a burden, or a command.

Declare someone free of blame, guilt, or an obligation.

  1. The internal review absolved the pilot of any responsibility for the landing.
  2. Signing the waiver does not absolve the gym of liability for broken equipment.
  3. Nothing in the settlement absolves the company of the duty to notify customers.
  4. He seemed to think an apology absolved him of everything.
  5. The bankruptcy court absolved her from the remaining medical debt.
  6. In the confessional the priest absolved him and sent him home.
See also
acquit of · convict of · accuse of · charge with · of

abstain from#

Pronunciation
/əbˈsteɪn frʌm/ · ub-STAYN frum · weak /əbˈsteɪn frəm/
Русский
воздерживаться от — намеренно не делать чего-то или не участвовать
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; parliamentary and corporate voting, medical instructions (abstain from solid food after midnight), and moral or religious discourse
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (abstain from commenting) — never a to-infinitive (✗ abstain from to comment) and never a that-clause. The verb also stands alone with no complement at all in the voting sense — three directors abstained — and there it is not a dependent-preposition pattern but a plain intransitive.
Pattern
abstain from [noun phrase] · abstain from [verb-ing] · abstain — no complement, in a vote · abstain from voting on [a motion]
Contrast
Abstain from and refrain from split by duration. Refrain from is a single act of holding back, usually on request and usually right now: please refrain from flash photography. Abstain from is a policy — a vote you sit out, a substance you give up for good: abstain from alcohol, and ✗ please abstain from flash photography sounds absurdly grand. Further along the same line, desist from is what a cease-and-desist letter demands, and forbear from is archaic outside legal prose. Note also that abstain never takes a direct object: ✗ abstain the vote.

Deliberately not do something, or not take part.

  1. Two board members abstained from the vote on the merger.
  2. Patients are asked to abstain from caffeine for twelve hours before the test.
  3. He has abstained from alcohol since the accident.
  4. She abstained from commenting on her opponent's finances.
  5. Observers at the talks abstained from taking sides in public.
  6. The union urged members to abstain from overtime until the contract was signed.
See also
prevent from · discourage from · retire from · from

accede to#

Pronunciation
/əkˈsid tu/ · uk-SEED too · weak /əkˈsid tə/
Русский
уступать, соглашаться на; присоединяться к (договору); всходить на (престол); книжн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; diplomacy and law above all — a country accedes to a treaty, a party accedes to a demand, a monarch accedes to the throne; an American meets the word in news coverage of negotiations, in contracts, and in history books, essentially never in conversation
Complement
noun phrase naming a request, a demand, a treaty, or an office. A gerund is grammatical and occasionally used (acceded to releasing the files), but never a to-infinitive (✗ acceded to release the files) and never a that-clause. There is no direct-object frame: ✗ acceded the request.
Pattern
accede to [a demand / a request] · accede to [a treaty / a convention] · accede to [the throne / the presidency] · accede to [verb-ing]
Contrast
The yielding verbs, from weakest resistance to strongest. Agree to is neutral and everyday; consent to grants permission, and is the word medicine and law use; assent to formally expresses approval; accede to gives in to pressure after resisting; acquiesce in accepts without approving and without objecting out loud. Two spelling traps: accede is not exceed (go beyond) and not concede (admit defeat, which takes a direct object or a that-clause, not to).

Give in to a demand or a request, or formally join a treaty or an office.

  1. After three weeks of picketing, management acceded to the union's demand for arbitration.
  2. Turkey acceded to the convention in 2004.
  3. The court declined to accede to the request for a sealed record.
  4. He acceded to the chairmanship when his predecessor resigned mid-term.
  5. She acceded to their wishes and let the story run without her name on it.
  6. Congress has never acceded to a proposal that broad.
See also
assent to · consent to · acquiesce in · agree to · to

account for#

Pronunciation
/əˈkaʊnt fɔr/ · uh-KOWNT for · weak /əˈkaʊnt fər/
Русский
объяснять; быть причиной; составлять (долю от целого)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral; heavily used in business, science, and reporting
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (how do you account for losing three days?) — never a to-infinitive (✗ account for to lose three days) and never a that-clause (✗ account for that sales fell; say account for the drop in sales). A percentage or share is the commonest complement of all: accounts for 40% of revenue.
Pattern
account for [a fact / a discrepancy] · [something] account for [a share or percentage] · be accounted for — be located and checked off · account for [verb-ing] · that accounts for [it]
Contrast
Account for and account to are different sentences. For takes the thing you explain or the share you make up; to takes the person or body you owe an explanation to: the treasurer accounts to the board for every dollar. Against explain, account for leans toward being the cause (the rain accounts for the low turnout) where explain leans toward telling (explain the rules to me), and explain takes its object bare: ✗ explain about the delay in careful writing. And allow for is a third thing again — it makes room in advance rather than explaining afterward.

Explain something, be the cause of it, or make up a given share of it.

  1. Overtime accounts for nearly a fifth of the department's budget.
  2. How do you account for the missing hour on the timesheet?
  3. Two states account for most of the country's almond crop.
  4. All twelve hikers were accounted for by Sunday night.
  5. A slow spring accounted for the weak first quarter.
  6. She could not account for having the key in her coat pocket.
See also
account to · allow for · explain to · answer for · for

account to#

Pronunciation
/əˈkaʊnt tu/ · uh-KOWNT too · weak /əˈkaʊnt tə/
Русский
быть подотчётным кому-то — обязан отчитываться о своих действиях; юр./офиц.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; corporate governance, fiduciary law, and organizational-chart talk — an American meets it in a partnership agreement, a trust document, or a court opinion about a trustee's duties
Complement
noun phrase naming a person or body you are answerable to — to the board, to shareholders, to Congress. Never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, never a that-clause. The thing being explained is added with a second preposition, for: account to the partners for every disbursement.
Pattern
account to [a person / a body] · account to [someone] for [something] · be required to account to [someone] · be accountable to — the adjective frame, far commoner in speech
Contrast
Account to takes the audience; account for takes the explanation. She accounts to the CFO* names her supervisor; she accounts for the variance names what she explains — and you can put both in one sentence, in that fixed order, account to X for Y. Never ✗ account for the CFO when you mean report to him. In ordinary speech Americans say answer to (he answers to the regional manager) or report to; account to survives mainly where a legal duty is at stake.

Be answerable to someone, and be obliged to explain your conduct to them.

  1. The trustee must account to the beneficiaries once a year.
  2. Under the partnership agreement, each partner accounts to the others for outside income.
  3. Agency heads account to Congress for how the appropriation was spent.
  4. He liked the job because he accounted to nobody but the owner.
  5. A general contractor accounts to the client, not to the subs.
  6. Directors who ignore a conflict of interest may have to account to the corporation for their profits.
See also
account for · answer for · explain to · belong to · to

accrue to#

Pronunciation
/əˈkru tu/ · uh-KROO too · weak /əˈkru tə/
Русский
накапливаться в пользу кого-то — доставаться (о выгоде, процентах); фин./юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; finance, economics, and law — an American meets it in a prospectus, an economics paper, or a contract clause about who gets the upside
Complement
noun phrase naming the party who receives the benefit — to shareholders, to the estate, to whoever holds the patent. Never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The subject is the benefit itself, never the recipient: the savings accrue to the buyer, ✗ the buyer accrues to the savings.
Pattern
[a benefit] accrue to [a party] · [interest / vacation days] accrue — intransitive, no preposition · [a gain] accrue from [a source] · accrue [expenses] — transitive accounting sense, no preposition
Contrast
Three prepositions, three roles. Accrue to names who gains; accrue from names where the gain comes from (the efficiencies accruing from the merger); and bare accrue just means pile up over time (interest accrues daily). In legal drafting the near-synonym is inure to, as in this warranty inures to the benefit of the buyer, which is older and stiffer but means the same thing. Do not confuse accrue with accrete (grow by addition of matter) or with ascribe.

Build up over time so that the benefit goes to a particular party.

  1. Most of the tax savings accrue to households in the top bracket.
  2. Any royalties accrue to the author's estate for seventy years.
  3. The benefits of the new interchange will accrue to the warehouses first.
  4. Interest accrues to the account holder from the date of deposit.
  5. Whatever prestige came out of the study accrued to the lab director.
  6. Rights that accrue to a tenant under this section cannot be waived by lease.
See also
amount to · account for · belong to · result in, result from · to

accuse of#

Pronunciation
/əˈkjuz əv/ · uh-KYOOZ uv · weak /əˈkjuz ə/
Русский
обвинять в — открыто заявлять, что кто-то поступил дурно
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (accused him of lying) — never a to-infinitive (✗ accused him of to lie) and never a that-clause (✗ accused him that he lied; for a clause you need a different verb, alleged that or claimed that). The person accused sits between the verb and of and can never be left out: ✗ accused of theft the manager.
Pattern
accuse [someone] of [noun phrase] · accuse [someone] of [verb-ing] · [someone] be accused of [noun phrase / verb-ing]
Contrast
accuse for and accuse about are errors — the preposition is fixed at of. Three verbs share this ground and none of them share a preposition: accuse someone of something (anyone can do it, publicly), charge someone with something (a prosecutor does it, formally), blame someone for something (assign fault, with no wrongdoing implied — you can blame the weather). Suspect someone of is the private, unspoken version of accuse.

Say openly that someone has done something wrong.

  1. They accused the contractor of padding the invoices.
  2. She was accused of plagiarism by two separate reviewers.
  3. Don't accuse me of something I didn't do.
  4. The senator accused the agency of stonewalling the committee.
  5. He was accused of leaking the memo, but nobody could prove it.
  6. Both sides accused the other of negotiating in bad faith.
See also
charge with · blame for · suspect of · admit to · of

acquiesce in#

Pronunciation
/ˌækwiˈɛs ɪn/ · ak-wee-ESS in · weak /ˌækwiˈɛs ən/
Русский
молча соглашаться с — не возражать, хотя и не одобряя; книжн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal and literary; court opinions and academic prose above all — the union acquiesced in the change for six years is the kind of sentence where an American actually meets it; in conversation the word sounds arch
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (acquiesced in being passed over) — never a to-infinitive (✗ acquiesced in to be passed over) and never a that-clause. The verb also stands alone with no complement (she acquiesced), and there is no direct object: ✗ acquiesced the plan.
Pattern
acquiesce in [a decision / an arrangement] · acquiesce in [verb-ing] · acquiesce — no complement · acquiescence in [something] — the noun keeps the same preposition
Variants
acquiesce to — now at least as common as in in American writing, especially where the object is a demand or a request (acquiesced to their demands). Careful legal and academic writers keep in for accepting a state of affairs, and to for yielding to a specific demand; either is defensible in general prose.
Contrast
Silence is the whole meaning. To acquiesce in something is to let it stand without objecting — approval is not implied and often is pointedly absent, which is why she acquiesced in the decision hints that she disliked it. Consent to grants permission, assent to expresses agreement, accede to gives in after resisting, and agree to is the neutral everyday word. The legal weight matters too: acquiescence over time can forfeit a right you never formally waived.

Accept something without protest, without necessarily approving of it.

  1. The board acquiesced in the chairman's decision rather than force a vote.
  2. For years the city acquiesced in the encroachment and lost the right to object.
  3. He acquiesced in the arrangement because fighting it would have cost more than it was worth.
  4. She would not acquiesce in being described as a junior author.
  5. Neighboring states quietly acquiesced in the annexation.
  6. The court held that the tenant had acquiesced in the landlord's use of the driveway.
See also
accede to · assent to · consent to · agree to · in

acquit of#

Pronunciation
/əˈkwɪt əv/ · uh-KWIT uv · weak /əˈkwɪt ə/
Русский
оправдывать по (обвинению) — признавать невиновным; юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; legal — this is a verdict word, and an American meets it in trial coverage, jury instructions, and appellate opinions, almost never outside them
Complement
noun phrase naming the charge or offense, or a gerund naming it (acquitted of killing his business partner) — never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause (✗ acquitted him that he was innocent). The passive is the normal frame, because the jury is usually left unnamed: was acquitted of all charges. The prepositions ✗ acquitted from and ✗ acquitted for are errors, though a count number takes on: acquitted on the first two counts.
Pattern
[a jury] acquit [someone] of [a charge] · [someone] be acquitted of [a charge] · be acquitted on [count / all counts] · acquit oneself well — an unrelated idiom, no preposition
Contrast
Acquit of and convict of are the two verdicts and they share a preposition — only the direction changes. Beside them, absolve of needs no court and often means moral clearing, and clear of is the neutral newspaper verb (cleared of any wrongdoing). Note what acquit does not claim: a verdict of not guilty says the state failed to prove its case, not that the defendant was innocent — which is why acquitted of and exonerated are not synonyms. Keep the reflexive idiom separate: he acquitted himself well in the interview means he performed creditably and has nothing to do with courts.

Find someone not guilty of a criminal charge.

  1. The jury acquitted him of both counts of fraud after four hours.
  2. She was acquitted of manslaughter but convicted of the weapons charge.
  3. A defendant acquitted of a crime cannot be tried for it again.
  4. The panel acquitted the officer of using excessive force.
  5. Two of the three defendants were acquitted of everything in the indictment.
  6. He was acquitted of stealing the equipment when the receipts turned up.
See also
convict of · absolve of · accuse of · charge with · suspect of · of

adapt to#

Pronunciation
/əˈdæpt tu/ · uh-DAPT too · weak /əˈdæpt tə/
Русский
приспосабливать(ся) к — менять себя или вещь под новые условия
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (adapt to working nights) — never a to-infinitive (✗ adapt to work nights, which says something else entirely). A wh-clause is fine after to whatever: adapt to whatever the market does.
Pattern
adapt to [noun phrase] · adapt to [verb-ing] · adapt [something] for / into [a new medium] · be adapted to [noun phrase]
Contrast
The trap is the gerund. Adapt to work nights reads as a purpose infinitive (adapt in order to work nights) and does not mean what a learner intends; the fixed preposition demands adapt to working nights. Keep adapt apart from adopt — you adapt to a climate but adopt a policy, a child, or an accent, and adopt takes no preposition at all. When the object is the thing being changed rather than the thing being fitted into, the preposition switches: adapt the novel for the screen, adapt it into a musical.

Change yourself, or change something, so that it fits new conditions.

  1. It took her a year to adapt to the altitude.
  2. Older workers adapted to the new system faster than anyone predicted.
  3. The species has adapted to living in brackish water.
  4. Small firms can adapt to a downturn more quickly than large ones.
  5. He never really adapted to being the boss.
  6. The curriculum was adapted to the needs of second-language students.
See also
react to · look forward to · get used to · cope with · to

add to#

Pronunciation
/ˈæd tu/ · AD too · weak /ˈæd tə/
Русский
добавлять к; усиливать — увеличивать количество или чувство
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase — a gerund is possible but rare (it only added to feeling shut out). Never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. In the two-object frame the thing added comes first: add sugar to the batter, never ✗ add to the batter sugar.
Pattern
add [something] to [something] · [something] add to [a quantity or a feeling] · add to [the confusion / the cost / the pressure]
Contrast
Add to increases something that already exists; add up totals a column of figures; add up to announces the sum or the upshot (it all adds up to a lousy quarter); and add up on its own, in the negative, means make sense (his story doesn't add up). Note that the simple transitive add takes no preposition when a clause follows — she added that the deadline had moved, never ✗ added about.

Put something in, or make an existing quantity or feeling greater.

  1. Add the zest to the dry ingredients before the butter.
  2. The delay only added to the cost of the project.
  3. Two more witnesses were added to the list.
  4. Her silence added to the awkwardness of the whole evening.
  5. Every new form adds to the paperwork somebody has to file.
  6. Add my name to the petition.
See also
contribute to · lead to · result in · in addition to

adhere to#

Pronunciation
/ədˈhɪr tu/ · ud-HEER too · weak /ədˈhɪr tə/
Русский
придерживаться чего-то; прилипать к — следовать правилу или плану; книжн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; policy manuals, audits, style guides, and clinical writing (adherence to the medication schedule) — in conversation Americans say stick to
Complement
noun phrase naming a standard, schedule, plan, principle, or surface — to the protocol, to the deadline, to the glass. A gerund is grammatical but stiff (adhered to using only the approved form); never a to-infinitive (✗ adhere to use the approved form) and never a that-clause. There is no direct-object frame: ✗ adhere the schedule.
Pattern
adhere to [a rule / a standard / a schedule] · [something] adhere to [a surface] · strictly adhere to [a protocol] · adherence to [something] — the noun keeps the preposition
Contrast
One verb, two lives. The physical sense is literal sticking — the label won't adhere to a wet bottle — and the figurative sense is following a rule over time. Against its neighbors: comply with meets a requirement someone else imposes and is the word regulators use; abide by accepts a decision already made; conform to matches a specification; stick to is the everyday spoken equivalent of the figurative sense and would sound out of place in an audit report. Note that adhere takes to in both senses and never ✗ adhere with or ✗ adhere on.

Stick to something — a rule, a plan, or a surface.

  1. Labs that adhere to the protocol get results everyone can replicate.
  2. The paint will not adhere to galvanized metal without a primer.
  3. She adheres to a strict rule about not reading email after nine.
  4. Few of the states adhered to the original timetable.
  5. The style guide asks writers to adhere to American spellings throughout.
  6. Patients who adhere to the regimen for a year do substantially better.
See also
comply with · abide by · conform to, conform with · commit to · to

admit to#

Pronunciation
/ədˈmɪt tu/ · ud-MIT too · weak /ədˈmɪt tə/
Русский
признавать(ся) в — соглашаться с тем, о чём предпочёл бы промолчать
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (admit to taking the money) — never a to-infinitive (✗ admit to take the money). The preposition is optional: admit taking the money is equally good. It disappears entirely before a clause: admit that he took the money, never ✗ admit to that he took it.
Pattern
admit to [noun phrase] · admit to [verb-ing] · admit [verb-ing] · admit that [clause] · be admitted to [a place, a school, a hospital]
Contrast
Two different verbs share the spelling. Admit to a crime is confessing; be admitted to a hospital, a college, or the bar is being let in — which is why She was admitted to Stanford is congratulations and She admitted to cheating is not. Against confess to, admit to is milder and covers small things (I admit to a weakness for cheap novels); confess implies guilt worth confessing. And unlike accuse of, admit can shed its preposition altogether before an -ing form.

Concede that something is true, usually something you would rather not say.

  1. He finally admitted to forging the signature.
  2. She admits to a certain nervousness about the whole arrangement.
  3. Nobody admitted to having seen the email.
  4. The company admitted to underreporting emissions for six years.
  5. I'll admit to being wrong about him.
  6. Two of the three suspects admitted to the burglary.
See also
confess to · accuse of · suspect of · boast about · look forward to

agree with, agree to, agree on#

Pronunciation
/əˈɡri wɪθ/ · uh-GREE with · weak /əˈɡri wəθ/
Русский
соглашаться — with разделять мнение, to принять предложение, on договориться о чём-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with takes a person, a group, or an opinion. to takes a noun phrase naming a proposal, a term, or a demand — and here is the trap: agree to do something is not this preposition at all but the infinitive marker, which is why agree to leave is correct while ✗ agree to leaving is not. on / about takes a noun phrase, a gerund, or a wh-clause (agree on what to charge). All three drop away before a clause: agree that the price is fair.
Pattern
agree with [person / opinion] · agree to [proposal] · agree to [verb — infinitive, not preposition] · agree on / about [topic] · agree with [someone] on / about [topic] · agree that [clause]
Contrast
This is the set that trips everyone. Agree with takes a side; agree to accepts something offered; agree on settles something jointly. I agree with the plan says you think it is right; I agree to the plan says you consent to it, whatever you think of it; we agreed on the plan says we hammered it out together. Two more things: ✗ I am agree is not English — agree is a verb, not an adjective — and ✗ agree me is not either. Agree with has a second life meaning suit: That shrimp didn't agree with me.

Share an opinion, consent to something, or settle something jointly — and English uses a different preposition for each.

PatternWhat it meansExample
agree with [person, view]share the opinionI agree with Dana.
agree with [food, climate]suit, sit well withLate coffee doesn't agree with me.
agree to [proposal, terms]consent, acceptThey agreed to our terms.
agree to do [verb]consent — infinitive, not prepositionHe agreed to testify.
agree on / about [topic]reach a joint decisionWe agreed on a date.
agree that [clause]state a shared belief — no prepositionEveryone agrees that it's late.
  1. I agree with you about the timeline, but not about the budget.
  2. The union agreed to the terms on Friday morning.
  3. We never agreed on a price, so the sale fell through.
  4. She agreed to stay another six months.
  5. The two economists agree about the diagnosis and disagree about the cure.
  6. Something at that potluck did not agree with me.
  7. Can we at least agree on what the problem is?
See also
decide on · comply with · object to · argue with · with · to

aim at#

Pronunciation
/ˈeɪm æt/ · AYM at · weak /ˈeɪm ət/
Русский
целиться в; стремиться к — направлять оружие или усилие на цель
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (aimed at cutting costs) — never a to-infinitive after at (✗ aimed at cut costs, ✗ aimed at to cut costs). Aim does take a bare infinitive directly, with no preposition: we aim to finish by June.
Pattern
aim [a weapon, a camera] at [target] · [a policy] be aimed at [a group] · be aimed at [verb-ing] · aim for [a goal] · aim to [verb]
Contrast
Three shapes, three meanings. Aim at points at a target, literal or figurative, and in the passive is the standard formula for describing what a policy is for: a program aimed at reducing recidivism — note the -ing, which is the single commonest error in this pattern. Aim for names a result you are trying to reach (aim for Tuesday). Aim to do is the plain infinitive of intention (we aim to please). Note also take aim at, which is now the standard newspaper idiom for attacking something publicly.

Point something at a target, or direct an effort toward a goal.

  1. The new rules are aimed at offshore accounts.
  2. She aimed the flashlight at the crawlspace.
  3. The campaign is aimed at getting first-time voters registered.
  4. That remark was aimed at me, and everyone in the room knew it.
  5. We're aiming for the end of the quarter.
  6. The grant program aims at closing the gap between rural and urban districts.
See also
point at · look at · concentrate on · prepare for · at

allow for#

Pronunciation
/əˈlaʊ fɔr/ · uh-LOW for · weak /əˈlaʊ fɚ/
Русский
учитывать, предусматривать — заранее оставлять запас на что-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (allow for having to reprint it) — never a to-infinitive (✗ allow for to reprint it). Do not confuse it with the bare transitive allow, which takes an object plus an infinitive and no preposition whatever: allow him to leave, never ✗ allow to him to leave.
Pattern
allow for [noun phrase] · allow for [verb-ing] · allow [someone] to [verb] · allow [verb-ing] · [a design] allow for [a possibility]
Contrast
Allow for means build in a margin for, or make provision for: allow for traffic, the budget allows for two hires. Bare allow means permit, and it is one of the verbs that takes an object plus a to-infinitive — ✗ allow to him and ✗ allow that he leaves are both errors. In the passive the two stay apart: He was allowed to leave (permitted) against Time was allowed for questions (set aside).

Take something into account in advance, or leave room for it.

  1. Allow for about forty minutes of traffic on a Friday.
  2. The estimate doesn't allow for the cost of permits.
  3. The schedule allows for one full day of testing.
  4. You have to allow for the fact that half the sample never responded.
  5. The design allows for adding a second story later.
  6. Even allowing for inflation, the increase is remarkable.
See also
consist of · provide for · prepare for · for

allude to#

Pronunciation
/əˈlud tu/ · uh-LOOD too · weak /əˈlud tə/
Русский
намекать на — упоминать, не называя прямо; книжн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal and literary; book reviews, literary criticism, careful journalism, and academic prose — in conversation an American says hint at or bring up
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (alluded to having been fired) — never a to-infinitive (✗ alluded to have been fired) and never a that-clause (✗ alluded to that he was leaving; either drop the preposition and switch verbs, hinted that he was leaving, or nominalize, alluded to his leaving). There is no direct-object frame: ✗ alluded the scandal.
Pattern
allude to [a person / an event / a text] · allude to [verb-ing] · an allusion to [something] — the noun keeps the preposition
Contrast
Allude to is indirect; refer to is direct. If you name the thing, you refer to it; if you gesture at it without naming it, you allude to it — which is why he alluded to her divorce means he never said the word. A writer who quotes Milton refers to him; one who echoes a line without a footnote alludes to him. Two traps come with it: elude means escape (the name eludes me), and an allusion is a hint while an illusion is a false impression.

Mention something indirectly, without naming it outright.

  1. The dean alluded to budget trouble but never used the word layoffs.
  2. Her acceptance speech alluded to the fight over the nomination.
  3. The title alludes to a psalm the author's mother used to recite.
  4. He alluded to having worked in the industry, though he gave no dates.
  5. Nobody at the table alluded to what had happened the night before.
  6. The opinion alludes to an earlier case without citing it.
See also
refer to · point at, point to · comment on · talk about · to

amount to#

Pronunciation
/əˈmaʊnt tu/ · uh-MOWNT too · weak /əˈmaʊnt tə/
Русский
составлять (сумму); быть равнозначным — по сути сводиться к чему-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase (a total, a sum, a characterization) or gerund (amounts to admitting the whole thing) — never a to-infinitive (✗ amounts to admit the whole thing) and never a that-clause. The verb has no passive: ✗ was amounted to. Its subject is the thing being totaled or characterized, never the person: the charges amount to $4,300.
Pattern
[figures] amount to [a total] · [an action] amount to [a characterization] · amount to [verb-ing] · not amount to much · never amount to anything
Contrast
Two senses, one preposition. The arithmetic sense adds up (the repairs amounted to nine hundred dollars); the evaluative sense says what something really is (that amounts to a confession), and it is the one that carries an edge. Add up to is the informal twin of both; come to is the spoken one at a cash register (that comes to $12.40); and equal takes no preposition at all (two plus two equals four, ✗ equals to four). The fixed phrase never amount to anything is the only common use with a human subject.

Add up to a total, or be equivalent to something in effect.

  1. The unpaid invoices amount to almost half the quarter's revenue.
  2. Refusing to answer amounts to an admission as far as the committee is concerned.
  3. Her share of the estate amounted to a few thousand dollars and a piano.
  4. What the policy amounts to is a pay cut for everyone under fifty.
  5. Three years of complaints amounted to nothing.
  6. The changes amount to rewriting the contract in the buyer's favor.
See also
add to · account for · accrue to · result in, result from · to

answer for#

Pronunciation
/ˈænsər fɔr/ · AN-sur for · weak /ˈænsər fər/
Русский
отвечать за — нести ответственность за что-то или ручаться за кого-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral, with a moral or legal edge — answer for his crimes is courtroom-adjacent, a lot to answer for is everyday
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (answer for letting the permit lapse) — never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. In the vouching sense the complement is a person or a quality: I can answer for her, answer for his honesty.
Pattern
answer for [something you did] · answer for [verb-ing] · have a lot to answer for · answer for [someone] — vouch for them · answer to [a person] — report to them
Contrast
Three related patterns pull apart cleanly. Answer for takes the deed you are accountable for; answer to takes the person you are accountable to (she answers to the board); and plain answer takes the question and no preposition at all — answer the question, ✗ answer to the question. Beside them, account for is about explaining and carries no blame, while answer for implies you may have to pay. The vouching sense is a separate idiom: I'll answer for him means I will stand behind him, the same job vouch for does.

Take responsibility for something, or stand behind someone.

  1. Somebody is going to have to answer for the missing deposit.
  2. He will answer for those decisions at the hearing next month.
  3. The airline has a lot to answer for after that weekend.
  4. I can answer for her — she has worked for me for nine years.
  5. She refused to answer for choices her predecessor had made.
  6. Nobody was ever made to answer for the spill.
See also
answer · account for · account to · blame for, blame on · pay for · for

apologize to, apologize for#

Pronunciation
/əˈpɑləˌdʒaɪz tu/ · uh-POL-uh-jize too · weak /əˈpɑləˌdʒaɪz tə/
Русский
извиняться — to перед тем, кого обидел, for за то, что сделал
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to takes a person or a group. for takes a noun phrase or a gerund (apologize for being late) — never a to-infinitive (✗ apologize for to be late). The person and the offense need different prepositions and cannot share one: ✗ apologize to being late, ✗ apologize for you.
Pattern
apologize to [person] · apologize for [noun phrase / verb-ing] · apologize to [person] for [noun phrase / verb-ing] · apologize (alone)
Contrast
Apologize someone is an error — the person always needs to, unlike thank, forgive, and excuse, which take the person as a direct object. Compare: I thanked her but I apologized to her; forgive me for that but I apologize to you for that. The noun follows the verb: an apology to the family for the delay. Note also that apologize for an act is not the same as the older, formal apologize for a position, which means defend it — that sense survives mostly in the noun apologist.

Say you are sorry — to the person wronged, for the thing you did.

  1. He apologized to the entire staff for the way he'd handled it.
  2. She apologized for missing the deadline and offered to work the weekend.
  3. You owe him an apology; go apologize to him.
  4. The airline apologized for the four-hour delay and refunded nothing.
  5. I apologize for the confusion in my last message.
  6. Nobody ever apologized to the family.
See also
thank for · forgive for · excuse for · blame for

appeal to#

Pronunciation
/əˈpil tu/ · uh-PEEL too · weak /əˈpil tə/
Русский
нравиться кому-то; взывать к кому-то; подавать апелляцию в (суд)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral; the legal sense is formal
Complement
noun phrase naming a person, group, court, or principle — never a gerund after to (✗ appeals to buying local; the attraction sense takes a person: buying local appeals to me). A to-infinitive after the person is licensed by the person, not by the preposition: appealed to donors to cover the gap. The thing requested takes for: appealed to the state for emergency funds.
Pattern
[something] appeal to [someone] — be attractive · appeal to [someone] for [something] · appeal to [someone] to [verb] · appeal to [a court] · appeal [a ruling] — American transitive legal use, no preposition
Contrast
Appeal to and appeal against divide by variety and by role. American lawyers appeal a decision transitively — she appealed the verdict — and use to for the court they take it to: appealed to the Ninth Circuit. Appeal against the verdict is the British construction and reads as an import in American writing. Keep the everyday senses apart too: the offer appeals to me is about attraction and has no request in it, while I appealed to him is a plea. And appeal to a principle (appealed to fairness) is arguing from it, not asking it for anything.

Be attractive to someone, ask them earnestly for something, or take a case to a higher court.

  1. The color scheme appeals to almost nobody under forty.
  2. Organizers appealed to the governor for National Guard support.
  3. He appealed to the committee to reopen the application window.
  4. The defense appealed to the state supreme court and lost again.
  5. That argument appeals to people who have never had to make payroll.
  6. She appealed to his sense of loyalty, which turned out to be a mistake.
See also
apply for, apply to · ask for, ask about · object to · long for · to

apply for, apply to#

Pronunciation
/əˈplaɪ fɔr/ · uh-PLY for · weak /əˈplaɪ fɚ/
Русский
подавать заявление на (for); относиться к, распространяться на (to)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for takes a noun phrase naming what you want (a job, a permit, asylum). to takes a noun phrase naming the body you approach, or the thing a rule covers. Neither takes a gerund: ✗ apply for working there — say apply for the job. Apply does take a to-infinitive of purpose (she applied to study nursing), which is the infinitive marker, not this preposition.
Pattern
apply for [a job, a grant, a visa] · apply to [an institution] · apply to [an institution] for [a thing] · [a rule] apply to [people] · apply [something] to [a surface] · apply [oneself] to [work]
Contrast
Apply for names the prize; apply to names the door you knock on: she applied to four schools for financial aid. A third sense of apply to has no applicant in it at all — the rule applies to everyone means it covers them, and that is the sense in does not apply. Keep it away from apply meaning spread on (apply the second coat), which takes a direct object and to.

Make a formal request, or — in a separate sense — be relevant to something.

  1. She applied for the fellowship twice before she got it.
  2. He applied to six graduate programs and heard back from two.
  3. You have to apply for a permit before any demolition starts.
  4. The overtime rule applies to salaried employees as well.
  5. Apply to the registrar's office, not to the department.
  6. None of that applies to contractors.
See also
ask for · qualify for · refer to · consult with

approve of#

Pronunciation
/əˈpruv əv/ · uh-PROOV uv · weak /əˈpruv ə/
Русский
одобрять — считать что-то правильным или приемлемым
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund, or possessive plus gerund (approve of his leaving early) — never a to-infinitive (✗ approve of to leave early) and never a that-clause (✗ approve of that he left).
Pattern
approve of [noun phrase] · approve of [verb-ing] · approve of [someone] -ing · approve [a budget, a plan, a drug] — no preposition
Contrast
The preposition changes the verb completely. Approve with a direct object and no preposition means authorize: the board approved the budget, the FDA approved the drug. Approve of means think well of, and carries no authority at all: her father doesn't approve of him. So The committee approved the proposal is a decision and The committee approves of the proposal is an opinion. Disapprove has only the of sense — there is no transitive ✗ disapprove the budget in careful American usage; use reject.

Think well of something, or find it acceptable.

  1. Her parents never approved of the marriage.
  2. I don't approve of airing this in a staff meeting.
  3. He approves of the goal but not the method.
  4. Nobody approves of being lectured at.
  5. The neighbors did not approve of the color she painted the porch.
  6. She approves of his taking the semester off.
See also
disapprove of · object to · agree with · agree to

argue with, argue about, argue over#

Pronunciation
/ˈɑrɡju wɪθ/ · AR-gyoo with · weak /ˈɑrɡju wəθ/
Русский
спорить — with с кем-то, about/over о чём-то; также доказывать мысль
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with takes a person or a group. about and over take a noun phrase, a gerund, or a wh-clause (argued about who would pay) — never a to-infinitive. Argue that takes a full clause and no preposition (she argued that the survey was flawed), and argue for / against takes the position being urged.
Pattern
argue with [person] · argue about / over [topic] · argue with [person] about [topic] · argue for / against [a position] · argue that [clause]
Contrast
Argue to someone is an error in the quarreling sense — you argue with people. About and over are interchangeable for the topic, though over leans toward something contested and divisible (argue over the check, over custody) and about toward a subject (argue about politics). The formal sense is different again: argue for a position means make the case for it, and argue that is what appears in a brief or a paper, where nobody is quarreling with anybody.

Quarrel, or make a case.

  1. They argued about money for most of the marriage.
  2. Don't argue with the umpire; it never works.
  3. The two sides argued over the wording for three hours.
  4. She argued that the study's control group was too small.
  5. He argued with his brother about the estate until neither would speak.
  6. The paper argues for a much narrower reading of the statute.
See also
agree with · complain to · compete with · object to

arrive at, arrive in#

Pronunciation
/əˈraɪv æt/ · uh-RIVE at · weak /əˈraɪv ət/
Русский
прибывать — at в точку (вокзал, дом), in в город или страну
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause. The one thing arrive never takes is to (✗ arrive to the airport). Before home, here, there, and abroad it takes no preposition at all: arrive home, never ✗ arrive at home.
Pattern
arrive at [a building, a point, an event, a small place] · arrive in [a country, a city, a large area] · arrive [home / here / there] · arrive at [a decision, a conclusion, a figure]
Contrast
Arrive to is the single most common preposition error made with this verb, imported from Spanish llegar a and French arriver à. The choice between the two correct options is the same one in and at make everywhere: in for a space you end up inside (arrive in Chicago, in Brazil), at for a point on the map (arrive at the hotel, at Gate 12, at the wedding). Compare reach, which is transitive and takes no preposition at all — reach the airport, ✗ reach to the airport — and get to, which is the everyday spoken form of both.

Come to the end of a journey.

  1. We arrived at the trailhead a little after six.
  2. She arrived in Denver on the last flight of the night.
  3. The package finally arrived at the office.
  4. They arrived in the country with two suitcases and no plan.
  5. He arrived home to find the power out.
  6. The jury arrived at a verdict in under two hours.
See also
reach · in · at · to

ascribe to#

Pronunciation
/əˈskraɪb tu/ · uh-SKRYBE too · weak /əˈskraɪb tə/
Русский
приписывать кому-то — авторство, причину или свойство; книжн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; scholarship, art history, criticism, and serious journalism — an American meets it in a museum label (a panel long ascribed to Giotto) or an academic paper
Complement
the thing ascribed is a direct object; to takes the person, work, or cause it is assigned to. A gerund is possible in the cause sense (ascribed the delay to being understaffed) but never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. The object cannot be omitted: ✗ ascribed to Giotto the panel garbles the order, and ✗ ascribed to carelessness alone is incomplete.
Pattern
ascribe [a quality / a cause / a work] to [someone or something] · be ascribed to [someone] · ascribe [an outcome] to [verb-ing]
Contrast
Ascribe to and attribute to are near-twins. Attribute* is the commoner word and does everything; ascribe leans toward assigning qualities, motives, and authorship, and sounds more bookish. Two neighbors sharpen the picture: impute to is the legal and moral version and is nearly always unflattering (imputed bad faith to the seller), and credit with reverses the frame and is always positive (credited her with the idea). Watch the spelling trap: subscribe to an idea means to hold it (I don't subscribe to that theory), which is not what ascribe to means at all.

Assign a quality, a cause, or an authorship to someone or something.

  1. Historians ascribe the collapse to a mix of drought and bad debt.
  2. The sonnet was ascribed to Shakespeare for two centuries.
  3. She ascribes her recovery to stubbornness more than to medicine.
  4. Don't ascribe motives to people whose reasoning you haven't heard.
  5. The auditors ascribed the shortfall to being three staffers short all spring.
  6. Powers ascribed to the office by custom are not powers granted by statute.
See also
attribute to · credit with · blame for, blame on · subscribe to · to

ask for, ask about#

Pronunciation
/ˈæsk fɔr/ · ASK for · weak /ˈæsk fɚ/
Русский
просить о чём-то (for); расспрашивать о чём-то (about)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for takes a noun phrase naming what you want. about takes a noun phrase, a gerund, or a wh-clause (asked about getting a refund, asked about what it would cost). Neither takes a to-infinitive — but ask itself does, in a separate frame with no preposition: ask someone to do something, ask to leave. The thing asked is a direct object, not a prepositional one: ask a question, ask me something.
Pattern
ask for [a thing] · ask [someone] for [a thing] · ask about [a topic] · ask [someone] about [a topic] · ask [someone] [a question] · ask [someone] to [verb] · ask if / whether [clause]
Contrast
Ask to me and ✗ ask a question to me are errors — the person is a direct object (ask me a question), which is why the two-preposition instinct from Romance languages misfires here. Within the set: ask for requests (ask for a raise), ask about inquires (ask about the raise), and ask after someone — a health inquiry — is the one member Americans mostly replace with ask about. Note that ask for someone means request to speak to them, so There's a man at the door asking for you is not about a favor. And You asked for it is a fixed idiom meaning you brought it on yourself.

Request something, or inquire about something.

PatternWhat it meansExample
ask for [thing]request itShe asked for more time.
ask for [person]request to speak to themA reporter is asking for you.
ask about [topic]inquire about itHe asked about the refund policy.
ask after [person]inquire about their health (formal, less common in the U.S.)She asked after your mother.
ask [person] [question]no prepositionAsk me anything.
ask [person] to [verb]request an action — infinitiveThey asked us to wait.
  1. She asked for a week's extension and got three days.
  2. He asked about the health insurance before he asked about the salary.
  3. Somebody at the front desk is asking for you.
  4. I asked about transferring the deposit, and nobody knew.
  5. Don't be afraid to ask for help.
  6. They asked about how long the warranty runs.
  7. Ask the pharmacist about the interaction before you take both.
See also
apply for · search for · hope for · hear about · wish for

assent to#

Pronunciation
/əˈsɛnt tu/ · uh-SENT too · weak /əˈsɛnt tə/
Русский
давать согласие на — формально одобрять; офиц./книжн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; parliamentary, diplomatic, and philosophical writing — the governor assented to the compromise, royal assent, assent to a proposition — and stiff enough that in speech an American simply says agreed to
Complement
noun phrase naming a proposal, request, terms, or a proposition. A gerund is possible but rare (assented to being interviewed); never a to-infinitive (✗ assented to be interviewed, which means something else — that is a passive infinitive after agreed) and never a that-clause. The verb also stands alone: she assented with a nod. There is no direct object: ✗ assented the plan.
Pattern
assent to [a proposal / terms] · assent — no complement · give one's assent to [something] · assent to [a proposition] — the philosophical sense, accept it as true
Contrast
Five verbs of saying yes, and they are not interchangeable. Agree to is the everyday word; consent to grants permission and is what medicine and law require (consent to treatment, consent to the search); assent to formally expresses approval, often of a document or a proposition; accede to is yielding after resistance; acquiesce in is not objecting, with no approval implied. Do not let the spelling slip to ascent, which is a climb.

Formally express agreement or approval.

  1. The board assented to the revised bylaws without debate.
  2. Both governments assented to the wording of the communiqué.
  3. She assented to the interview only after her lawyer read the questions.
  4. The bill became law when the president assented to it.
  5. No serious scientist assents to that proposition anymore.
  6. He assented to the change in venue but asked for a week's delay.
See also
accede to · consent to · acquiesce in · agree to · to

attest to#

Pronunciation
/əˈtɛst tu/ · uh-TEST too · weak /əˈtɛst tə/
Русский
свидетельствовать о — подтверждать истинность или служить доказательством; юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; legal and documentary — a notary attests to a signature, a witness attests to what she saw, and academic prose uses as the record attests
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (attested to having seen him leave) — never a to-infinitive (✗ attested to have seen him leave). A that-clause is fine only after the preposition is dropped: attested that the copy was genuine, never ✗ attested to that the copy was genuine. Legal drafting also uses a bare transitive frame: attest a will, attest the signature.
Pattern
[a person] attest to [a fact] · [a thing] attest to [a quality] · attest to [verb-ing] · attest that [clause] — no preposition · attest [a document] — legal transitive
Contrast
Attest to accepts an inanimate subject; testify to mostly does not. The lines outside attest to the show's popularity is idiomatic; ✗ the lines testify to is strained, because testifying is something a person does under oath in a proceeding. Beside them, swear to is a personal oath (I'd swear to it), and vouch for backs a person's character rather than a fact. Note that the preposition disappears before a clause, which is where learners most often add it.

State or confirm that something is true, or serve as evidence of it.

  1. Two coworkers attested to his whereabouts that afternoon.
  2. The bank requires a notary to attest to every signature on the transfer.
  3. The wear on the steps attests to three centuries of foot traffic.
  4. She attested to having warned the supervisor twice.
  5. Her colleagues attest to how hard she worked that year.
  6. Anyone who has driven that stretch in February can attest to the potholes.
See also
confess to · account for · answer for · refer to · to

attribute to#

Pronunciation
/əˈtrɪbjut tu/ · uh-TRIB-yoot too · weak /əˈtrɪbjət tə/
Русский
приписывать кому-то/чему-то — называть источник, причину или автора
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; standard in science, journalism, and museum catalogs
Complement
the thing attributed is a direct object; to takes the cause, person, or source — a noun phrase or a gerund (attributed the delay to being short-staffed). Never a to-infinitive (✗ attributed the delay to be short-staffed) and never a that-clause. Both slots are obligatory in the active: ✗ attributed to the weather with no object is incomplete; the passive fills the gap instead — the delay was attributed to the weather.
Pattern
attribute [something] to [a cause / a person] · be attributed to [someone] · attribute [an outcome] to [verb-ing] · attributable to — the adjective keeps the preposition
Contrast
The three assignment verbs split by tone. Attribute to is neutral and works for causes, quotes, and paintings alike; blame on is the negative version (blamed the delay on the weather); credit with is the positive one and flips the frame, putting the person first (credited the pilot with the landing). Ascribe to is the bookish twin of attribute to, and impute to is its legal, usually unflattering cousin. Watch the stress: the verb is uh-TRIB-yoot, the noun AT-ruh-byoot.

Say that something was caused by, said by, or made by a particular source.

  1. Investigators attributed the fire to a failed extension cord.
  2. The quotation is usually attributed to Twain, who never said it.
  3. She attributes her stamina to twenty years of swimming.
  4. The panel was attributed to a follower of Rembrandt, not the master.
  5. Analysts attribute the rebound to falling interest rates.
  6. He attributed the mistake to being handed the file an hour before the meeting.
See also
ascribe to · credit with · blame for, blame on · result in, result from · to

avail oneself of#

Pronunciation
/əˈveɪl wʌnˈsɛlf əv/ · uh-VAYL wun-SELF uv · weak /əˈveɪl wənˈsɛlf ə/
Русский
воспользоваться чем-то — прибегнуть к предложенной услуге или праву; книжн.
Type
verb + reflexive object + preposition
Register
formal; official letters, contracts, and court opinions — tenants who avail themselves of this remedy, please avail yourself of the shuttle — and pompous in ordinary speech, where Americans say use or take advantage of
Complement
a reflexive pronoun matching the subject is obligatory, then of plus a noun phrase naming an opportunity, service, right, or remedy. A gerund after of is grammatical but clumsy (availed himself of being allowed to appeal); never a to-infinitive. There is no plain transitive frame: ✗ avail the opportunity, ✗ avail this service.
Pattern
avail [oneself / yourself / themselves] of [an opportunity] · not avail oneself of [a remedy] · to no avail — a fixed noun phrase, unrelated frame
Contrast
The reflexive is the whole difficulty. American usage requires it: avail yourself of the discount, and ✗ avail of the discount — very common in Irish and Indian English — reads as an error in American writing. The everyday equivalent is take advantage of, which is neutral about opportunities (take advantage of the sale) but accusatory about people (took advantage of her); avail oneself of never carries that second reading. Note also the survival of the old intransitive in to no avail, meaning without success.

Make use of an opportunity or a service that is offered to you.

  1. Employees may avail themselves of the tuition benefit after one year.
  2. Few defendants avail themselves of the right to a speedy trial.
  3. Guests are welcome to avail themselves of the airport shuttle.
  4. She never availed herself of the appeal the contract allowed her.
  5. The statute lets small businesses avail themselves of an expedited filing.
  6. I availed myself of his offer of a spare room for the week.
See also
take care of · benefit from · qualify for · of

B#

bear on#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɛr ɑn/ · BAIR on · weak /ˈbɛr ən/
Русский
иметь отношение к — быть относящимся к делу; также давить на что-то; книжн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; legal briefs, academic argument, and editorial writing — evidence bearing on the question of intent; the everyday paraphrase is have to do with
Complement
noun phrase naming a question, issue, or matter — on the question, on the outcome, on our decision. A gerund is possible but rare (bears on deciding who pays); never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. The subject is the evidence or consideration, never the person: ✗ I bear on the question. In the physical sense the subject is a weight or force: the load bears on the center beam.
Pattern
[evidence / a fact] bear on [a question] · have a bearing on [something] — the noun idiom, far commoner in speech · [a load] bear on [a structure] · bring [resources] to bear on [a problem]
Variants
bear upon — identical in meaning, one degree more formal, at home in legal and philosophical prose
Contrast
Do not confuse the preposition with the particles. Bear on means be relevant to; bear down on means advance threateningly (the storm bore down on the coast); bear with means be patient (bear with me); bear out means confirm (the data bore out her hunch), and unlike a fixed preposition its object can move — bore the theory out. Note also the separate frame bring to bear on, which applies force or resources rather than merely being relevant. In speech, have a bearing on almost always replaces the verb.

Be relevant to something, or press physically on it.

  1. Nothing in the deposition bears on the question of who signed first.
  2. Those findings bear directly on how the city should spend the settlement.
  3. The court excluded testimony that did not bear on intent.
  4. Two factors bear on the decision: cost and timing.
  5. The whole weight of the roof bears on four columns.
  6. His early letters bear upon everything he wrote afterward.
See also
refer to · depend on · comment on · apply to · on

believe in#

Pronunciation
/bɪˈliv ɪn/ · buh-LEEV in · weak /bɪˈliv ən/
Русский
верить в — считать существующим или ценным
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (believe in taking the stairs) — never a to-infinitive (✗ believe in to take the stairs) and never a that-clause (✗ believe in that it works; drop the preposition and say believe that it works).
Pattern
believe in [a thing's existence] · believe in [a person] · believe in [verb-ing] · believe [someone / something] — no preposition · believe that [clause]
Contrast
I believe you and I believe in you are two different sentences. Bare believe plus an object means accept what was said as true; believe in means hold that something exists, or trust its value, or have confidence in a person. I believe her is about one statement; I believe in her is about her character. Likewise Do you believe the report? asks whether it is accurate, and Do you believe in the report? is barely English. Before a clause the preposition always goes: believe that, never ✗ believe in that.

Hold that something exists, or trust its value or its worth.

  1. She doesn't believe in ghosts, but she won't stay in that house alone.
  2. I believe in paying people before the invoice is due.
  3. He believes in the program even though the numbers are flat.
  4. My grandfather believed in a cold shower every morning of his life.
  5. Believe in yourself is easy advice and hard work.
  6. Half the committee doesn't believe in the forecast at all.
See also
rely on · count on · depend on · approve of · in

belong to#

Pronunciation
/bɪˈlɔŋ tu/ · buh-LAWNG too · weak /bɪˈlɔŋ tə/
Русский
принадлежать кому-то; состоять в (организации); быть на своём месте
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase only — no gerund, no infinitive, no clause. Belong is a stative verb and does not take the progressive: ✗ This is belonging to me. When the sense is proper place rather than ownership, the preposition changes to in, on, with, or under, and can never be to: these belong in the drawer, ✗ these belong to the drawer.
Pattern
[something] belong to [an owner] · [someone] belong to [a club, a party, a generation] · [something] belong in / on / with [its proper place] · belong (alone — feel at home)
Contrast
Ownership takes to; location takes in, on, or with. That coat belongs to Marcus names the owner; That coat belongs in the closet says where it goes. Membership also takes to (she belongs to the bar association), but note that Americans more often say is a member of. Bare belong with no complement means feel accepted — I never belonged there — and is one of the most common uses of the verb in ordinary speech.

Be owned by someone, be a member of something, or have a proper place.

  1. That truck belongs to my father-in-law.
  2. She belongs to three different birding groups.
  3. The copyright belongs to the estate, not the publisher.
  4. Those files belong in the archive, not on your desktop.
  5. Which of these keys belongs to the shed?
  6. He never felt he belonged at that school.

benefit from#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɛnəfɪt frʌm/ · BEN-uh-fit from · weak /ˈbɛnəfɪt frəm/
Русский
извлекать пользу из — получать выгоду от чего-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (benefit from having a second reader) — never a to-infinitive (✗ benefit from to have a second reader). Note the American spelling of the inflections: one t — benefited, benefiting.
Pattern
[someone] benefit from [something] · benefit from [verb-ing] · [something] benefit [someone] — no preposition · be of benefit to [someone]
Contrast
The verb runs both directions and the preposition tells you which. Farmers benefited from the subsidy means they gained; The subsidy benefited farmers means it helped them — transitive, no preposition. Getting these backwards (✗ the subsidy benefited from farmers) reverses the sentence. Benefit by is older and now rare in American prose; from is the live form. The noun pattern is different again: a benefit to the community.

Gain an advantage from something.

  1. Small clinics benefited from the change more than hospitals did.
  2. The manuscript would benefit from another round of cuts.
  3. She benefited from having a mentor early in her career.
  4. Who actually benefits from a rule like that?
  5. Coastal towns benefit from the tourism and pay for it in traffic.
  6. Everyone benefits from clearer instructions.

blame for, blame on#

Pronunciation
/ˈbleɪm fɔr/ · BLAYM for · weak /ˈbleɪm fɚ/
Русский
винить — blame smb for smth = blame smth on smb, аргументы меняются местами
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for takes a noun phrase or a gerund (blamed her for losing the file) — never a to-infinitive. on takes the person or thing held responsible. The two frames carry the same meaning with the arguments swapped, so each preposition goes with a fixed slot: ✗ blame the delay for the weather says the opposite of what you mean.
Pattern
blame [someone] for [something] · blame [something] on [someone] · [someone] be to blame for [something] · be blamed for [something]
Contrast
Same meaning, mirrored frames: They blamed the pilot for the crash = They blamed the crash on the pilot. Keep blame apart from accuse and charge: blame assigns fault and needs no wrongdoing at all (you can blame the rain), while accuse someone of alleges misconduct and charge someone with files it formally. The adjective frame keeps for: the contractor is to blame for the leak, never ✗ to blame of.

Say that someone or something is responsible for something bad.

  1. Don't blame the intern for a policy she didn't write.
  2. He blamed the missed deadline on the printer.
  3. The report blames the outage on deferred maintenance.
  4. She was blamed for everything that went wrong that summer.
  5. Nobody is to blame for an honest mistake.
  6. They blamed each other for the whole mess.

boast about#

Pronunciation
/ˈboʊst əˌbaʊt/ · BOHST uh-bowt · weak /ˈboʊst əbət/
Русский
хвастаться чем-то — говорить о своём с излишней гордостью
Type
verb + preposition
Register
somewhat formal; brag about is the everyday word
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (boasted about winning) — never a to-infinitive (✗ boasted about to win). A clause takes no preposition: boasted that he had won.
Pattern
boast about / of [noun phrase / verb-ing] · boast that [clause] · [a place or thing] boast [a feature] — no preposition
Contrast
Two senses split on the preposition. With about or of, boast means brag, and it is unflattering. With a direct object and no preposition it means possess something worth showing off, and it is flattering — the hotel boasts three restaurants is advertising copy, not a criticism of the hotel. Boast of is a shade more formal than boast about; both are correct. In speech Americans overwhelmingly say brag about.

Talk with too much pride about something you have or have done.

  1. He boasts about his commute the way other people boast about their kids.
  2. She never boasted about the award; we found out from the newsletter.
  3. They boast of a hundred percent placement rate, which is technically true.
  4. Boasting about how little you sleep is not the flex you think it is.
  5. The company boasted about the acquisition for a year and then wrote it off.
  6. The lodge boasts a wraparound porch and no cell service at all.

borrow from#

Pronunciation
/ˈbɑroʊ frʌm/ · BAR-oh from · weak /ˈbɑroʊ frəm/
Русский
занимать, брать взаймы у — брать на время с возвратом
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause. The thing borrowed is a direct object and the source takes from: borrow a ladder from Ken. ✗ borrow of is archaic and ✗ borrow to someone reverses the direction entirely.
Pattern
borrow [something] · borrow [something] from [source] · borrow [an idea, a word, a style] from [a source] · borrow against [an asset]
Contrast
Borrow and lend point in opposite directions and are constantly swapped. You borrow from someone; they lend to you. ✗ Can you borrow me twenty dollars? is nonstandard in American English — the sentence you want is Can you lend me twenty dollars? or Can I borrow twenty dollars from you? Note that borrow from also covers influence (the score borrows heavily from Copland), and that borrow against a house or a policy means take a loan secured by it.

Take something temporarily, intending to give it back.

  1. I borrowed a folding table from the church next door.
  2. She borrowed the phrase from an essay she'd read in college.
  3. Small businesses are borrowing from family instead of banks.
  4. Can I borrow your charger?
  5. The design borrows from Shaker furniture without copying it.
  6. He borrowed against the equity in the house to cover tuition.
