care about, care for#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɛr əˌbaʊt/ · KAIR uh-bowt · weak /ˈkɛr əbət/
Русский
care about считать важным; care for ухаживать за; not care for не любить
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
about takes a noun phrase, a gerund (cares about winning), or a wh-clause (cares about what people think) — never a to-infinitive. for takes a noun phrase only. Care also takes a bare to-infinitive with no preposition (care to join us?), and a that-clause in the negative (I don't care that it's raining).
Pattern
care about [person / thing / issue] · care for [someone — tend] · care for [something — like, usually negative] · would you care for [a thing] · care to [verb] · take care of [someone / something] · not care (alone)
Contrast
Four patterns, four meanings, and they are not interchangeable. Care about = think it matters (she cares about the outcome). Care for = look after (he cared for his mother for six years) or, chiefly in the negative and in offers, like (I don't care for oysters; Would you care for coffee?). Care to do = be willing. Take care of = handle or look after, and is what Americans actually say for the tending sense. Note the idiom: I couldn't care less is the logical form, and the widespread American I could care less means exactly the same thing despite saying the opposite.

Think something matters, or look after someone — depending entirely on the preposition.

PatternWhat it meansExample
care about [thing]think it mattersShe cares about accuracy.
care for [person]tend, nurse, look afterHe cared for her after the surgery.
care for [thing]like — mostly in the negativeI don't care for jazz.
Would you care for [thing]?polite offerWould you care for dessert?
care to dobe willing — infinitiveCare to explain?
take care of [thing]handle it, look after itI'll take care of the check.
  1. Nobody on that committee cares about the deadline.
  2. She cared for her father at home until the last month.
  3. I don't care for his tone.
  4. He cares about getting it right more than getting it done.
  5. Would you care for another slice?
  6. They care about what happens to the building after they sell it.
  7. Take care of the paperwork and I'll handle the movers.
See also
take care of · look after · worry about · think about

center on, center around#

Pronunciation
/ˈsɛntər ɑn/ · SEN-tur on · weak /ˈsɛntər ən/
Русский
быть посвящённым чему-то, вращаться вокруг — иметь главной темой
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral; center on is the safe choice in edited prose, center around the one a copy editor may query
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (the argument centered on raising the cap) — never a to-infinitive (✗ centered on raise the cap) and never a that-clause (✗ centered on that costs were rising), though a wh-clause is fine: the hearing centered on what the city knew. The participial form is where most Americans meet it: a novel centered on one summer.
Pattern
[a discussion / a story] center on [a topic] · center around [a topic] · be centered on / centered around [something] · center [something] on [something] — transitive, position it
Contrast
Center around is the famous usage quarrel. The objection is geometric: a center is a point, and nothing can be around a point — so the purist demands center on* or revolve around. The defense is that the phrase has been in respectable American prose since the 1860s and that center here means a cluster, not a dot. Practical advice: in a report, a brief, or a paper, write center on and lose nothing; in speech, center around passes unnoticed. Two neighbors do related work — focus on narrows to one thing, revolve around names what everything else moves in relation to.

Have something as its main subject or its main point.

  1. The debate centered on whether the district could afford both schools.
  2. Her research centers on migration between the wars.
  3. The whole weekend centered around the wedding rehearsal.
  4. The complaint centers on two paragraphs in the disclosure.
  5. Most of the questions centered on the timing of the layoffs.
  6. It is a quiet novel centered on one family and one street.
See also
concentrate on · comment on · depend on · bear on · on

charge with, charge for#

Pronunciation
/ˈtʃɑrdʒ wɪθ/ · CHARJ with · weak /ˈtʃɑrdʒ wəθ/
Русский
обвинять в, поручать (with); брать плату за (for)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
charge with is legal or formal; charge for is everyday
Complement
with takes a noun phrase or a gerund (charged with defrauding investors) — never a to-infinitive. for takes a noun phrase or a gerund naming the service billed (charged us for parking). Neither takes a clause.
Pattern
charge [someone] with [an offense / verb-ing] · be charged with [a duty] · charge [someone] for [a service] · charge [an amount] for [a thing] · charge [something] to [an account] · charge at [someone]
Contrast
Three prepositions, three unrelated meanings. Charge with files an accusation (charged with arson) or assigns a responsibility (the panel is charged with reviewing appeals — note the -ing). Charge for bills money. Charge to puts it on an account. And charge at is a bull. Against its neighbors: accuse of is what a person does, charge with is what a prosecutor does, blame for assigns fault with no proceeding at all. In American criminal usage the person is charged with the crime, never ✗ charged of or ✗ charged for it — charged for would mean billed.

Accuse formally, assign a duty, or bill money.

  1. He was charged with two counts of wire fraud.
  2. The clinic charged us for a visit we never made.
  3. The committee is charged with recommending a candidate by June.
  4. How much do they charge for delivery?
  5. She charged the whole dinner to the department.
  6. Prosecutors declined to charge anyone with a felony.
See also
accuse of · blame for · pay for · suspect of · with

cheat on#

Pronunciation
/ˈtʃit ɑn/ · CHEET on · weak /ˈtʃit ən/
Русский
изменять (партнёру); списывать на (экзамене) — жульничать ради выгоды
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to informal
Complement
noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause. On takes either the test or the partner; at takes the game. Bare transitive cheat takes the victim (cheat a customer), and cheat someone out of something names what was taken.
Pattern
cheat on [a test, an exam, a tax return] · cheat on [a spouse or partner] · cheat at [a game] · cheat [someone] · cheat [someone] out of [something]
Contrast
American English says cheat on a test; British English says cheat in an exam. The preposition splits by the kind of activity: on for a test or a tax return, at for a game of cards or golf. With a person as object of on, the meaning is infidelity — he cheated on her — which is why cheat on the exam and cheat on his wife share a preposition and nothing else. Transitive cheat means defraud, and cheat someone out of names the loss (cheated out of a pension).

Break the rules to gain an advantage — on a test, at a game, or on a partner.

  1. Two students were caught cheating on the midterm.
  2. He cheats at cards and thinks nobody notices.
  3. She found out he'd been cheating on her for a year.
  4. Nearly a fifth of filers admit to cheating on their taxes.
  5. The proctor rearranged the room so nobody could cheat on the final.
  6. They cheated him out of six months' commission.

check up on#

Pronunciation
/ˈtʃɛk ˈʌp ɑn/ · chek UP on · weak /tʃɛk ʌp ən/
Русский
проверять, как дела у кого-то — наведываться, чтобы убедиться; разг.
Type
verb + adverb + preposition
Register
informal to neutral; everyday American speech
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun naming a person or a situation, or a wh-clause (check up on how she's settling in) — never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. The three words are frozen in order and the object always follows all of them: check up on him, never ✗ check him up on.
Pattern
check up on [someone] · check up on [something] · check on [someone] — the two-word variant, slightly more neutral · have a checkup — the unrelated noun
Contrast
The object test separates the two constructions. In check out a book, out is a particle and the object can move — check the book out, check it out — but in check up on the tenant nothing can move: ✗ check the tenant up on, which is what marks on here as a fixed preposition. In meaning, check up on implies a little suspicion or supervision (he's checking up on me), while check on is neutral concern (check on the roast), and check in on is a friendly short visit (check in on your grandmother).

Find out how someone or something is doing, often to make sure nothing is wrong.

  1. Would you check up on the dog while we're at the game?
  2. Her manager keeps checking up on her, which is why she is job-hunting.
  3. The state occasionally checks up on licensed facilities without warning.
  4. I called the pharmacy to check up on the refill.
  5. He drives past the rental every few weeks to check up on the place.
  6. Nobody checked up on the numbers until the audit.

choose between#

Pronunciation
/ˈtʃuz bɪˌtwin/ · CHOOZ bih-tween · weak /ˈtʃuz bɪtwən/
Русский
выбирать между — брать одно из противопоставленных вариантов
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
between takes two items joined by and, or a plural noun phrase (choose between the two offers) — the joiner must be and, never or: ✗ choose between staying or going. It also takes a gerund pair (choose between staying and going). Choose itself takes a direct object or a to-infinitive with no preposition at all: choose a color, choose to stay.
Pattern
choose between [A] and [B] · choose between [plural noun phrase] · choose among / from [a set of three or more] · choose [something] · choose to [verb]
Contrast
Between is for options weighed one against another; among or from is for picking out of a pool. Choose between the two finalists against choose from a field of nine. The old rule that between is only for two is too strict — choose between the four candidates is fine when they are being weighed against one another — but choose among remains the safer formal choice with a large set. The fixed error is ✗ choose between X or Y; the coordinator inside between is always and.

Pick one of two or more options that are set against each other.

  1. She had to choose between the fellowship and the job offer.
  2. Nobody should have to choose between rent and insulin.
  3. Choose between the two color schemes and we'll order the paper.
  4. Voters are choosing between two very different visions of the city.
  5. You can choose from a dozen plans, which is exactly the problem.
  6. He chose between staying in Tulsa and starting over in Austin.
See also
decide on · prefer to · compare to · vote for · between

cite to#

Pronunciation
/ˈsaɪt tu/ · SYTE too · weak /ˈsaɪt tə/
Русский
ссылаться на конкретное место источника — юр.; вне права предлог to не ставят
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; legal writing almost exclusively — briefs, opinions, and law reviews (counsel cited to Brown, cite to the record). Outside law the to is not used, and an editor will strike it.
Complement
noun phrase naming an authority, case, statute, page, or record — never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The everyday construction is bare transitive: cite three sources, ✗ cite to three sources in ordinary prose.
Pattern
cite to [a case / a page / the record] · cite [a source] — standard, no preposition · cite [someone] for [an offense] — the ticket sense · cite [someone] as [an authority]
Contrast
Three constructions, three different jobs. Bare cite takes the source as a direct object and is what every writer outside a courtroom uses. Cite to is a term of art in American legal writing, where it points the reader to a specific place in an authority. Cite for is enforcement — cited for speeding, cited for a code violation — and has nothing to do with quotation. Spelling traps come free with this one: sight is vision and site is a place.

Point a reader to a source, especially to a particular page or authority.

  1. The brief cites to three Ninth Circuit cases and one treatise.
  2. Please cite to the page in the transcript, not to the exhibit.
  3. The opinion cites to a statute that had already been repealed.
  4. Local rules require counsel to cite to the record for every factual claim.
  5. She cited to the deposition twice and never quoted it.
  6. In a seminar paper you simply cite the article — ✗ cite to it sounds like a filing.
See also
refer to · allude to · attribute to · to

collaborate with#

Pronunciation
/kəˈlæbəreɪt wɪθ/ · kuh-LAB-uh-rayt with · weak /kəˈlæbəreɪt wəθ/
Русский
сотрудничать с — работать вместе над чем-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; research, the arts, and business
Complement
noun phrase naming a person or organization — never a gerund (✗ collaborated with writing the score; the work takes on: collaborated on the score). Never a to-infinitive and never a clause. There is no direct-object frame: ✗ collaborated the album.
Pattern
collaborate with [someone] · collaborate with [someone] on [a project] · collaborate in [an effort] — formal · a collaboration with [someone] — the noun keeps the preposition
Contrast
Collaborate with and cooperate with are not synonyms. Collaborating is making something together — a paper, a record, a design. Cooperating is not getting in the way: the company cooperated with investigators says it handed over documents, not that it helped write the report. And collaborate carries one sense cooperation does not: working with an occupying enemy, which is why he collaborated with the regime is an accusation. Note the two prepositions divide the roles — with a person, on a project — and cannot swap.

Work jointly with someone to produce something.

  1. She collaborated with two other economists on the paper.
  2. The studio wanted him to collaborate with a younger songwriter.
  3. Our lab collaborates with a hospital group in Cleveland.
  4. He refuses to collaborate with anyone he hasn't met in person.
  5. The two agencies collaborated with each other for the first time in a decade.
  6. Historians still argue about who collaborated with the occupiers and who had no choice.
See also
cooperate with · work with · consult with · share with · with

come up with#

Pronunciation
/ˈkʌm ˈʌp wɪθ/ · kum UP with · weak /kʌm ʌp wəθ/
Русский
придумать, найти (идею, ответ, деньги) — разг.
Type
verb + adverb + preposition
Register
informal to neutral; ubiquitous in American speech and ordinary business writing
Complement
noun phrase — an idea, a plan, a name, a number, the money. A gerund is possible (came up with moving the deadline) but a noun phrase is the norm; never a to-infinitive (✗ came up with to move the deadline) and never a clause. The word order is frozen and the object follows all three words: ✗ come the money up with.
Pattern
come up with [an idea / a plan] · come up with [an amount of money] · come up with [an excuse] · come up with [verb-ing]
Contrast
The object test again marks the boundary. Think up has a movable particle — think it up, think a name up — so its up is a particle; come up with freezes, so with is a fixed preposition and the whole three-word unit behaves like a single transitive verb. Do not confuse it with its neighbors: come up alone means arise (the question came up), come up against means run into an obstacle, and come across means find by chance. In money contexts, come up with implies scraping the sum together under pressure.

Produce an idea, an answer, or a sum of money.

  1. Somebody has to come up with a name for the thing before Friday.
  2. They couldn't come up with the deposit in time.
  3. She came up with the only workable schedule anyone had seen.
  4. He comes up with an excuse every single week.
  5. The team came up with three prototypes in a month.
  6. Nobody has come up with a good explanation for the discrepancy.
See also
think about, think of · decide on, decide against · account for · with

comment on#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑmɛnt ɑn/ · KOM-ent on · weak /ˈkɑmɛnt ən/
Русский
комментировать что-то — высказываться по поводу чего-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund, or wh-clause (commented on how quiet it was) — never a to-infinitive. Before a full clause the preposition drops: commented that the room was cold, never ✗ commented on that.
Pattern
comment on [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] · comment that [clause] · decline to comment on [something] · no comment on [something]
Contrast
Comment on is the standard American form; comment about is heard but is weaker in edited prose. The noun keeps on (a comment on the draft) and so does the fixed newsroom formula declined to comment on the report. Compare remark on (interchangeable, slightly more formal), note and observe (transitive, no preposition: he noted the discrepancy), and criticize (also transitive: criticize the plan, never ✗ criticize on the plan).

Say something in reaction to a subject.

  1. She declined to comment on the settlement.
  2. Several reviewers commented on the length of the second act.
  3. He commented on how little had changed in twenty years.
  4. The mayor's office would not comment on the audit.
  5. Feel free to comment on the draft in the margins.
  6. Nobody commented on his new haircut, which disappointed him.

commit to#

Pronunciation
/kəˈmɪt tu/ · kuh-MIT too · weak /kəˈmɪt tə/
Русский
обязываться, брать на себя обязательство — после to герундий
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (commit to hiring two more nurses) — to is a preposition here, not the infinitive marker, so ✗ commit to hire two more nurses is an error. Never a that-clause after the preposition. The reflexive frame follows the same rule: commit yourself to finishing it. A separate transitive commit takes a bare object and no preposition at all: commit a crime, commit funds, commit it to memory.
Pattern
commit to [noun phrase] · commit to [verb-ing] · commit [oneself] to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · be committed to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · commit [a crime, an error, resources] — no preposition
Contrast
Another member of the look forward to family, and the noun test settles it the same way: you can say commit to the plan, so to is a preposition, so it has to be commit to redrawing the plan. Notice how far the transitive senses sit from this one — commit a felony and commit a hundred thousand dollars take no preposition, and commit someone to an institution is a third thing again. The participle is the form you meet most often, and the -ing after it is not optional: a company committed to lowering emissions, ✗ committed to lower emissions. Against its neighbors, commit to is firmer than plan on and softer than promise to, which takes the infinitive because promise has no preposition at all.

Bind yourself to a course of action.

  1. The board committed to replacing every lead service line by 2032.
  2. She won't commit to a date until the permits clear.
  3. Both candidates committed to three debates.
  4. He's committed to getting the certification this year.
  5. Don't commit to anything before you've read the fine print.
  6. The district has committed to smaller classes without saying how it will pay for them.
See also
look forward to · object to · dedicate to · plan on · agree to · to

communicate with#

Pronunciation
/kəˈmjunəkeɪt wɪθ/ · kuh-MYOO-nuh-kayt with · weak /kəˈmjunəkeɪt wəθ/
Русский
общаться с; быть связанным с (о технике) — обмениваться информацией
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; management and HR writing, technical documentation (the sensor communicates with the hub), and clinical notes
Complement
noun phrase naming a person, group, or system — never a gerund (✗ communicate with telling them early), never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The message itself is never the object of with: it is a direct object, and the audience takes to: communicate the decision to staff.
Pattern
communicate with [someone] · communicate [something] to [someone] · [two systems] communicate with [each other] · communication with [someone] — the noun keeps the preposition
Contrast
With is two-way; to is one-way. She communicates with the field offices* describes an exchange; she communicates the policy to the field offices describes a transmission — and mixing them (✗ communicate the policy with staff) is the usual error. There is no bare transitive frame for the person: ✗ communicate somebody. In speech Americans mostly say talk to or get in touch with and reserve communicate with for the formal register, which is why we are unable to communicate with the crew sounds like an official statement, because it is one.

Exchange information with someone, or be connected to something.

  1. The hospital could not communicate with the family for two days.
  2. Sales and engineering barely communicate with each other.
  3. The buoy communicates with a satellite twice an hour.
  4. He communicates with his students almost entirely by email.
  5. Air traffic control communicated with the aircraft until 3:14 a.m.
  6. Two of the rooms communicate with the courtyard through French doors.

compare to, compare with#

Pronunciation
/kəmˈpɛr tu/ · kum-PAIR too · weak /kəmˈpɛr tə/
Русский
сравнивать — with сопоставлять по признакам, to уподоблять одно другому
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral; the to / with distinction is formal-register only
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (compared moving to starting over) — no to-infinitive, no clause. Compare also takes two objects joined by and: compare the 2019 and 2024 figures. The passive participle compared to / compared with works as a preposition in its own right (compared with last year, sales are flat).
Pattern
compare [A] to [B] · compare [A] with [B] · compare [A] and [B] · [something] compare to / with [something — measure up] · compared to / with [noun phrase] (sentence adverbial)
The textbook rule
compare to likens two unlike things to make a point (he compared the deficit to a leaky bucket), while compare with sets two like things side by side to weigh them (compare this quarter with last). In actual American usage the distinction is fading and compared to has taken over both jobs, especially in speech; compared with survives in statistical and scientific writing, where the side-by-side sense matters. What is not optional: in the intransitive sense, to is standard — nothing compares to a real vacation.

Set two things side by side, or liken one to the other.

  1. He compared the reorganization to rearranging deck chairs.
  2. Compare this year's numbers with the same quarter in 2022.
  3. Revenue rose four percent compared with the prior year.
  4. Critics compared her early work to Munro's, which she found irritating.
  5. Nothing compares to the first cold morning in October.
  6. It's hard to compare a rural district with an urban one on test scores alone.
See also
resemble · differ from · prefer to · choose between · compared to · compared with

compensate for#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑmpənseɪt fɔr/ · KOM-pun-sayt for · weak /ˈkɑmpənseɪt fər/
Русский
компенсировать, возмещать — восполнять потерю или слабое место
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; engineering, psychology, insurance, and payroll
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (compensate for arriving late) — never a to-infinitive (✗ compensate for arrive late) and never a that-clause. Two frames share the verb: compensate for [a loss] offsets it, and compensate [someone] for [something] pays them, with the person as a direct object.
Pattern
compensate for [a loss / a weakness] · compensate [someone] for [something] · compensate for [verb-ing] · overcompensate for [something]
Contrast
Two senses hang on the same preposition. The offsetting sense — thicker insulation compensates for the north exposure — has no money in it at all; the paying sense always has a person as direct object — compensate the homeowners for the damage. The everyday equivalent of the first is make up for; the technical neighbors are correct for and adjust for, which remove a distortion from a measurement rather than balancing a deficiency. And offset does the same work with no preposition: offset the loss, ✗ offset for the loss.

Balance out a loss or a weakness, or pay someone for one.

  1. Extra rehearsal compensated for the missing week.
  2. The airline compensated passengers for the canceled leg with vouchers.
  3. She compensates for a weak serve with a very good net game.
  4. Nothing compensates for losing that much institutional memory at once.
  5. The system compensates for temperature drift automatically.
  6. He tried to compensate for being new by staying late every night.
See also
pay for · correct for · substitute for · allow for · for

compete with, compete against, compete for#

Pronunciation
/kəmˈpit wɪθ/ · kum-PEET with · weak /kəmˈpit wəθ/
Русский
соперничать — with/against с кем-то, for за что-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with and against take a rival. for takes the prize. in takes the event (compete in the 400). No gerund and no clause follows any of them: ✗ compete for winning — say compete for the title. Compete is intransitive and never takes a bare object: ✗ compete the other firms.
Pattern
compete with / against [a rival] · compete for [a prize, a contract, attention] · compete in [an event, a market] · compete with [a rival] for [a prize]
Contrast
Compete with and compete against are genuinely interchangeable for the rival, with against a shade more adversarial and more common in sports. Keep the slots straight: the rival takes with or against, the prize takes for, the arena takes in — three firms competing with each other for one contract in a shrinking market. Note that compete with has a second, non-adversarial sense meaning match or rival in quality: no supermarket tomato competes with one out of the garden.

Try to win something that someone else also wants.

  1. Small presses can't compete with the big houses on advances.
  2. She competed against her own sister in the semifinal.
  3. Four firms are competing for the airport contract.
  4. He competes in three or four triathlons a year.
  5. Streaming services are competing for the same twelve hours of anyone's week.
  6. Nothing in a jar competes with fresh basil.

complain to, complain about#

Pronunciation
/kəmˈpleɪn əˌbaʊt/ · kum-PLAYN uh-bowt · weak /kəmˈpleɪn əbət/
Русский
жаловаться — to кому-то, about на что-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to takes the person or office hearing it. about takes a noun phrase, a gerund (complained about having to reapply), or a wh-clause. of takes a symptom, in medical and formal use (complained of dizziness). None takes a to-infinitive. A full clause drops the preposition: complained that the room was cold.
Pattern
complain to [person / office] · complain about [noun phrase / verb-ing] · complain to [person] about [thing] · complain of [a symptom] · complain that [clause]
The person and the grievance take different prepositions and cannot share one
✗ complain to the noise, ✗ complain about the landlord when you mean you told the landlord. Complain of is the narrow medical and legal use — the patient complained of chest pain — and sounds odd elsewhere. Compare object to, which registers formal opposition, and criticize, which is transitive and takes no preposition at all.

Say that something is wrong, unsatisfactory, or unfair.

  1. She complained to the building manager about the elevator.
  2. Everyone complains about the parking and nobody goes to the meetings.
  3. He complained of headaches for a week before anyone made him see a doctor.
  4. Two neighbors complained to the city.
  5. They complained about having to submit the same form twice.

comply with#

Pronunciation
/kəmˈplaɪ wɪθ/ · kum-PLY with · weak /kəmˈplaɪ wəθ/
Русский
соблюдать, выполнять — делать то, чего требует правило или предписание; офиц.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — regulatory, legal, and corporate
Complement
noun phrase only, naming a rule, order, request, or standard. No gerund in normal use (✗ comply with reporting quarterly — write comply with the quarterly reporting requirement) and no clause. Comply is intransitive and never takes a bare object: ✗ comply the order.
Pattern
comply with [a rule, an order, a request, a standard] · fail to comply with [something] · in compliance with [something]
Contrast
Comply to and ✗ comply of are errors — the preposition is fixed at with. Its close neighbors each keep a different one: conform to a standard, adhere to a policy, abide by a rule, obey an order (transitive, no preposition), and follow instructions (also transitive). Comply with is heavier and more institutional than any of them; in ordinary speech an American says follow the rules.

Do what a rule, an order, or a request requires.

  1. The lab failed to comply with federal handling standards.
  2. All vendors must comply with the new security requirements by October.
  3. The company complied with the subpoena within a week.
  4. Buildings put up before 1978 don't comply with the current code.
  5. He was fired for refusing to comply with a direct order.
  6. The device complies with Part 15 of the FCC rules.

concentrate on#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑnsənˌtreɪt ɑn/ · KON-sun-trayt on · weak /ˈkɑnsənˌtreɪt ən/
Русский
сосредоточиваться на — отдавать всё внимание одному делу
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (concentrate on finishing the draft), or wh-clause — never a to-infinitive (✗ concentrate on finish the draft, ✗ concentrate on to finish it).
Pattern
concentrate on [noun phrase / verb-ing / wh-clause] · concentrate (alone) · be concentrated in [a place — distribution] · concentrate [efforts, resources] on [something]
Contrast
Concentrate in is a different verb. With on, it means direct your attention or effort — concentrate on the road. With in, and usually in the passive, it means be densely gathered somewhere: most of the state's jobs are concentrated in three counties. Focus on is exactly interchangeable with the on sense and is the more common word in American business speech; center on is the same again — and note that careful American usage prefers center on, since center around is widely criticized as illogical.

Give your full attention or effort to one thing.

  1. Concentrate on the first two chapters and leave the rest.
  2. It's impossible to concentrate on anything with that leaf blower going.
  3. The district is concentrating on third-grade reading scores.
  4. She concentrated on getting the wiring right before touching the drywall.
  5. Let's concentrate on what we can actually change this quarter.
  6. Ninety percent of the growth is concentrated in the metro area.

confer on#

Pronunciation
/kənˈfɜr ɑn/ · kun-FUR on · weak /kənˈfɜr ən/
Русский
присуждать, наделять кого-то (правом, званием, полномочием); юр./офиц.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; statutes, charters, and ceremonies — the Act confers jurisdiction on the district courts, the university conferred an honorary degree on her
Complement
the thing granted is a direct object — a right, a power, an honor, a benefit — and on takes the recipient. Never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, never a clause. Both slots are required: ✗ conferred on the courts with nothing granted is incomplete.
Pattern
confer [a right / a power / a degree] on [someone] · be conferred on [someone] · confer with [someone] — a different verb, meaning consult · confer about [a matter]
Variants
confer upon — identical in meaning and the older form; standard in statutes, court opinions, and ceremonial citations, where upon still outnumbers on
Contrast
Confer on and confer with are two different verbs wearing one spelling. Confer on* is transitive and means grant from a position of authority; confer with takes no direct object and means consult — may I confer with my client? You cannot mix them: ✗ conferred the degree with her, ✗ conferred on my client about the offer. Nearby, bestow on is the ceremonial synonym, grant to is the everyday one (granted the license to the applicant), and vest in is the drafting term for placing a power somewhere permanently.

Grant a right, a power, or an honor to someone.

  1. The charter confers broad zoning authority on the county board.
  2. An honorary doctorate was conferred on her in 1998.
  3. Nothing in the lease confers a right of first refusal on the tenant.
  4. The medal is conferred upon fewer than a dozen people a year.
  5. Membership confers borrowing privileges on the whole household.
  6. The statute conferred jurisdiction on a court that did not yet exist.

confess to#

Pronunciation
/kənˈfɛs tu/ · kun-FES too · weak /kənˈfɛs tə/
Русский
признаваться в — открыто говорить о своей вине; после to герундий
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (confessed to taking the money) — never a to-infinitive (✗ confessed to take the money). The preposition is optional before a bare object: confess a crime, confess his sins. Before a clause it disappears: confessed that he had lied.
Pattern
confess to [a crime / verb-ing] · confess [a sin, a crime] · confess that [clause] · confess to [a priest, the police] · confess to [a small weakness]
Contrast
Confess to has two objects it can take and you must read which one is meant: confess to the police names the hearer, confess to the burglary names the deed. Against admit to, confess is heavier — it implies guilt and often a hearer — which is why I admit to liking bad television is light and I confess to liking bad television is arch. Own up to is the informal American equivalent, and it too takes a gerund: own up to breaking it.

Say openly that you did something wrong.

  1. He confessed to the arson after nine hours of questioning.
  2. She confessed to having read his email.
  3. I'll confess to a certain fondness for the old logo.
  4. Two of them confessed to the police and one never said a word.
  5. He confessed that he had never actually read the report.
  6. Nobody confessed to moving the thermostat.

conform to, conform with#

Pronunciation
/kənˈfɔrm tu/ · kun-FORM too · weak /kənˈfɔrm tə/
Русский
соответствовать чему-то — отвечать стандарту, норме или образцу
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal and technical; building codes, specifications, standards, and social commentary
Complement
noun phrase naming a standard, code, specification, type, or expectation. A gerund is grammatical but rare (conform to using the approved fasteners); never a to-infinitive (✗ conform to use the approved fasteners) and never a that-clause. The verb also stands alone in the social sense, with no complement at all: the pressure to conform. There is no direct-object frame: ✗ conform the code (though conform something to something — bring it into line — exists in legal drafting).
Pattern
[a thing] conform to [a standard] · [a statement] conform with [another] · conform — no complement, social sense · conform [a document] to [a requirement] — formal transitive
Variants
conform with — interchangeable in most contexts and slightly more formal; American usage prefers to for matching a standard (conforms to code) and leaves with for two things that agree with each other (the summary conforms with the audited figures)
Contrast
Things conform; people comply. Comply with is what a person, a company, or an agency does about a requirement someone imposes — the plant complies with the permit — while conform to says a thing matches a specification: the wiring conforms to code. Beside them, adhere to is following a rule steadily over time and abide by is accepting a decision already made. Note the third life of the word, in social criticism, where conform needs no preposition and always sounds faintly like a criticism.

Match a standard, a rule, or a type.

  1. None of the outlets in the basement conform to current code.
  2. The proposal must conform to the format described in the RFP.
  3. Her account conforms with what the two other witnesses said.
  4. Products that fail to conform to the standard cannot carry the label.
  5. He never much cared about conforming to what the neighbors expected.
  6. The revised bylaws were conformed to the new state statute.
See also
comply with · adhere to · abide by · correspond to, correspond with · to

congratulate on#

Pronunciation
/kənˈɡrætʃəˌleɪt ɑn/ · kun-GRACH-uh-layt on · weak /kənˈɡrætʃəˌleɪt ən/
Русский
поздравлять с — выражать радость по поводу чужого успеха

Register

Complement
noun phrase or gerund (congratulated her on winning) — never a to-infinitive (✗ congratulated her on to win) and never a clause. The person is a direct object and never takes a preposition: ✗ congratulate to her.
Pattern
congratulate [someone] on [noun phrase / verb-ing] · congratulate [someone] · congratulate [oneself] on [something] · Congratulations on [noun phrase / verb-ing]
Contrast
Congratulate for is nonstandard in careful American English, though it is common in speech; the fixed preposition is on, and the greeting keeps it too — Congratulations on the baby, ✗ Congratulations for the baby. British usage also allows congratulate upon, which sounds stiff in the U.S. Compare thank someone for something and praise someone for something, which take for and never on — three near-synonyms with three fixed and different prepositions.

consent to#

Pronunciation
/kənˈsɛnt tu/ · kun-SENT too · weak /kənˈsɛnt tə/
Русский
давать согласие на — разрешать; мед. и юр. контексты
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; medicine, law, and privacy notices — consent to treatment, consent to the search, consent to the terms
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (consented to being recorded) — never a that-clause in modern American usage (✗ consented to that we record it). The one thing to keep straight: consent to be recorded, with a bare infinitive, is also correct — but there the to is the infinitive marker belonging to the verb consent, not this preposition, exactly as with agree. Both are good English and they mean the same thing; what is wrong is mixing them (✗ consented to be questioning).
Pattern
consent to [a procedure / a search / the terms] · consent to [verb-ing] · consent to [verb — infinitive, not preposition] · give consent to [something] · consent — no complement
Contrast
Consent to grants permission; the neighbors do other things. Agree to* is the everyday word and carries no notion of authority; assent to formally approves; accede to gives in after resisting; acquiesce in merely fails to object. Legally the distinction matters: only the person with the right to permit can consent, which is why informed consent is a term of art and why ✗ consent with is not English. Note that object to, the opposite pattern, takes only the gerund — objected to being recorded, never ✗ objected to be recorded — so the two are not symmetrical.

Give permission for something.

  1. She would not consent to another round of tests.
  2. The driver consented to a search of the trunk.
  3. Both parties consented to the change of venue.
  4. He consented to being photographed only from a distance.
  5. Users must consent to the collection of location data.
  6. Her father never consented to the marriage and never explained why.
See also
agree to · assent to · accede to · object to · to

consist in#

Pronunciation
/kənˈsɪst ɪn/ · kun-SIST in · weak /kənˈsɪst ən/
Русский
заключаться в — составлять самую суть чего-то; книжн. (не путать с consist of)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal and philosophical; essays, theology, and academic prose — happiness consists in contentment — and rare enough in speech that many Americans know only consist of
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (consists in knowing when to stop) — never a to-infinitive (✗ consists in to know when to stop) and never a that-clause. The verb takes no direct object, has no passive (✗ is consisted in) and no progressive (✗ is consisting in).
Pattern
[an abstraction] consist in [its essence] · consist in [verb-ing] · the difficulty consists in [something]
Contrast
Consist in names the essence; consist of lists the parts. The committee consists of nine members* is an inventory; good management consists in hiring well and then leaving people alone is a definition — swap them and you get nonsense: ✗ the committee consists in nine members. A useful test: consist of answers what is it made of, consist in answers what does it really amount to, and the second can almost always be replaced by lies in. Both refuse the passive and the progressive, which is the error learners make most often with either.

Have as its essential nature — be constituted by.

  1. Her whole argument consists in one unproven assumption.
  2. Real freedom, he wrote, consists in having nothing left to prove.
  3. The difficulty consists in getting three agencies to sign the same document.
  4. Virtue does not consist in never being tempted.
  5. The novel's charm consists in how little it explains.
  6. Most of the work consists in checking numbers somebody else typed.
See also
consist of · amount to · result in, result from · in

consist of#

Pronunciation
/kənˈsɪst əv/ · kun-SIST uv · weak /kənˈsɪst ə/
Русский
состоять из — быть составленным из частей
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (consists of filling out three forms) — never a to-infinitive and never a clause. Consist is intransitive: it has no passive (✗ is consisted of) and no progressive (✗ is consisting of).
Pattern
[a whole] consist of [its parts] · [something] consist in [its essence] — formal · be composed of / be made up of [parts]
Contrast
Is consisted of is an error — the verb is already active and cannot be passivized. Consist of lists the physical parts; the rarer consist in names where something's essence lies (freedom consists in the absence of coercion). The neighbors sort out like this: consist of and be made up of are the plain forms; be composed of is formal; and comprise means the same thing but takes a direct object and no preposition — the collection comprises 400 prints — so the very common is comprised of remains disputed in edited American prose. If you are unsure, consists of is always safe.

Be made up of certain parts.

  1. The committee consists of five members and two alternates.
  2. Breakfast consisted of cold toast and an apology.
  3. The exam consists of forty questions and one essay.
  4. Her job largely consists of answering email she didn't ask for.
  5. The archive consists of letters, photographs, and one reel of film.
  6. True courage, he said, consists in doing it anyway.
See also
lack · belong to · contribute to · separate from · of

consult with#

Pronunciation
/kənˈsʌlt wɪθ/ · kun-SULT with · weak /kənˈsʌlt wəθ/
Русский
советоваться с — обсуждать вопрос со специалистом
Type
verb + preposition
Register
consult with is neutral American; bare consult is more formal
Complement
noun phrase only, naming a person or body. No gerund, no clause. The bare transitive is a different pattern: consult a doctor, consult the manual — a reference source is a direct object and never takes with: ✗ consult with the dictionary.
Pattern
consult with [a person, a colleague, a firm] · consult [a doctor, a lawyer, a manual, a map] · consult [someone] about / on [a matter] · consult for [a client]
Contrast
Consult with someone is standard, everyday American English and is far more common in the U.S. than in Britain, where it can sound redundant next to plain consult. The useful distinction: you consult a source (it gives you information) and consult with a person (you talk it over together). A third pattern, consult for, means work as a paid consultant: she consults for two hospital systems. The matter under discussion takes about or on.

Talk something over with someone, or look something up in a source.

  1. Let me consult with my partner before I commit to anything.
  2. You should consult a tax attorney, not a message board.
  3. The city consulted with residents about the bike lanes for eight months.
  4. She consults for three startups and teaches one class.
  5. Consult the appendix for the full list of exclusions.
  6. The surgeon consulted with two colleagues before scheduling the operation.
See also
talk with · refer to · apply to · agree with · discuss

contribute to#

Pronunciation
/kənˈtrɪbjut tu/ · kun-TRIB-yoot too · weak /kənˈtrɪbjut tə/
Русский
вносить вклад в; способствовать чему-то — после to герундий
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (contributed to lowering the rate) — never a to-infinitive (✗ contributed to lower the rate, which changes the meaning to purpose). A wh-clause is possible after to whatever. Never a that-clause.
Pattern
contribute to [a fund, a cause, a result] · contribute [something] to [something] · contribute to [verb-ing] · contribute [an article] to [a publication]
Contrast
Watch the to. In contributed to lowering the rate the to is the fixed preposition and the verb must be -ing; in contributed money to lower the rate the to is an infinitive of purpose and belongs to a different structure entirely. The two read almost alike and mean different things. Note also that contribute covers both giving and causing: contribute to a pension is generous, contribute to a collapse is not.

Give something toward a total, or help bring something about.

  1. Everyone in the office contributed to the gift.
  2. Deferred maintenance contributed to the failure of the pump.
  3. She has contributed to the magazine for twenty years.
  4. Three factors contributed to the drop in enrollment.
  5. The tax credit contributed to lowering the uninsured rate.
  6. He contributed a chapter to the anthology and never got paid.
See also

converge on, converge to#

Pronunciation
/kənˈvɜrdʒ ɑn/ · kun-VURJ on · weak /kənˈvɜrdʒ ən/
Русский
сходиться к одной точке — on съезжаться куда-то, to сходиться (мат.)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral for converge on; converge to is technical, at home in mathematics and statistics
Complement
noun phrase naming a place, target, or value — never a gerund, never a to-infinitive, never a clause. There is no direct-object frame: ✗ converged the plaza. The subject is plural or collective, because convergence takes at least two things: ✗ the reporter converged on city hall.
Pattern
[people / vehicles] converge on [a place] · [estimates / opinions] converge on [a figure] · [a sequence] converge to [a limit] · [two lines / two paths] converge with [each other]
Contrast
The preposition marks the field you are writing in. Converge on is the general word — crowds converge on a stadium, forecasters converge on a number. Converge to belongs to mathematics, where a series converges to a limit, and using it of a crowd sounds like a translation error. Converge with takes the other party rather than the destination (the trail converges with the highway). The opposite is diverge from, and note that converge has no passive: ✗ the plaza was converged on is at best jocular.

Come together from different directions toward one point.

  1. Reporters converged on the courthouse before the verdict was read.
  2. Independent estimates have converged on about four percent.
  3. Three storm systems converged on the valley in a single week.
  4. The sequence converges to zero faster than the textbook example.
  5. Both parties eventually converged on the same compromise figure.
  6. The old logging road converges with the highway two miles north.
See also
agree on · arrive at, arrive in · amount to · on

convict of#

Pronunciation
/kənˈvɪkt əv/ · kun-VIKT uv · weak /kənˈvɪkt ə/
Русский
признавать виновным в — выносить обвинительный приговор; юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal; legal and news reporting
Complement
noun phrase naming the crime, or a gerund naming it (convicted of lying to investigators) — never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. The passive is the normal frame: was convicted of. Counts and charges take on instead: convicted on three counts, convicted on all charges; ✗ convicted for murder and ✗ convicted with murder are errors, though convicted for a crime he did not commit passes as causal for.
Pattern
[a jury / a court] convict [someone] of [a crime] · [someone] be convicted of [a crime] · be convicted on [counts] · a convicted felon · convict [someone] of [verb-ing]
Contrast
Convict of and acquit of are the two verdicts and share one preposition. Upstream of both, charge with* is what the prosecutor files and indict for is what a grand jury returns, so the preposition changes at every stage: charged with fraud, indicted for fraud, convicted of fraud, sentenced to three years. Watch the stress: the verb is kun-VIKT, the noun KON-vikt. And convict never takes a that-clause — for that you need find: the jury found that he had lied.

Find someone guilty of a crime in a court of law.

  1. He was convicted of wire fraud and sentenced in March.
  2. The jury convicted her of the lesser charge only.
  3. Anyone convicted of a felony loses the license automatically.
  4. Two officers were convicted of falsifying the reports.
  5. She was convicted on three counts and acquitted on the rest.
  6. He was convicted of selling equipment that belonged to the county.
See also
acquit of · accuse of · charge with, charge for · absolve of · punish for · of

cooperate with#

Pronunciation
/koʊˈɑpəreɪt wɪθ/ · koh-OP-uh-rayt with · weak /koʊˈɑpəreɪt wəθ/
Русский
сотрудничать с — содействовать, а не мешать (часто о следствии)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral; extremely common in reporting on investigations
Complement
noun phrase naming a person, agency, or organization — never a gerund (✗ cooperated with handing over the files; the activity takes on or in: cooperated on the study, cooperated in the investigation). Never a to-infinitive, never a clause, and there is no direct object: ✗ cooperated the inquiry. The verb also stands alone: the witness cooperated.
Pattern
cooperate with [someone] · cooperate with [someone] on / in [something] · cooperate — no complement · cooperation with [someone] — the noun keeps the preposition
Contrast
Cooperating is not obstructing; collaborating is co-authoring. The company cooperated with regulators means it answered the questions and produced the documents; the company collaborated with regulators would oddly suggest they wrote the rules together. In criminal practice cooperate with has hardened into a term of art — a cooperating witness trades information for leniency — and refused to cooperate is nearly a formula. Note that ✗ cooperate to someone and ✗ cooperate someone are both impossible.

Work together with someone, or help them rather than obstruct them.

  1. The airline cooperated with federal investigators from the first day.
  2. Two states cooperated with each other on the watershed plan.
  3. He declined to cooperate with the committee and was subpoenaed.
  4. Local police cooperate with the sheriff's office on anything over the county line.
  5. Tenants who cooperate with the inspection get the repairs done first.
  6. If the weather cooperates with us, we'll be on the water by six.
See also
collaborate with · work with · comply with · consult with · with

cope with#

Pronunciation
/ˈkoʊp wɪθ/ · KOHP with · weak /ˈkoʊp wəθ/
Русский
справляться с — успешно преодолевать трудность
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (cope with being alone) — never a to-infinitive (✗ cope with to be alone) and never a clause. Cope is intransitive and cannot take a bare object: ✗ cope the situation. It does stand alone: she's coping.
Pattern
cope with [a difficulty] · cope with [verb-ing] · cope (alone) · manage to [verb] — a different verb, different pattern
Contrast
Cope to and ✗ cope the problem are both errors. The preposition is fixed at with, and the verb never takes a direct object. Its neighbors split: deal with is the neutral everyday synonym, handle and manage are transitive with no preposition (handle the complaints, manage the fallout), and put up with means tolerate rather than manage. Bare cope, with nothing after it, has become the standard American shorthand for barely managing: I'm coping.

Deal successfully with something difficult.

  1. She's coping with a heavy caseload and a sick parent.
  2. Small districts can't cope with an enrollment swing that size.
  3. He coped with being passed over better than anyone expected.
  4. The system wasn't built to cope with that kind of traffic.
  5. How do you cope with the noise?
  6. They're coping, but only just.
See also

correct for#

Pronunciation
/kəˈrɛkt fɔr/ · kuh-REKT for · weak /kəˈrɛkt fər/
Русский
вносить поправку на — устранять известное искажение; науч./техн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal and technical; statistics, laboratory science, economics — corrected for inflation, corrected for altitude
Complement
noun phrase naming a distorting factor — inflation, seasonality, temperature, sample bias. A gerund is rare but possible (correcting for having oversampled retirees); never a to-infinitive and never a clause. Keep it apart from the plain transitive: correct the error takes no preposition, and correct someone on something is a third pattern.
Pattern
correct for [a distortion] · be corrected for [inflation / seasonality] · correct [an error] — no preposition · correct [someone] on [a point]
Contrast
Three technical near-neighbors, and the difference is real. Correct for and adjust for remove a known distortion from figures you already have — adjust for is the commoner word in finance. Control for is a design decision made in advance, holding a variable constant so it cannot vary; a study controls for age, and saying it corrected for age instead tells the reader the fix came afterward. And account for merely explains a number rather than changing it.

Adjust a figure or a measurement to remove a known distortion.

  1. Wages have barely moved once you correct for inflation.
  2. The model corrects for seasonal swings in construction hiring.
  3. Their instruments correct for the curvature of the lens automatically.
  4. The survey did not correct for the fact that only landlines were called.
  5. Test scores corrected for school size tell a different story.
  6. Blood values are corrected for altitude before they are compared.
See also
account for · allow for · compensate for · for

correlate with#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɔrəleɪt wɪθ/ · KOR-uh-layt with · weak /ˈkɔrəleɪt wəθ/
Русский
коррелировать с — изменяться согласованно с чем-то; науч.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal and technical; statistics, medicine, and the social sciences, plus the business writing that borrows from them
Complement
noun phrase or gerund naming the other variable (correlates with having gone to preschool) — never a to-infinitive (✗ correlates with to go to preschool) and never a clause. There is also a transitive frame in which the researcher is the subject: correlate the survey data with census figures. The verb can stand alone with a plural subject: the two measures correlate.
Pattern
[one variable] correlate with [another] · correlate [one set of data] with [another] · [two things] correlate · be strongly / weakly / negatively correlated with [something]
Variants
correlate to — heard in business and journalistic writing; with is the standard in scientific prose and the safer choice everywhere
Contrast
The whole point of the word is that it does not claim causation. Ice cream sales correlate with drownings asserts only that the two move together; cause, lead to, and result in assert direction, and swapping them in is the classic error. Two softer neighbors: relate to is vague about the relationship, and vary with describes co-movement without the statistical machinery (the fee varies with the size of the lot). Note the adjective frame is the commonest of all in papers: strongly correlated with income.

Move together with something in a measurable way.

  1. Household income correlates with almost every health outcome in the study.
  2. The two tests correlate with each other well enough to use either one.
  3. Absenteeism correlates with the length of the commute.
  4. Nothing in the data correlates with the change they expected to see.
  5. The researchers correlated the sensor readings with hospital admissions.
  6. Test scores correlate with having a parent who finished college.
See also
correspond to, correspond with · compare to, compare with · result in, result from · depend on · with

correspond to, correspond with#

Pronunciation
/ˌkɔrəˈspɑnd tu/ · kor-uh-SPOND too · weak /ˌkɔrəˈspɑnd tə/
Русский
соответствовать чему-то (to); переписываться с кем-то (with)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal; the letter-writing sense is formal and a little old-fashioned
Complement
noun phrase — never a gerund (✗ corresponds to being late; say corresponds to the delay), never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The verb has no passive (✗ is corresponded to) and no direct object.
Pattern
[one thing] correspond to [another] — match · [a person] correspond with [another] — exchange letters · [two things] correspond · the corresponding [figure / period] — the participle, very common in reports
Contrast
The two prepositions have drifted apart in American usage, and the split is worth keeping. Correspond to means match or be equivalent: the numbers on the invoice correspond to the packing list, the American term corresponds to the British one. Correspond with means write letters back and forth: they corresponded with each other for forty years. Older and British writing also uses with for matching, so the figures correspond with the audit is not wrong — but in a document where both senses could be read, to for matching keeps the sentence clear. Compare conform to, which measures a thing against a standard rather than pairing two things off.

Match something, or exchange letters with someone.

  1. Each color on the map corresponds to a census tract.
  2. The two economists corresponded with each other for a decade before meeting.
  3. The serial number does not correspond to anything in our records.
  4. Roughly, a bachelor's degree there corresponds to an associate's here.
  5. She corresponded with the family in Odessa until the mail stopped.
  6. Revenue for the quarter fell short of the corresponding period last year.
See also
conform to, conform with · correlate with · compare to, compare with · communicate with · to

count on#

Pronunciation
/ˈkaʊnt ɑn/ · KOWNT on · weak /ˈkaʊnt ən/
Русский
рассчитывать на — быть уверенным, что не подведёт
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to informal
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (counting on getting the grant) — never a to-infinitive after the preposition itself (✗ count on to get the grant). A to-infinitive is correct after a person: count on her to remember. A wh-clause is fine: don't count on what he tells you. Count on also takes a that-clause in speech (count on it that he'll be late), but the smoother form is count on his being late.
Pattern
count on [someone / something] · count on [someone] to [verb] · count on [verb-ing] · you can count on it
Contrast
Same family as depend on and rely on, with a difference of register: count on is the warm, spoken one, rely on the neutral one, depend on the one that also means be determined by. Count on almost never carries that second sense — That counts on the weather is not English; you want That depends on the weather. Note the unrelated phrasal verbs count in (count me in), count out (exclude), and count up (total), none of which are this pattern.

Trust that someone or something will not let you down.

  1. You can count on her to have read every page.
  2. Don't count on the mail getting there by Friday.
  3. Half the neighborhood counts on that bus line.
  4. I'm counting on you.
  5. We were counting on a bigger turnout than forty people.
  6. Count on it — he'll bring up the parking again.
See also
depend on · rely on · believe in · wait for

cover with#

Pronunciation
/ˈkʌvɚ wɪθ/ · KUV-er with · weak /ˈkʌvɚ wəθ/
Русский
покрывать чем-то — накладывать слой поверх
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause. The passive takes either preposition: covered with snow and covered in snow are both standard, with in a shade more thorough. Cover itself is transitive: cover the pot, ✗ cover with the pot if the pot is the lid.
Pattern
cover [something] with [something] · be covered with / in [something] · cover [a topic, a distance, a shift] — no preposition · be covered by [insurance, a policy, a warranty]
Contrast
Covered with and covered in are genuinely interchangeable; in suggests more complete coverage (covered in mud beats covered with mud for effect). Covered by is a different thing entirely — it names an insurer, a policy, or a reporter: the surgery is covered by insurance, the trial was covered by three networks. And bare cover has a raft of transitive senses that take no preposition at all: cover the material, cover forty miles, cover for a colleague (that last one being cover for, meaning substitute or make excuses).

Put a layer over something.

  1. Cover the dough with a damp towel and let it rest.
  2. By morning the deck was covered with ice.
  3. His desk is permanently covered in sticky notes.
  4. She covered the stain with a rug and hoped nobody looked.
  5. The whole field was covered with frost at sunrise.
  6. The procedure isn't covered by our plan.

credit with#

Pronunciation
/ˈkrɛdɪt wɪθ/ · KRED-it with · weak /ˈkrɛdɪt wəθ/
Русский
приписывать кому-то заслугу — признавать чей-то вклад
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral; standard in journalism, citations, and banking
Complement
the person or thing honored is a direct object, and with takes a noun phrase or a gerund (credited her with saving the account) — never a to-infinitive (✗ credited her with to save the account) and never a clause. Both slots are obligatory in the active: ✗ credited with the discovery alone needs a passive subject — she was credited with the discovery.
Pattern
credit [someone] with [an achievement] · credit [someone] with [verb-ing] · be credited with [something] · credit [an achievement] to [someone] · credit [an account] with [an amount]
Contrast
Credit with and blame for are mirror images, one for things that went right and one for things that went wrong, and they take different prepositions: credited her with the turnaround, blamed her for the collapse. Attribute to and ascribe to say the same thing with the arguments reversed — attributed the turnaround to her — so the achievement leads and the person follows. American speech also uses credit someone for something, which careful writers reserve for the effort or the reason (credit him for trying) while keeping with for the accomplishment itself. The banking sense runs both ways: credit the account with $50 or credit $50 to the account.

Say that someone deserves recognition for something.

  1. Reviewers credited the director with rescuing the second act.
  2. She is credited with discovering two of the three isotopes.
  3. Nobody credits the mayor with the drop in crime except the mayor.
  4. He credits his sister with talking him out of dropping the case.
  5. The bank credited the account with the disputed charge the next morning.
  6. Historians now credit her with having written most of the report.
See also
attribute to · ascribe to · blame for, blame on · thank for · with

cut down on#

Pronunciation
/ˈkʌt ˈdaʊn ɑn/ · kut DOWN on · weak /kʌt daʊn ən/
Русский
сокращать потребление чего-то — есть, пить или тратить меньше; разг.
Type
verb + adverb + preposition
Register
informal to neutral; the everyday American way to talk about consuming less of something
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (cut down on eating out) — never a to-infinitive (✗ cut down on eat out) and never a clause. The three words are frozen and the object follows all of them: ✗ cut sugar down on, ✗ cut down sugar on.
Pattern
cut down on [something] · cut down on [verb-ing] · cut back on [something] — the same meaning · cut out [something] — quit it entirely
Contrast
This entry is where the particle and the preposition can be seen side by side. In cut down a tree, down is a particle and the object moves freely — cut the tree down, cut it down. Add on and everything freezes: cut down on salt admits no rearrangement, which is the test that shows on is a fixed preposition and the three words a single unit. In meaning, cut down on and cut back on are interchangeable, though budgets and spending prefer cut back; cut out is total (cut out soda, and note the movable particle: cut it out); and reduce does the same job with no preposition at all — reduce sodium, ✗ reduce on sodium.

Do or use less of something.

  1. The doctor told him to cut down on salt, not to eliminate it.
  2. We've cut down on driving since gas went back over four dollars.
  3. The new form cuts down on the number of calls the office gets.
  4. She cut down on coffee for a month and slept no better.
  5. Both departments were asked to cut down on travel this quarter.
  6. Sorting the recycling properly cuts down on contamination at the plant.
See also
check up on · abstain from · come up with · prevent from · on
