deal with, deal in#

Pronunciation
/ˈdil wɪθ/ · DEEL with · weak /ˈdil wəθ/
Русский
иметь дело с, улаживать, быть о чём-то (with); торговать чем-то (in)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (deal with getting the permits), or a wh-clause (deal with whatever the inspector finds) — never a to-infinitive (✗ deal with to get the permits) and never a that-clause (✗ deal with that he quit; use deal with the fact that he quit). Deal in takes a noun phrase naming goods or a kind of thing, and nothing else. The preposition is fixed in both: the object never moves, so ✗ deal it with is not English.
Pattern
deal with [a problem, a person, a subject] · deal with [verb-ing] · deal in [antiques, futures, rumor] · deal [someone] [cards, a hand] — no preposition · deal [a blow] to [someone]
Contrast
Deal with handles something; deal in trades in something. A manager deals with complaints; a gallery deals in prints. The two are never interchangeable: ✗ the gallery deals with prints says it is coping with them. Note also that deal with covers two jobs English usually splits — take action about a problem (deal with the leak) and be about a subject (the chapter deals with tariffs) — and that the transitive deal takes no preposition at all: deal the cards, deal a blow.

Handle a problem or a person, be about a subject — or, with in, trade in a kind of goods.

  1. Somebody has to deal with the flooding in the basement before the drywall goes up.
  2. I'll deal with him on Monday when everyone has cooled off.
  3. The second chapter deals with the collapse of the savings-and-loans.
  4. She dealt with being laid off better than anyone expected.
  5. His uncle deals in rare coins out of a shop on Grand Avenue.
  6. That firm deals in municipal bonds and nothing else.
See also
cope with · put up with · take care of · dispose of · with

decide on, decide against#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈsaɪd ɑn/ · dih-SIDE on · weak /dɪˈsaɪd ən/
Русский
останавливаться на чём-то (on); решать отказаться от чего-то (against)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
on and against take a noun phrase, a gerund (decided against moving), or a wh-clause (decided on where to eat) — never a to-infinitive after the preposition (✗ decided on to move). Decide also takes a bare to-infinitive with no preposition (decided to move) and a that-clause (decided that it wasn't worth it).
Pattern
decide on [a choice] · decide against [a course / verb-ing] · decide to [verb] · decide not to [verb] · decide that [clause] · decide between [A] and [B] · decide [a case, a question]
Contrast
Decide to leave and decide on leaving are both correct and mean the same thing — the first is the infinitive frame, the second the prepositional one — but you cannot mix them: ✗ decide on to leave, ✗ decide leaving. Decide against is the negative of decide on, not of decide to: the negative of decided to sell is decided not to sell, and decided against selling is its prepositional twin. Note also transitive decide, which takes no preposition and belongs to courts and referees: the judge decided the case.

Settle on one option after weighing them.

  1. They finally decided on the yellow one.
  2. She decided against law school after two weeks of shadowing.
  3. We haven't decided on where to hold it.
  4. The board decided against raising dues this year.
  5. He decided on the cheaper contractor and regretted it by March.
  6. After all that, they decided to stay put.
See also
choose between · agree on · insist on · plan on · vote for

dedicate to, devote to#

Pronunciation
/ˈdɛdəˌkeɪt tu/ · DED-uh-kayt too · weak /ˈdɛdəˌkeɪt tə/
Русский
посвящать чему-то или кому-то — отдавать целиком; после to герундий
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral; dedicate is the more ceremonial of the two
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (devoted the summer to rebuilding the porch) — to is a preposition here, so a verb after it must be -ing: ✗ dedicated to preserve the wetland, ✗ devoted himself to restore it. Never a clause. In the active, both verbs need a direct object before the to — the thing given over, or a reflexive: dedicate the fund to scholarships, devote yourself to the work. In the participial forms that object drops away, but the to and its gerund do not: dedicated to ending hunger, devoted to running the shop.
Pattern
dedicate [something] to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · devote [time, money, oneself] to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · be dedicated to / be devoted to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · dedicate [a book, a building] to [a person]
Contrast
Dedicated to preserving and ✗ dedicated to preserve differ by a suffix and by a whole grammar. This is the look forward to trap wearing formal clothes, and it shows up constantly in mission statements, where an organization dedicated to ending hunger is right and ✗ dedicated to end hunger is not. The two verbs part company in one place: dedicate alone carries the ceremonial sense of naming something in someone's honor — dedicate the library to her memory, the album is dedicated to his father — which devote never does. In the other direction devote is the ordinary word for spending time (devoted three weeks to the appendix), where dedicate sounds grand. Note that the adjective devoted can stand alone meaning loyal (a devoted friend), and that ✗ dedicate on and ✗ devote for are errors.

Give something over entirely to a purpose or a person.

  1. The foundation is dedicated to restoring salmon habitat.
  2. She devoted most of her thirties to getting the clinic open.
  3. He dedicated the book to the teacher who failed him twice.
  4. Two floors are devoted to storage that nobody has opened in years.
  5. They devoted themselves to raising the money before the roof gave out.
  6. The wing was dedicated to the founder's wife in 1961.
See also
commit to · look forward to · object to · contribute to · specialize in · to

deduce from#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈdus frʌm/ · dih-DOOSS from · weak /dɪˈdus frəm/
Русский
выводить, заключать из — доходить рассуждением от известного
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral, leaning formal — logic, detective stories, and academic prose
Complement
from takes a noun phrase naming the evidence, or a gerund (deduced from watching the tape) — never a to-infinitive (✗ deduced from to watch the tape). What is deduced is the direct object, and it may be a noun phrase or a that-clause: deduced that the lock had been picked. The preposition disappears in front of that clause: ✗ deduced from that the lock had been picked.
Pattern
deduce [something] from [evidence] · deduce that [clause] from [evidence] · [something] can be deduced from [evidence] · deduce [something] — no preposition, when the evidence is understood
Contrast
Deduce from and infer from share a preposition and split the work. Deduce reasons down from a rule or a premise and claims certainty; infer reads a likely conclusion off the evidence and claims only probability. Derive from is about origin rather than reasoning (the word derives from Latin), and extrapolate from projects a known trend past the data you have. All four take from and none of them takes of: ✗ deduce of the evidence.

Work something out by reasoning from what you already know.

  1. From the mud on the boots he deduced that she had come through the orchard.
  2. You can deduce the missing figure from the two subtotals.
  3. Nothing about his motive can be deduced from the emails alone.
  4. She deduced from the silence on the call that the offer had already been made.
  5. The students were asked to deduce the rule from eight sample sentences.
  6. He deduced from watching two rehearsals that the show would run long.
See also
infer from · derive from · extrapolate from · follow from · from

default on#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈfɔlt ɑn/ · dih-FAWLT on · weak /dɪˈfɔlt ən/
Русский
не платить по (кредиту), нарушать обязательство; фин./юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral — finance, lending, and law
Complement
noun phrase naming an obligation: a loan, a mortgage, a payment, a bond, a lease, an agreement. A gerund is unidiomatic (✗ defaulted on paying the rent — say defaulted on the rent), and no clause is possible (✗ defaulted on that he owed us). The verb is also fine with no object at all (the borrower defaulted in March), and there it is not a dependent preposition, just an intransitive verb.
Pattern
default on [a loan, a payment, an obligation] · default — intransitive · default to [a setting, a value] — a different verb · be in default on [a loan] · default judgment — the noun
Contrast
Default on is failing to pay; default to is falling back on a preset. The county defaulted on its bonds is a financial disaster; the form defaults to Eastern time is a software convenience, and the two never mix: ✗ defaulted to its bonds. The legal fixed phrase is in default of (in default of payment), which is a complex preposition rather than this verb. Compare renege on a promise, which is the same frame applied to a commitment nobody signed.

Fail to make a payment or meet an obligation you are bound to.

  1. Two homeowners on the block defaulted on their mortgages that spring.
  2. If the company defaults on the note, the collateral goes to the bank.
  3. He defaulted on a student loan in 2011 and spent a decade fixing his credit.
  4. The country has defaulted on its external debt three times in forty years.
  5. Tenants who default on rent get thirty days' notice under the ordinance.
  6. Nobody expected a AAA borrower to default.
See also
foreclose on · dispose of · pay for · in default of · on

defer to#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈfɚ tu/ · dih-FUR too · weak /dɪˈfɚ tə/
Русский
уступать чьему-то мнению или власти — полагаться на чужое суждение
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — courts, committees, and polite disagreement
Complement
noun phrase naming a person, a body, or somebody's judgment or expertise. A gerund is grammatical but rare (deferred to having the committee decide), and a to-infinitive is impossible (✗ defer to decide — that is the infinitive of a different verb, meaning postpone). No clause: ✗ defer to that she knows more.
Pattern
defer to [a person, a court, an authority] · defer to [someone's judgment, expertise, wishes] · defer [a decision, payment, maintenance] — postpone, no preposition · defer [something] to [a later date, the next meeting]
Contrast
One spelling, two verbs. Defer to the board is yielding to somebody else's authority; defer the vote is putting it off, and that second verb takes a direct object, not to. Both can appear with to and mean different things: we deferred to the chair (yielded) versus we deferred the item to June (postponed). In appellate writing defer to is a term of art — the court defers to the agency's reading — and deference to is its noun. Do not confuse it with differ from.

Yield to somebody else's judgment or authority instead of your own.

  1. On anything involving the tax code I defer to her.
  2. The panel deferred to the trial judge's findings of fact.
  3. Younger associates learn quickly which partners expect to be deferred to.
  4. He deferred to his brother's wishes about the funeral arrangements.
  5. Reviewing courts defer to the agency where the statute is genuinely ambiguous.
  6. She rarely defers to anyone on questions of structural engineering.
See also
refer to · comply with · go along with · rely on · to

depart from#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈpɑrt frʌm/ · dih-PART from · weak /dɪˈpɑrt frəm/
Русский
отправляться из; отступать от (правила, традиции, текста)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — travel announcements for the literal sense, policy and legal prose for the figurative one
Complement
noun phrase naming a place, or a practice, text, precedent, or norm. A gerund is possible but stiff (departed from following the script); no to-infinitive (✗ depart from to follow the script) and no clause. Depart is also intransitive with no object at all (the train departs at six), and there nothing is a dependent preposition.
Pattern
depart from [a city, a gate, a station] · depart from [precedent, the text, standard practice] · depart for [a destination] · depart — intransitive · a departure from [the norm] — the noun keeps from
Contrast
From is where you leave; for is where you are headed. Departed from Denver and departed for Denver are opposite trips. In the figurative sense the neighbors divide finely: depart from a practice is a deliberate break, deviate from a plan is a step off a required path, diverge from a route is a gradual separation, and differ from is not motion at all but a static comparison. Note that in ordinary American speech nobody departs from a house — that is leave — and the verb belongs to airports, timetables, and briefs.

Leave a place, or break with an established practice or text.

  1. The 6:12 to Chicago departs from Track 9.
  2. The opinion departs from thirty years of settled precedent in two paragraphs.
  3. He never departs from the recipe, which is why the bread is always the same.
  4. The translation departs from the original in ways the preface never admits.
  5. Delegates departed from the capital before the vote was certified.
  6. Any departure from the protocol has to be logged.
See also
deviate from · diverge from · differ from · derogate from · from

depend on#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈpɛnd ɑn/ · dih-PEND on · weak /dɪˈpɛnd ən/
Русский
зависеть от; полагаться на — определяться чем-то или доверять кому-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (depend on winning) · also a wh-clause (it depends on what she says) — never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. A to-infinitive is correct only after a person: depend on him to be late.
Pattern
[subject] depend on [noun phrase] · depend on [someone] to [verb] · depend on [verb-ing] · it depends on [wh-clause]
Variants
depend upon — identical in meaning, more formal and more common in writing
Contrast
Depend of and depend from are errors — the preposition is fixed. Bare depend with no preposition works only in the elliptical It depends. Note the two senses: depend on someone (rely on) versus depend on something (be determined by), which is why That depends on the weather is not about trust. Against its neighbors, rely on is the neutral synonym of the first sense only, and count on the warm spoken one; neither carries the be-determined-by sense.

Rely on, or be determined by.

  1. The whole schedule depends on whether the permit clears.
  2. She has three kids depending on her.
  3. You can depend on him to be five minutes late.
  4. How long it takes depends on traffic.
  5. Funding for next year depends upon the audit.
  6. Everything depends on getting the roof done before November.
See also
rely on · count on · believe in · on · depending on

deprive of#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈpraɪv əv/ · dih-PRIVE uv · weak /dɪˈpraɪv ə/
Русский
лишать чего-то — отнимать или не давать иметь
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal to neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (deprived of seeing his kids) — never a to-infinitive (✗ deprived of to see them) and never a clause. The person deprived is a direct object: deprive someone of something, never ✗ deprive of someone something.
Pattern
deprive [someone] of [something] · be deprived of [something] · sleep-deprived, oxygen-deprived (compounds)
Contrast
Deprive from is an error. Compare the family: deprive someone of takes something away that they had or needed; rob someone of does the same thing with more violence or unfairness; strip someone of a title or a rank is formal and public. All three keep of. Contrast prevent someone from doing, which blocks an action rather than removing a thing — a different preposition because a different structure. The past participle has become a productive compound: sleep-deprived, deprived neighborhoods.

Take something away from someone, or keep them from having it.

  1. The blackout deprived half the county of power for three days.
  2. He was deprived of counsel for the first thirty-six hours.
  3. A newborn deprives both parents of sleep for about a year.
  4. The redistricting deprived the neighborhood of a representative it had had since 1974.
  5. Deprived of sunlight, the seedlings went pale and leggy.
  6. She refused to deprive herself of one small pleasure a day.
See also
rob of · prevent from · protect from · save from · of

derive from#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈraɪv frʌm/ · dih-RIVE from · weak /dɪˈraɪv frəm/
Русский
происходить от; извлекать из — брать начало в источнике
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral, leaning formal — etymology, finance, and academic prose
Complement
from takes a noun phrase naming a source, or a gerund (derives from being the only supplier in the county) — never a to-infinitive (✗ derives from to be the only supplier) and never a clause. Two frames share the verb: transitive derive [pleasure, income, authority] from [a source], and intransitive derive from, where the subject is the thing that came out of the source.
Pattern
derive [something] from [a source] · [something] derive from [an origin] · be derived from [a source] · a derivative of [something] — the noun takes of
Contrast
Derive from is about where something came from; deduce from is about how you reasoned. A word derives from Latin, but a conclusion is deduced from the evidence, and swapping them is a real error in student writing. Against the neighbors: stem from points at a cause (the delay stems from a supply problem), result from names the outcome of an event, and follow from is strictly logical. Note the noun switches prepositions — a derivative of morphine, never ✗ a derivative from morphine.

Come from a source, or take something from a source.

  1. Most of the county's revenue derives from a single property tax.
  2. The word derives from an Old French verb meaning to trap.
  3. She derives an unusual amount of satisfaction from labeling things.
  4. The board's authority derives from a 1974 ordinance nobody has read since.
  5. Their advantage derives from being the only supplier within four hundred miles.
  6. The drug is derived from a fungus that grows on rye.
See also
stem from · result in, result from · deduce from · follow from · from

derogate from#

Pronunciation
/ˈdɛrəɡeɪt frʌm/ · DAIR-uh-gayt from · weak /ˈdɛrəɡeɪt frəm/
Русский
умалять, ущемлять (право, норму) — юр., оговорка в договорах
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal and legal — contract boilerplate, treaties, and judicial opinions. An American meets it in one place above all: the clause reading nothing in this Agreement shall derogate from the rights of either party. It is not a word anyone says out loud.
Complement
noun phrase naming a right, an authority, a standard, or a reputation. No gerund, no infinitive, no clause in real use: ✗ derogate from being paid. The verb has no everyday intransitive life; the fixed legal phrase in derogation of covers the same ground as a complex preposition.
Pattern
derogate from [a right, an obligation, an authority] · nothing herein shall derogate from [something] · derogate from [someone's reputation, achievement] — literary · in derogation of [a right] — the fixed phrase
Contrast
Do not reach for it when you mean denigrate. Derogate from takes something away from a right or a standard; denigrate attacks a person's character, and it is transitive: denigrated his work, never ✗ denigrated from his work. The plain-English equivalents are detract from and take away from, and either is better anywhere outside a contract. The adjective derogatory belongs to the denigrate side of the family, which is exactly why the verb gets misused.

Take away from a right, a rule, or a standard — chiefly in legal drafting.

  1. Nothing in this section shall derogate from the tenant's statutory remedies.
  2. The amendment does not derogate from the treaty's core protections.
  3. Counsel argued that the waiver derogated from rights the legislature made non-waivable.
  4. No local ordinance may derogate from the state minimum.
  5. None of this derogates from the achievement of the people who built it.
  6. The court refused to read the clause as derogating from the warranty.
See also
depart from · desist from · divest of · in derogation of · from

desist from#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈzɪst frʌm/ · dih-ZIST from · weak /dɪˈzɪst frəm/
Русский
прекращать что-то делать — обычно по требованию; юр. (cease and desist)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal and legal. In American life it is nearly a one-phrase word: the cease and desist letter, and the injunction ordering a party to cease and desist from something. Using it conversationally sounds like a joke about lawyers.
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (desist from contacting her) — never a to-infinitive (✗ desist from to contact her) and never a clause. The verb also stands alone with no object (the picketing did not desist), where nothing follows it at all.
Pattern
desist from [conduct] · desist from [verb-ing] · cease and desist from [verb-ing] · desist — intransitive
Contrast
Refrain from is the neutral version and stop is what people actually say. All three take the same complement — refrain from smoking, desist from smoking — but desist implies an order already given, usually in writing, while refrain is a request or a personal choice. Its formal cousin abstain from is about deliberately going without something (food, alcohol, a vote). None of them takes an infinitive.

Stop doing something, usually because you have been told to.

  1. The letter demanded that he desist from using the company's logo.
  2. The court ordered the contractor to cease and desist from further excavation.
  3. She was told to desist from contacting the witnesses directly.
  4. The agency asked two clinics to desist from advertising the treatment as a cure.
  5. Neither side would desist from the filings long enough to talk.
  6. They desisted from the practice only after the fine doubled.
See also
refrain from · prevent from · discourage from · derogate from · from

deviate from#

Pronunciation
/ˈdiviˌeɪt frʌm/ · DEE-vee-ayt from · weak /ˈdiviˌeɪt frəm/
Русский
отклоняться от — отступать от курса, правила или стандарта
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — protocols, procedures, statistics
Complement
noun phrase naming a plan, a script, a protocol, a norm, or a value. A gerund is possible but uncommon (deviated from following the checklist); no to-infinitive (✗ deviate from to follow the checklist) and no clause. The verb is otherwise intransitive: the readings deviated sharply takes no object at all.
Pattern
deviate from [a plan, the script, the mean] · deviate — intransitive · a deviation from [the norm] — the noun keeps from · standard deviation — the fixed statistical term
Contrast
Deviate from implies there was a rule to leave. You deviate from a protocol, a flight path, or an agreed script — something prescribed. Depart from is the same move made deliberately and often defensibly (depart from precedent); diverge from is a gradual parting of two things that started together; differ from is not movement at all, just unlikeness. And the noun of deviate is deviation: a deviation from the norm, never ✗ a deviation of the norm.

Leave a course, a rule, or a standard you were expected to follow.

  1. Do not deviate from the checklist, even when you are sure of the next step.
  2. The pilot deviated from the filed route to get around the weather.
  3. This year's numbers deviate from the ten-year average by about four percent.
  4. He gave the same talk for a decade and never once deviated from it.
  5. Any lab that deviates from the published method has to say so in the writeup.
  6. The remodel deviated from the approved drawings in three places.
See also
depart from · diverge from · differ from · derogate from · from

devolve on, devolve upon#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈvɑlv ɑn/ · dih-VAHLV on · weak /dɪˈvɑlv ən/
Русский
переходить к кому-то по наследству или по закону; юр./книжн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal and legal — wills, constitutions, and successions; an American meets it in an estate document or a line about who takes over when an office falls vacant
Complement
noun phrase naming the person or body that receives the duty, estate, or power. No gerund, no infinitive, no clause: ✗ the duty devolved on running the office. The subject is the thing handed down — a duty, a title, an estate, an authority — not the person taking it.
Variants
Devolve upon is the same pattern in a more formal key, and older drafting prefers it. Devolve to appears where the point is transfer downward to a smaller unit — powers devolved to the states, devolved government. Keep all three apart from devolve into, which is the everyday word meaning degenerate.
Pattern
[a duty, an estate, a title] devolve on / upon [someone] · [powers] be devolved to [a body] · devolve into [chaos, a shouting match] — a different sense · devolution of [power] — the noun
Contrast
Devolve on is passive inheritance; delegate to is a deliberate handoff. A responsibility devolves on whoever is left standing; a manager delegates it to somebody chosen. And devolve into is not this word at all — the meeting devolved into a shouting match means it deteriorated, and swapping the prepositions produces nonsense: ✗ the chairmanship devolved into the vice chair. In plain American English the whole first sense is usually fall to: the job fell to me.

Pass down to somebody by default, succession, or operation of law.

  1. On his death the estate devolved on his sister.
  2. If the chair resigns, the duties devolve upon the ranking member.
  3. Responsibility for the audit devolved on the one accountant who had not quit.
  4. Under the 1998 act, substantial powers were devolved to the regional assemblies.
  5. The title devolves upon the eldest surviving child, whatever the will says.
  6. Everything the founder used to handle personally has devolved on a committee.
See also
impose on, impose upon · preside over · inhere in · belong to · upon

die of, die from#

Pronunciation
/ˈdaɪ əv/ · DYE uv · weak /ˈdaɪ ə/
Русский
умирать от — предлог называет причину смерти
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (died from falling) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. Die also takes for (a cause), in (an event or place), and by in a small set of fixed phrases (die by suicide, die by the sword).
Pattern
die of [a disease, a condition] · die from [an injury, a cause, complications] · die for [a cause] · die in [a crash, a fire, a war] · be dying to [verb] — idiom, infinitive
Contrast
Die of and die from overlap heavily in American English and reversing them will not confuse anyone. The tendency, not a rule: of goes with diseases and internal conditions (died of pancreatic cancer, died of a heart attack), from with external causes, injuries, and chains of consequence (died from his injuries, died from complications of surgery). Die by is restricted — by suicide, by fire, by his own hand — and died by suicide is now the preferred American phrasing over the older committed suicide. Note the hyperboles, which take of: dying of thirst, died of embarrassment.

Stop living — and the preposition names the cause.

  1. Her grandfather died of emphysema at eighty-one.
  2. Two firefighters died from smoke inhalation.
  3. He nearly died of embarrassment when the microphone stayed on.
  4. Thousands died from the complications rather than the infection itself.
  5. She died in a crash on the interstate.
  6. I'm dying of thirst — is there anything cold in there?
See also
suffer from · recover from · result from · save from

differ from#

Pronunciation
/ˈdɪfɚ frʌm/ · DIF-er from · weak /ˈdɪfɚ frəm/
Русский
отличаться от — быть непохожим; differ with расходиться во мнениях
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal to neutral
Complement
noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause. Differ is intransitive and never takes an object: ✗ differ the other model. In the disagree sense it takes with: I differ with her on that. The related adjective takes its own preposition — see the contrast.
Pattern
[A] differ from [B] · [A] differ from [B] in [respect] · differ with [someone] on / about [something] · differ (plural subject, alone): opinions differ
Variants
different from — the safe standard everywhere · different than — established American, and the natural form before a clause · different to — British; avoid in American writing
Contrast
The adjective is where the trouble is. Different from is standard everywhere and always safe. Different than is fully established in American English, and is the natural choice before a clause (a different city than I remembered), where from forces an awkward rewrite. Different to is British and sounds wrong to American ears. The verb itself allows only from for unlikeness — ✗ differs than, ✗ differs to — and with only when people disagree: the two economists differ with each other about the cause.

Be unlike something else — or, with with, disagree.

  1. This year's model differs from last year's in almost every respect.
  2. American practice differs from the European standard here.
  3. The two accounts differ from each other on every material point.
  4. Her reading of the statute differs from mine in one important way.
  5. I differ with him about how much of this the public needs to see.
  6. On the question of timing, reasonable people differ.
See also
compare with · separate from · compete with · different from · from

disapprove of#

Pronunciation
/ˌdɪsəˈpruv əv/ · diss-uh-PROOV uv · weak /ˌdɪsəˈpruv ə/
Русский
не одобрять — считать неправильным или неприемлемым
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral, slightly formal
Complement
noun phrase, gerund, or possessive plus gerund (disapproved of her going) — never a to-infinitive (✗ disapprove of to go) and never a that-clause.
Pattern
disapprove of [noun phrase] · disapprove of [verb-ing] · disapprove of [someone] -ing · disapprove (alone) · a disapproving look
Contrast
Unlike approve, disapprove has no ordinary transitive use. The board approved the budget is authorization; ✗ The board disapproved the budget is not the mirror image American editors want — write rejected or voted down. So disapprove survives almost entirely in the of frame, where it means think badly of. Note the register: it is a mild, slightly prim word, which is why she disapproves of him is funnier than she dislikes him.

Think that something is wrong or unacceptable.

  1. My mother disapproved of the whole arrangement and said so once, at length.
  2. He disapproves of eating at your desk.
  3. The faculty disapproved of the change but voted for it anyway.
  4. She disapproved of his taking the money.
  5. Nobody actually disapproves of a shorter meeting.
  6. They disapprove of almost everything the new coach does.
See also
approve of · object to · complain about · comment on

discourage from#

Pronunciation
/dɪsˈkɜrɪdʒ frʌm/ · diss-KUR-ij from · weak /dɪsˈkɜrɪdʒ frəm/
Русский
отговаривать от — убеждать не делать чего-то; после from герундий
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
gerund or noun phrase (discouraged him from applying) — never a to-infinitive (✗ discouraged him to apply). This is the classic asymmetry: encourage takes an infinitive and discourage takes from plus -ing.
Pattern
discourage [someone] from [verb-ing] · discourage [an activity] · be discouraged from [verb-ing] · encourage [someone] to [verb] — the mirror pattern
Contrast
The pair does not mirror. Encourage her to apply / discourage her from applying — same idea, opposite structures, and ✗ discourage her to apply is one of the most persistent advanced errors in English. The same shape governs prevent from, stop from, keep from, ban from, prohibit from, and bar from: all of them take from plus -ing while their positive counterparts take an infinitive. Note also that discourage has a plain transitive use meaning dishearten (the results discouraged her), which takes no preposition.

Try to persuade someone not to do something.

  1. The counselor discouraged him from taking five APs at once.
  2. Steep fees discourage small filers from appealing at all.
  3. She was discouraged from applying by an advisor who barely knew her.
  4. Nothing discourages a reader from finishing a book like a bad first paragraph.
  5. The policy is designed to discourage people from driving downtown.
  6. Don't let one rejection discourage you from sending it out again.
See also
prevent from · punish for · allow for · protect from

dispose of#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈspoʊz ʌv/ · dih-SPOHZ uv · weak /dɪˈspoʊz əv/
Русский
избавляться от; распродавать; окончательно решать (дело); книжн. к get rid of
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — waste rules, estate documents, and court dockets
Complement
noun phrase naming what is being got rid of, sold, or settled: dispose of the packaging, dispose of the assets, dispose of the motion. A gerund is rare and stiff (dispose of having to store it), and there is no infinitive and no clause: ✗ dispose of to sell the house. The preposition is fixed and the object never moves: ✗ dispose the trash of.
Pattern
dispose of [waste, property, a body, an argument, a case] · be disposed of [properly, at auction] · dispose [someone] to [something] — a different verb · be disposed to [verb] — an adjective with an infinitive · at [someone's] disposal — the noun
Contrast
Dispose of is the formal twin of get rid of, and it does three jobs: throw away (dispose of the solvent), sell off (dispose of the property), and settle for good (the court disposed of the appeal in a footnote). Keep it clear of be disposed to, which is an adjective meaning inclined and takes an infinitive: disposed to agree, never ✗ disposed to agreeing. And the noun is disposal — the disposal of hazardous waste, the money at your disposal.

Get rid of something, sell it off, or settle a matter finally.

  1. There is a fee for disposing of paint and motor oil.
  2. The estate had to dispose of the house before the taxes came due.
  3. The panel disposed of the objection in two sentences.
  4. Nobody could say how the company disposed of the old servers.
  5. She disposed of her shares the week before the merger closed.
  6. He disposed of the last three questions without looking at his notes.
See also
get rid of · divest of · deprive of · take care of · of

diverge from#

Pronunciation
/daɪˈvɚdʒ frʌm/ · dye-VURJ from · weak /dɪˈvɚdʒ frəm/
Русский
расходиться с — начать отличаться от того, чему прежде соответствовал
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — mathematics, data, narrative, and commentary
Complement
noun phrase naming a path, a text, a norm, or another account. A gerund is unidiomatic (✗ diverged from following the trail), and there is no infinitive and no clause. The verb is very often intransitive with a plural subject and no preposition at all: the two roads diverge, their estimates diverge sharply.
Pattern
diverge from [a route, an account, the consensus] · [two things] diverge — intransitive · diverge on / over [a question] — disagree about · a divergence from [the mean] · divergent [views]
Contrast
Four verbs, one preposition, four pictures. Diverge from is a gradual parting of two things that started together; deviate from is a step off a path you were required to keep to; depart from is a deliberate break with a practice or a text; differ from is not motion at all, only unlikeness. So the trail diverges from the creek (they separate), the driver deviated from the route (he broke a rule), the opinion departs from precedent (a considered break), my copy differs from yours (a static fact). Its opposite is converge, which takes on or toward rather than from.

Move apart, or come to differ, from something you started out matching.

  1. The bike path diverges from the highway just past the reservoir.
  2. Her account diverges from his in every detail that matters.
  3. After 1994 the two economies diverged from one another for good.
  4. This year's figures diverge from the model's forecast by a wide margin.
  5. The screenplay diverges from the novel about halfway through.
  6. Where the sources diverge, the footnote says so.
See also
deviate from · depart from · differ from · derive from · from

divest of#

Pronunciation
/daɪˈvɛst ʌv/ · dye-VEST uv · weak /dɪˈvɛst əv/
Русский
лишать(ся) чего-то, продавать (активы, доли); фин./юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — securities filings, trust documents, and university endowment fights; the literary sense (divested of illusions) belongs to essays rather than speech
Complement
a person, company, or fund is the object, and of takes a noun phrase naming what is given up: holdings, powers, a title, illusions. A gerund is rare (divested itself of having to run the plant), and there is no infinitive and no clause. The reflexive frame is extremely common: divest itself of the subsidiary.
Variants
Divest from — the trustees voted to divest from fossil fuels — has become standard American usage in the divestment-campaign sense, where the object of the verb disappears and from names the sector being exited. Older editors still prefer divest itself of, but divest from is now the ordinary way the argument is phrased.
Pattern
divest [someone / a fund] of [holdings, power, a title] · divest oneself of [something] · be divested of [something] · divest from [a sector] · divestiture / divestment of [assets] — the nouns keep of
Contrast
Divest of removes something formally; deprive of takes it away against your interest. A board divests itself of a money-losing division on purpose; a storm deprives a town of power. Rob of and strip of are the hostile members of the same family, and get rid of is the plain-English one. Note that the of is not optional in the transitive frame: ✗ divested the subsidiary means something quite different — that you sold the subsidiary itself.

Give up a holding, a power, or an illusion — usually deliberately and formally.

  1. The company divested itself of three regional papers in a single quarter.
  2. Cabinet nominees are expected to divest themselves of conflicting holdings.
  3. The statute divested the board of any say in the appointment.
  4. Two decades on the road had divested him of every romantic idea about touring.
  5. The endowment finally voted to divest from coal and tar sands.
  6. Once divested of its patents, the firm was worth very little.
See also
deprive of · rob of · dispose of · get rid of · of

draw on, draw upon#

Pronunciation
/ˈdrɔ ɑn/ · DRAW on · weak /ˈdrɔ ən/
Русский
черпать из — использовать запас денег, опыта или материала как источник
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — scholarship, journalism, and banking (draw on a line of credit)
Complement
noun phrase naming a resource — savings, experience, sources, a reserve — or a gerund (drew on having run a kitchen for ten years). No to-infinitive (✗ draw on to have run a kitchen) and no clause. The preposition is fixed, not a movable particle: ✗ draw my savings on, ✗ draw it on.
Variants
Draw upon is the same pattern one register higher, common in academic prose and prefaces; the meaning is identical.
Pattern
draw on / upon [a resource, an account, experience] · draw [something] from [a source] · draw down [a balance] · draw on — intransitive, of time (as evening drew on)
Contrast
Do not confuse the fixed draw on with the separable phrasal verbs. Draw up a contract, draw out a meeting, and draw in a crowd all allow the object to move — draw the contract up — which is what makes their second words particles rather than prepositions; those belong with the phrasal verbs, not here. This draw on never separates. Note the sister frame draw [something] from [a source] — drew her material from letters — where the resource follows from and what you took becomes the object. And as evening drew on is a third, intransitive verb meaning approach.

Use a store of money, experience, or material as a source.

  1. The essay draws on three unpublished diaries.
  2. They had to draw on savings for the second year of treatment.
  3. She draws on twenty years in emergency medicine every time she teaches.
  4. The company drew on its credit line to make payroll in February.
  5. His novels draw upon a childhood he describes only obliquely.
  6. The report draws on interviews with forty-one former employees.
See also
rely on · depend on · count on · derive from · on

dream about, dream of#

Pronunciation
/ˈdrim əˌbaʊt/ · DREEM uh-bowt · weak /ˈdrim əbət/
Русский
видеть во сне (about); мечтать о (of)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (dreamed of playing at Carnegie Hall) — never a to-infinitive (✗ dream of to play there). A clause takes no preposition: dreamed that the house was on fire. American English prefers dreamed; dreamt is chiefly British.
Pattern
dream about [what happened in a dream] · dream of [an ambition] · dream of / about [verb-ing] · dream that [clause] · wouldn't dream of [verb-ing] · dream up [an idea]
Contrast
Dream about reports what your sleeping brain did; dream of names what your waking self wants. I dreamed about my old apartment is a night's event; I dream of a corner office is an ambition. The two overlap freely for daydreaming (dreaming about / of retirement), but the fixed idiom takes only of: I wouldn't dream of asking means I would never — note the gerund, since ✗ wouldn't dream of to ask is impossible.

See something while asleep, or long for something while awake.

  1. I dreamed about that house for years after we sold it.
  2. She dreams of opening a bakery somewhere with a real winter.
  3. He dreamed that every tooth in his head fell out at once.
  4. They dreamed of going back and never did.
  5. I wouldn't dream of charging you for that.
  6. Half the class dreams about the exam the night before it.
See also
hope for · long for · wish for · think about · plan on

dress in#

Pronunciation
/ˈdrɛs ɪn/ · DRESS in · weak /ˈdrɛs ən/
Русский
одеваться во что-то — носить одежду определённого вида
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase naming clothing or a color — no gerund, no clause. As takes a character or role (dressed as a pirate), and up as is the costume version. Dress on its own is intransitive (dress warmly) or transitive with a person (dress the baby), and takes no preposition either way.
Pattern
be dressed in [clothing / a color] · dress in [a style] · dress as / up as [a character] · dress [someone] · dress for [an occasion, the weather] · dress up / down
Contrast
Dressed in is about the clothes; dressed as is about who you are pretending to be. Dressed in black describes fabric; dressed as a nun describes a costume. Dress for names the occasion or condition you are dressing to suit (dress for the weather, dress for the job you want). Note that dressed with is wrong for clothing but right for salad — dressed with a little vinaigrette — and that dress down means both wear casual clothes and reprimand someone.

Wear, or put on, a particular kind of clothing.

  1. She came to the reading dressed in head-to-toe corduroy.
  2. Everyone at the funeral was dressed in black except the deceased's brother.
  3. He dresses in the same three shirts on rotation.
  4. The kids came dressed as characters from books nobody had read.
  5. Dress for the weather; it's supposed to drop twenty degrees by four.
  6. The waiters were dressed in white jackets that had clearly seen better decades.
See also
cover with · provide with · in · as

E–G#

elaborate on#

Pronunciation
/ɪˈlæbəreɪt ɑn/ · ih-LAB-uh-rayt on · weak /ɪˈlæbəreɪt ən/
Русский
развивать мысль о — рассказывать подробнее о уже сказанном
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral, common in interviews, seminars, and reporting
Complement
noun phrase (elaborate on the second point), gerund (elaborate on being passed over), or a wh-clause (elaborate on what went wrong) — never a to-infinitive (✗ elaborate on to explain the delay) and never a that-clause: ✗ elaborate on that the delay was ours. The verb also stands alone with no object (Could you elaborate?), and there it takes no preposition at all.
Variants
Elaborate upon is the formal-register twin, at home in an academic paper or a brief; the meaning is identical, and on is what an American says.
Pattern
elaborate on / upon [a point, a claim, a topic] · elaborate on [verb-ing] · elaborate — intransitive · elaborate [a theory, a design] — transitive, meaning develop in detail
Contrast
Elaborate on and expand on are interchangeable; comment on is a step lighter, meaning simply to say something about a subject rather than to say more about it. Watch two things. First, transitive elaborate exists and means something else — elaborate a theory is to work it out in detail, not to say more about it. Second, explain takes no preposition before the thing explained (explain the rule, ✗ explain on the rule); the to in explain to me points at the listener, not the topic. And the adjective elaborate is stressed differently: /ɪˈlæbərət/.

Say more about something you have already mentioned.

  1. Would you elaborate on the second recommendation?
  2. She declined to elaborate on the reasons for her resignation.
  3. The memo elaborates on a policy that had been one sentence long.
  4. He elaborated on being the only engineer in a building full of salespeople.
  5. The witness was asked to elaborate on what he had seen from the porch.
  6. I'd rather not elaborate in front of the whole committee.
See also
comment on · explain to · concentrate on · refer to · on

encroach on, encroach upon#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈkroʊtʃ ɑn/ · in-KROHCH on · weak /ɪnˈkroʊtʃ ən/
Русский
посягать на, вторгаться в — понемногу захватывать чужое
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal-leaning — property law, zoning disputes, and civil-liberties commentary. An American most often meets it in a survey report (the fence encroaches on the neighbor's lot) or an editorial about government power.
Complement
noun phrase naming territory, a right, a domain, or time. A gerund is possible but uncommon (encroaching on having any evening to yourself); no to-infinitive (✗ encroach on to take his land) and no clause. The subject is usually a thing — a fence, a program, a deadline — rather than a person acting deliberately.
Variants
Encroach upon is the same pattern in a higher register and is common in judicial writing. The noun keeps the preposition: an encroachment on / upon [something].
Pattern
encroach on / upon [land, rights, authority, time] · an encroachment on [a right] · encroach — intransitive, of vegetation or development
Contrast
Encroach on is gradual advance across a line; infringe on is violation of a right. A hedge encroaches on a driveway inch by inch, but a knockoff infringes on a trademark the moment it ships. Impinge on is the physics-flavored version (one thing pressing against another), intrude on is social (intrude on a private conversation), and interfere with is the most general. All four resist the direct object: ✗ encroach the property line.

Advance gradually onto somebody else's land, rights, or time.

  1. The new deck encroaches on the setback by almost two feet.
  2. He argued that the ordinance encroaches on rights the state constitution protects.
  3. Work has begun to encroach on the one evening a week she keeps for herself.
  4. Sagebrush is encroaching upon what used to be grassland.
  5. The agency's new rule encroaches on territory Congress reserved to the states.
  6. Each department resented any attempt to encroach on its budget.
See also
infringe on, infringe upon · impose on, impose upon · interfere with, interfere in · on

escape from#

Pronunciation
/ɪˈskeɪp frʌm/ · ih-SKAYP from · weak /ɪˈskeɪp frəm/
Русский
убегать из, спасаться от — вырываться из заточения или опасности
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (escaped from having to testify) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. But the verb also has a transitive use with no preposition at all: escape injury, escape prosecution, escape notice — and there the from would be wrong.
Pattern
escape from [a place, a captor, a situation] · escape [a fate, notice, detection] — no preposition · escape to [somewhere] · [something] escape [someone] — elude the memory
Contrast
Escape from a place, but escape a consequence. You escape from prison, a burning car, or a bad marriage — a thing you were physically or literally inside. You escape injury, blame, punishment, or someone's notice — no preposition, because you were never inside those. Both are correct; using from with the second group (✗ escaped from injury) is the error. Note the idiom it escapes me, meaning I can't recall it, which is the transitive sense turned around.

Get away from confinement, danger, or something unpleasant.

  1. Two inmates escaped from the county facility on a Sunday morning.
  2. She escaped from the meeting by inventing a dentist appointment.
  3. Everyone in the front car escaped serious injury.
  4. He spent his twenties trying to escape from the town he grew up in.
  5. The detail had escaped everyone's notice until the audit.
  6. The name escapes me, but I'd know the face.
See also
recover from · prevent from · hide from · rescue from · save from

excuse for#

Pronunciation
/ɪkˈskjuz fɔr/ · ik-SKYOOZ for · weak /ɪkˈskjuz fɚ/
Русский
прощать за (небольшую провинность) — а excuse from освобождать от обязанности
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for takes a noun phrase or a gerund (excuse me for interrupting) — never a to-infinitive (✗ excuse me for to interrupt). from takes a duty or requirement. The person excused is a direct object and takes no preposition: ✗ excuse to me.
Pattern
excuse [someone] for [something / verb-ing] · excuse [someone] from [a duty] · excuse [someone] · excuse [behavior] · Excuse me · there's no excuse for [something]
Contrast
Excuse for forgives; excuse from releases. The dean excused her for the late filing means it was forgiven; The dean excused her from the requirement means she doesn't have to do it. Both are standard, and mixing them (✗ excused from the late filing) says something else. Note that excuse takes the person directly — like forgive and thank, unlike apologize, which needs to. The noun keeps for: no excuse for that.

Forgive a small offense, or release someone from an obligation.

  1. Excuse me for asking, but is any of this actually funded?
  2. The judge excused two jurors from service.
  3. You'll have to excuse the mess.
  4. Nothing excuses the way he spoke to her.
  5. She was excused from gym for the rest of the semester.
  6. There is no excuse for sending it out unproofed.
See also
forgive for · apologize for · blame for · punish for · thank for

explain to#

Pronunciation
/ɪkˈspleɪn tu/ · ik-SPLAYN too · weak /ɪkˈspleɪn tə/
Русский
объяснять кому-то — только explain smth to smb, не ✗ explain me smth
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to takes the person hearing it, and the person always needs it: ✗ explain me the rules. The thing explained is a direct object, a that-clause, or a wh-clause: explain the rules, explain that the rules changed, explain how it works. A gerund is possible as the object (explain being late) but a to-infinitive is not.
Pattern
explain [something] to [someone] · explain to [someone] that / how / why [clause] · explain [something] · explain [oneself]
Contrast
Explain me something is one of the most persistent errors in English, because explain looks like tell and behaves like say. Tell, give, show, send, and offer take the person as a bare indirect object — tell me the rules — while explain, say, describe, suggest, mention, announce, report, and introduce all require to: explain the rules to me, say it to me, describe it to me. When the explanation is long, put the person first: explain to the class why the second method fails.

Make something clear to someone.

  1. Could you explain the billing cycle to me one more time?
  2. She explained to the board why the pilot had run over budget.
  3. He never explained himself and never had to.
  4. Try explaining that to a nine-year-old.
  5. The letter explains to applicants exactly what documentation is required.
  6. Nobody could explain the discrepancy to the auditors.
See also
introduce to · refer to · comment on · apologize to

extrapolate from#

Pronunciation
/ɪkˈstræpəleɪt frʌm/ · ik-STRAP-uh-layt from · weak /ɪkˈstræpəleɪt frəm/
Русский
экстраполировать из — переносить известную закономерность за пределы данных
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral-technical — statistics, forecasting, lab work, and business planning
Complement
from takes a noun phrase naming the data or the case you are reasoning out of, or a gerund (extrapolating from having seen two quarters of it). No to-infinitive (✗ extrapolate from to see two quarters) and no clause. What is projected may appear as a direct object before from: extrapolate a trend from the sample.
Pattern
extrapolate from [data, a sample, one case] · extrapolate [a trend, a value] from [data] · extrapolate [a curve] to [a future point] · extrapolation from [the data] — the noun keeps from
Contrast
Extrapolate from projects past the edge of your data; interpolate fills in between points you already have. Against the reasoning verbs: deduce from claims certainty from a premise, infer from claims probability from evidence, and extrapolate from claims a continuation of a pattern — which is why it so often carries a warning: you can't extrapolate from a single quarter. Note the second preposition in the technical frame: you extrapolate from the known values to the unknown one.

Project a known pattern beyond the evidence you actually have.

  1. It's a mistake to extrapolate from one unusually warm winter.
  2. The team extrapolated the growth curve from eighteen months of billing data.
  3. Nothing in the sample lets you extrapolate from the city to the state as a whole.
  4. She extrapolated from two cases and got the policy badly wrong.
  5. Forecasters extrapolate from the last five days of ice loss, which is why the numbers move so much.
  6. Extrapolating from the pilot program, the district projected a shortfall of $4 million.
See also
infer from · deduce from · derive from · follow from · from

face up to, back out of, drop out of, walk out on#

Pronunciation
/ˈfeɪs ʌp tu/ · FAYSS up too · weak /ˈfeɪs əp tə/
Русский
признать и принять (face up to); отступиться — back out of, drop out of, walk out on
Type
verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional)
Register
informal to neutral — ordinary spoken American English
Complement
noun phrase or gerund after the final preposition — face up to being broke, backed out of buying the house, dropped out of teaching altogether, walked out on a twenty-year marriage. Never a to-infinitive (✗ face up to be broke, ✗ back out of to buy it) and never a clause. Each of the four takes its own kind of object: face up to a fact or a failing, back out of an agreement, drop out of a school or a process, walk out on a person or a commitment.
Pattern
face up to [a fact, a mistake, a debt] · back out of [a deal, a wedding, a promise] · drop out of [school, a race, the workforce] · walk out on [a spouse, a job, a negotiation] · [any of them] + [verb-ing]
Contrast
These are three-part verbs, and the giveaway is that the object cannot move. With a genuine separable particle you have a choice — turn the light on or turn on the light, throw the mail out or throw out the mail — and those belong to the phrasal-verb discussion rather than to this file, because the second word is a particle, not a preposition. Here the whole string is welded together: ✗ face it up to, ✗ back the deal out of, ✗ drop school out of, ✗ walk his wife out on. The final word behaves exactly like the dependent prepositions in the rest of this file, which is why a gerund follows it and an infinitive never does. Note the two-part relatives that mean something else: face the music (no preposition), back out with no object (he backed out at the last minute), drop out alone (she dropped out in March), and walk out alone (the union walked out).

Confront something unpleasant, or withdraw from a commitment — four fixed three-word verbs on the same frame.

  1. At some point he is going to have to face up to what the surgery costs.
  2. She never faced up to being the reason the partnership dissolved.
  3. The buyer backed out of the deal two days before closing.
  4. Half the cohort dropped out of the program in the first semester.
  5. He walked out on the negotiation and did not come back.
  6. Nobody walks out on a pension that size without a plan.
  7. They backed out of renovating and sold the place as it stood.
See also
put up with · get away with · keep up with, catch up with, catch up on · live up to · look up to, look down on

follow from#

Pronunciation
/ˈfɑloʊ frʌm/ · FAH-loh from · weak /ˈfɑloʊ frəm/
Русский
следовать из — быть логическим следствием
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — logic, mathematics, and argument
Complement
from takes a noun phrase naming a premise, an assumption, or an earlier result, or a gerund (follows from assuming the rate is constant). No to-infinitive and no clause after the preposition. The subject is the conclusion, and the commonest frame puts it in a dummy it: it follows from the first two lemmas that the series converges.
Pattern
[a conclusion] follow from [a premise] · it follows from [X] that [clause] · it follows that [clause] · follow [a rule, a recipe, a car] — transitive, no preposition
Contrast
Follow from is logical consequence, not sequence. B follows A means B comes next; B follows from A means B is entailed by A, and the from is doing all the work. Keep it apart from transitive follow (follow the instructions, ✗ follow to the instructions) and from follow up on (follow up on the complaint), which means chase a matter further. The British follow on from — following on from last week's memo — is not American usage; write following or further to. Against the neighbors: result from names a real-world outcome, stem from names a cause, and follow from names an entailment.

Be a logical consequence of something already established.

  1. Nothing in the report follows from the data they actually collected.
  2. It follows from the first assumption that the payments cannot both be optional.
  3. The conclusion follows from the premises only if the sample was random.
  4. Two corollaries follow from the theorem, and neither is obvious.
  5. Does it follow from his silence that he agreed? It does not.
  6. Everything else in the argument follows from that one definition.
See also
result in, result from · stem from · deduce from · infer from · from

foreclose on#

Pronunciation
/fɔrˈkloʊz ɑn/ · for-KLOHZ on · weak /fɚˈkloʊz ən/
Русский
обращать взыскание на (заложенное имущество); исключать (возможность)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral — banking, real estate, and the courts
Complement
noun phrase naming the property, the loan, or the borrower: foreclose on the house, on the mortgage, on the family. No gerund (✗ foreclose on missing three payments), no infinitive, and no clause. The verb also appears with no object at all (the bank moved to foreclose).
Pattern
foreclose on [a property, a mortgage, a borrower] · foreclose — intransitive · foreclose [an option, a possibility, an argument] — transitive, no preposition · foreclosure on [a home] — the noun keeps on
Contrast
The financial sense takes on; the abstract sense usually takes nothing. A lender forecloses on a house, but a ruling forecloses an argument — direct object, no preposition — and the statute forecloses that reading is the standard legal phrasing. (Foreclose on the possibility does occur, and nobody will misunderstand it, but the bare transitive is cleaner.) Note the sequence in ordinary use: a borrower defaults on the loan, the bank forecloses on the property, and the house is then disposed of at auction.

Take back mortgaged property after the borrower stops paying — or, transitively, rule something out.

  1. The bank foreclosed on the property after eleven months of missed payments.
  2. Three houses on that block were foreclosed on in the same year.
  3. They refinanced twice to keep the credit union from foreclosing on them.
  4. Nobody wants to foreclose on a family with two kids in the local school.
  5. The decision forecloses any argument that the deadline was advisory.
  6. Filing late does not foreclose the claim entirely.
See also
default on · dispose of · petition for · sue for · on

forget about#

Pronunciation
/fɚˈɡɛt əˌbaʊt/ · fer-GET uh-bowt · weak /fɚˈɡɛt əbət/
Русский
забывать о — не вспомнить или намеренно выбросить из головы
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
about takes a noun phrase, a gerund, or a wh-clause — never a to-infinitive after the preposition. Without the preposition, forget takes three different complements that mean three different things: a direct object, a to-infinitive (forget to lock it — you failed to do it), and a gerund (forget locking it — you did it but don't remember).
Pattern
forget about [something / verb-ing] · forget [something] · forget to [verb] · forget [verb-ing] · forget that [clause] · Forget it.
Contrast
This is the classic three-way split, and the tenses point in opposite directions. I forgot to call = the call never happened. I forgot calling = the call happened and the memory didn't. I forgot about the call = it slipped my mind, usually before the fact. The same split governs remember, stop, regret, and try: stop to smoke (pause in order to) against stop smoking (quit). Forget about it also works as a dismissal — drop the subject — and Forget it as a refusal.

Fail to remember, or deliberately put something out of mind.

  1. I completely forgot about the parent-teacher conference.
  2. Don't forget to take the trash out tonight.
  3. I'll never forget seeing that field for the first time.
  4. Forget about getting a table on a Saturday without a reservation.
  5. She forgot that the office closes at three on Fridays.
  6. Forget it — I'll do it myself.
See also
remind of · think about · worry about · care about

forgive for#

Pronunciation
/fɚˈɡɪv fɔr/ · fer-GIV for · weak /fɚˈɡɪv fɚ/
Русский
прощать за — перестать держать обиду (человек без предлога: forgive me for)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (forgive her for missing it) — never a to-infinitive (✗ forgive her for to miss it). The person forgiven is a direct object: ✗ forgive to her. An older two-object frame survives in fixed religious language: forgive us our trespasses.
Pattern
forgive [someone] for [something / verb-ing] · forgive [someone] · forgive [a debt, a loan] · forgive [someone] [something] — formal, fixed · You could be forgiven for [verb-ing]
Contrast
Like excuse and thank, forgive takes the person directly and the offense with for — ✗ forgive to someone and ✗ forgive about something are both wrong. Note the specialized financial sense: forgive a loan means cancel the obligation, and takes no preposition at all. And note the useful hedge you could be forgiven for thinking — always with a gerund — which is how American writers concede a reasonable misreading.

Stop holding something against someone.

  1. She never quite forgave him for missing the funeral.
  2. Forgive me for asking, but how much did that cost?
  3. He was forgiven for the first two lapses and fired for the third.
  4. You could be forgiven for thinking the building was abandoned.
  5. The lender agreed to forgive half the balance.
  6. I forgive you, but I'm not forgetting it.
See also
excuse for · apologize to · blame for · punish for · thank for

get along with#

Pronunciation
/ɡɛt əˈlɔŋ wɪθ/ · get uh-LAWNG with · weak /ɡɪt əˈlɔŋ wəθ/
Русский
ладить с — уживаться с человеком; разг.
Type
verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional)
Register
informal to neutral — the ordinary American way to talk about relationships at work and at home
Complement
noun phrase naming a person or group — get along with your sister, with the new manager, with everybody. No gerund (✗ get along with working nights) and no clause; if you want a manner, use an adverb instead: they get along fine. The string is fixed and the object never moves: ✗ get her along with.
Pattern
get along with [a person, a group] · get along — intransitive (they get along) · get along without [something] — manage · get along — of age or time (getting along in years)
Contrast
Get along with is about the relationship; go along with is about agreeing. You can get along with a colleague you never go along with. The British get on with someone means the same as get along with, but in American English get on with it means only stop stalling. And note the third member of the family, get along without, which means manage in the absence of something: we got along without a car for a year.

Have an easy enough relationship with somebody.

  1. She gets along with everyone on the crew except the foreman.
  2. The two of them have never gotten along, and the trip did not help.
  3. He gets along with his ex better now than when they were married.
  4. New hires who get along with the schedulers get the shifts they want.
  5. Do you think you could get along with someone that particular for six months?
  6. They got along without air conditioning for thirty summers.
See also
go along with · hang out with · put up with · deal with, deal in · with

get around to#

Pronunciation
/ˈɡɛt əˈraʊnd tu/ · get uh-ROWND too · weak /ˈɡɛt əˈraʊnd tə/
Русский
наконец добраться до чего-то — сделать отложенное; после to герундий
Type
verb + adverb + preposition
Register
informal to neutral; everyday American speech and email
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (never got around to calling her) — to is a preposition, the last word of a fixed three-part unit, so a verb after it must be -ing: ✗ got around to call her, ✗ get around to fix it. Never a clause. The three words are fixed in order and the object always follows all of them — get around to it, never ✗ get it around to. It appears overwhelmingly in the negative or with finally: never got around to it, still haven't gotten around to it, finally got around to it.
Pattern
get around to [noun phrase] · get around to [verb-ing] · never get around to [verb-ing] · finally get around to [verb-ing] · get round to — British
Contrast
The same gerund rule as look forward to, and the same test proves it: get around to the taxes is fine, so to is a preposition, so it is get around to doing the taxes, never ✗ to do the taxes. The phrase carries a built-in apology — it means you got there eventually, after putting it off — which is why I finally got around to it is a confession and I'll get around to it is a deflection. British English says get round to, with the same grammar. Do not confuse it with the unrelated get around meaning circumvent (get around the rule) or travel (she gets around), neither of which takes to.

Finally do something you have been putting off.

  1. I never got around to framing any of those photographs.
  2. She finally got around to replacing the smoke detectors.
  3. We'll get around to the basement one of these years.
  4. He hasn't gotten around to answering my email from March.
  5. Nobody ever gets around to reading the terms of service.
  6. If I get around to it before Friday, I'll send you the draft.
See also
look forward to · get used to · resort to · plan on · forget about · to

get away with#

Pronunciation
/ɡɛt əˈweɪ wɪθ/ · get uh-WAY with · weak /ɡɪt əˈweɪ wəθ/
Русский
сойти с рук — сделать дурное и остаться безнаказанным; разг.
Type
verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional)
Register
informal to neutral
Complement
noun phrase (get away with murder, with it, with a warning) or gerund (got away with lying about his hours) — never a to-infinitive (✗ got away with to lie) and never a clause. The three words are fixed: ✗ get it away with, though get away with it is of course correct because it follows the preposition.
Pattern
get away with [something, verb-ing] · get away with it · get away from [a place, a person] — escape · get away — intransitive (leave, take a trip)
Contrast
Get away with is escaping the consequence; get out of is escaping the obligation. You get away with padding an expense report and get out of filing it at all. Escape from is the physical version, and get away from sits between the two. Note the fixed hyperbole get away with murder, and note that the subject is always the person who did the thing — the verb cannot be turned around: ✗ the fraud got away with him.

Do something wrong or risky and not be caught or punished for it.

  1. He got away with it for six years because nobody reconciled the accounts.
  2. You can get away with one late paper in that class, not two.
  3. She got away with charging twice what anyone else did.
  4. Kids that age get away with murder when the substitute is new.
  5. Nobody gets away with skipping the safety walkthrough on that site.
  6. They got away with a warning and a small fine.
See also
escape from · put up with · face up to, back out of, drop out of, walk out on · punish for · with

get rid of#

Pronunciation
/ɡɛt ˈrɪd ʌv/ · get RID uv · weak /ɡɪt ˈrɪd əv/
Русский
избавляться от — убирать, выбрасывать, освобождаться от чего-то
Type
verb + adjective + preposition
Register
informal to neutral — the everyday American twin of dispose of
Complement
noun phrase naming a thing, a person, or a problem. No gerund (✗ get rid of smoking — English says quit smoking), no infinitive, no clause. Nothing may be inserted into the string: ✗ get it rid of, ✗ get rid the couch of; the object always follows of.
Pattern
get rid of [something, someone] · be rid of [something] · rid [a place] of [something] — transitive, formal · good riddance — the fixed noun
Contrast
Get rid of is the plain version of dispose of, and it covers throwing out, selling, firing, and curing: get rid of the couch, of the car, of the manager, of a cough. The formal relatives are rid X of Y (rid the building of asbestos) and be rid of (glad to be rid of him). Note that rid here is not a movable particle but an adjective-like word inside a fixed frame, which is why the object cannot climb over it. For a habit, English switches verbs: quit smoking, give up smoking, never ✗ get rid of smoking.

Remove or discard something, or free yourself of it.

  1. We finally got rid of the couch that had been in the garage since 2009.
  2. He can't get rid of this cough.
  3. The new director got rid of three vice presidents in a month.
  4. If you want to get rid of the smell, you have to pull up the pad underneath.
  5. I'd get rid of the second paragraph entirely.
  6. They were glad to be rid of the whole arrangement.
See also
dispose of · divest of · deprive of · deal with, deal in · of

get used to, get accustomed to#

Pronunciation
/ˈɡɛt ˈjust tu/ · get YOOST too · weak /ˈɡɛt ˈjust tə/
Русский
привыкать к — после to герундий: get used to working, не ✗ to work
Type
verb + adjective + preposition
Register
get used to is everyday; get accustomed to is formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (got used to driving on the left) — to is a preposition, so ✗ get used to drive on the left is an error, and so is ✗ I'm getting used to work nights. Never a clause. Note the pronunciation and spelling of used here: /just/ with an s, not /juzd/, and the d is silent in used to, which is why learners write ✗ use to.
Pattern
get used to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · be used to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · get accustomed to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · grow accustomed to [verb-ing] · used to [verb — a different pattern entirely]
Contrast
Two different used tos share a spelling and nothing else, and this is the distinction to nail down first. I used to smoke is a past habit — to is the infinitive marker, the verb is bare, and there is no be or get in front of it. I'm used to the smoke is tolerating a smoky room and I'm used to smoking means the habit no longer bothers me — in both of those to is a preposition, so the verb has to be -ing. The noun test separates them instantly: I'm used to the noise works, so that to is a preposition; ✗ I used to the noise does not, so that one is not. Get marks the process and be marks the finished state: it took a month to get used to it, and now I'm used to it. Accustomed is the formal twin with identical grammar, and the adjective forms are catalogued in the companion adjective file.

Grow familiar with something so that it stops bothering you.

  1. It took her most of a winter to get used to the cold.
  2. You never really get used to being on call.
  3. Don't get used to the corner office; the lease is up in March.
  4. He's getting accustomed to the new scheduling software, slowly.
  5. I'm still not used to seeing his name on the door.
  6. The horses have gotten used to the trains going by.
See also
look forward to · get around to · adapt to · cope with · object to · accustomed to

go along with#

Pronunciation
/ɡoʊ əˈlɔŋ wɪθ/ · goh uh-LAWNG with · weak /ɡoʊ əˈlɔŋ wəθ/
Русский
соглашаться с, идти на поводу — принимать, не возражая
Type
verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional)
Register
informal to neutral
Complement
noun phrase naming a plan, a decision, a story, or the person proposing it, or a gerund (went along with selling the house). No to-infinitive (✗ go along with to sell the house) and no clause. Fixed string, object after the preposition: ✗ go it along with.
Pattern
go along with [a plan, a decision, a person] · go along with [verb-ing] · go with [an option] — choose · go along — intransitive (I'll go along)
Contrast
Go along with is consent without enthusiasm. Agree with says you think it is right, agree to says you accept it, and go along with says you are not going to fight it — which is why it so often appears with just: I just went along with it. Keep it clear of get along with, which is about relationships, and of go with, which means pick: we went with the cheaper bid. Its formal cousins are acquiesce in and defer to, and its opposite here is object to.

Agree to something, or accept it, without pushing back.

  1. The rest of the committee went along with the chair's recommendation.
  2. He went along with the story until the dates stopped matching.
  3. She went along with selling the house, but she never wanted to.
  4. If the county goes along with the plan, construction starts in April.
  5. I'm not going to go along with something I think is unsafe.
  6. Two holdouts refused to go along.
See also
get along with · agree with, agree to, agree on · comply with · defer to · object to

hang out with#

Pronunciation
/ˈhæŋ aʊt wɪθ/ · HANG out with · weak /ˈhæŋ aʊt wəθ/
Русский
проводить время с, тусоваться с — разг.
Type
verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional)
Register
informal — conversational American English, out of place in a report or a cover letter
Complement
noun phrase naming a person or group. No gerund (✗ hang out with playing cards) and no clause; what you do together goes in a separate phrase: hang out with them playing cards. The string is fixed: ✗ hang him out with.
Pattern
hang out with [people] · hang out — intransitive (we just hung out) · hang out at / in [a place] · hang around with [people] — the same idea, slightly more idle
Contrast
Two verbs, one spelling. Hang out the laundry is a separable particle verb — you can say hang the laundry out — and it belongs with the phrasal verbs; hang out with somebody is fixed and cannot be split. The neutral-register equivalents are spend time with and see (I saw a lot of her that year), and socialize with is the word a résumé would use. In American English hang out with implies unstructured time; meet with implies an appointment.

Spend unstructured time with somebody.

  1. He hangs out with the same four people he met in high school.
  2. Nobody hangs out with the interns because they leave at five.
  3. She hung out with her cousins all summer at the lake.
  4. They hang out at the taqueria on Fourth most nights.
  5. I don't hang out with anyone from work anymore.
  6. My daughter would rather hang out with the dog than with the family.
