hear about, hear from, hear of#

Pronunciation
/ˈhɪr əˌbaʊt/ · HEER uh-bowt · weak /ˈhɪr əbət/
Русский
слышать — from получить весть от кого-то, about узнать новости о, of знать о существовании
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
about takes a noun phrase, a gerund, or a wh-clause. from takes a person or organization only. of takes a noun phrase or a gerund and is largely restricted to existence and to the negative. None takes a to-infinitive. Bare hear is transitive (hear the alarm) and also takes someone do or someone doing: I heard her leave, I heard her singing. A clause takes no preposition: I heard that they sold it.
Pattern
hear about [news, details] · hear from [a person — receive word] · hear of [existence] · never hear of [something] · won't hear of [verb-ing] · hear [something] · hear that [clause] · hear [someone] do / doing
Contrast
Three prepositions, three jobs, and one of the highest-value distinctions in the language. Hear from Marcus means Marcus contacted you. Hear about Marcus means somebody told you news of him. Hear of Marcus means you know he exists — which is why I've never heard of her is a statement about your ignorance and I've never heard from her is a complaint. The fixed idiom won't hear of it means flatly refuse, and takes a gerund: she wouldn't hear of our paying. Note the business-letter formula I look forward to hearing from you — from, and the gerund.

Receive news, learn that something exists, or get word from someone.

PatternWhat it meansExample
hear from [person]receive a call, letter, or message from themI heard from Dad this morning.
hear about [thing]get the news or the detailsDid you hear about the layoffs?
hear of [thing]know it existsI've never heard of that town.
won't hear of [verb-ing]flatly refuse to allowHe wouldn't hear of us leaving.
hear [thing]perceive with your ears — no prepositionI heard a door slam.
hear that [clause]learn the fact — no prepositionI hear that she's retiring.
  1. Did you hear about the fire on Kingsbury?
  2. I haven't heard from my brother since Thanksgiving.
  3. I've never heard of the band, but the tickets were free.
  4. We heard about the merger from a reporter before anyone told the staff.
  5. You'll hear from our attorney.
  6. She wouldn't hear of our paying for the room.
  7. Let me know as soon as you hear from them.
See also
learn about · listen to · ask about · talk about

hide from#

Pronunciation
/ˈhaɪd frʌm/ · HIDE from · weak /ˈhaɪd frəm/
Русский
прятать(ся) от — держать себя или вещь вне поля зрения
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause (✗ hide from being seen; say hide so nobody sees you). The verb works both intransitively (hide from the police) and transitively with two arguments (hide the letter from his wife). Note the irregular forms: hide, hid, hidden.
Pattern
hide from [someone] · hide [something] from [someone] · hide [somewhere] · be hidden from [view, sight] · hide out · nothing to hide
Contrast
Hide from is the direction of concealment and cannot be reversed: He hid the diagnosis from his kids is the only order that works — ✗ hid his kids from the diagnosis says something else entirely. Compare keep something from someone, which is the everyday synonym and takes the same shape, and conceal something from someone, the formal one. Note hidden from view, a fixed phrase, and the idiomatic I have nothing to hide, which takes no preposition at all.

Keep yourself or something out of sight.

  1. The cat hides from anyone who isn't me.
  2. He hid the diagnosis from his kids for almost a year.
  3. You can't hide from a subpoena forever.
  4. The entrance is hidden from the road by a hedge.
  5. She hid the good chocolate from her roommates.
  6. Two of them hid from the storm under a picnic shelter.
See also

hinge on#

Pronunciation
/ˈhɪndʒ ɑn/ · HINJ on · weak /ˈhɪndʒ ən/
Русский
целиком зависеть от — определяться одним решающим обстоятельством
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral — journalism, argument, and courtroom summary
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (hinges on getting the votes), or a wh-clause (hinges on whether the Senate acts, on how the judge reads the statute) — never a to-infinitive (✗ hinges on to get the votes) and never a bare that-clause: ✗ hinges on that the Senate acts. The subject is abstract — a case, an outcome, a decision, the whole thing — because people do not hinge on anything: ✗ she hinges on her lawyer.
Variants
Hinge upon is the same pattern one register up, at home in a brief or an editorial.
Pattern
[an outcome] hinge on / upon [a fact, a condition] · hinge on [verb-ing] · hinge on whether / how / what [clause] · everything hinges on [X] — the commonest frame
Contrast
Hinge on is narrower than depend on in two ways. Its subject must be a thing, not a person — the case hinges on the timeline, but she depends on her sister — and it names a single pivot rather than a general reliance, which is why everything hinges on one witness sounds right and ✗ everything hinges on my family does not. Turn on is its close synonym in legal writing (the appeal turns on a question of standing), and rest on is the static version: a claim rests on an assumption without pivoting on it.

Depend entirely on one deciding fact or condition.

  1. The whole case hinges on what the security camera recorded.
  2. Their forecast hinges on interest rates holding through the spring.
  3. Everything hinges on getting the permit before the season closes.
  4. The appeal hinges on whether the notice was timely.
  5. His argument hinges upon a definition the statute never gives.
  6. Nothing much hinges on which of the two spellings you use.
See also
depend on · rely on · count on · result in, result from · on

hope for#

Pronunciation
/ˈhoʊp fɔr/ · HOHP for · weak /ˈhoʊp fɚ/
Русский
надеяться на — хотеть, чтобы что-то случилось, и считать это возможным
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for takes a noun phrase only — no gerund and no to-infinitive after the preposition (✗ hope for to win, ✗ hope for winning). To put a verb after hope, drop the preposition and use the infinitive: hope to win. A clause also takes no preposition: hope that it clears up — and note that the present tense is normal there, not will: I hope it clears up tomorrow.
Pattern
hope for [a noun phrase] · hope to [verb] · hope that [clause] · I hope so / I hope not · hoping for [the best] · hope against hope
Contrast
Hope for winning is not English. The preposition takes a noun and only a noun — hope for rain, hope for a quick decision — and the moment you want a verb you must switch frames to hope to. This is the opposite of look forward to, where the preposition demands the -ing form. The fixed pair hope for the best and hope against hope are frozen. Note also that after hope the future is usually expressed with a present tense: I hope the flight lands on time.

Want something to happen and think it might.

  1. They're hoping for a settlement before the trial date.
  2. We hoped for rain all August and got two inches in September.
  3. She hopes to finish the draft before the semester starts.
  4. Everyone was hoping for a shorter meeting.
  5. Hope for the best, plan for the other thing.
  6. I hope the connection holds.
See also
wish for · long for · look forward to · dream of · plan on

impose on, impose upon#

Pronunciation
/ɪmˈpoʊz ɑn/ · im-POHZ on · weak /ɪmˈpoʊz ən/
Русский
налагать (налог, правило) на кого-то; злоупотреблять чьей-то любезностью
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — taxes, sanctions, deadlines, and polite apology
Complement
in the transitive frame the thing imposed is the direct object and on takes the person or body that has to bear it: impose a curfew on the neighborhood. In the intransitive frame on takes a person and the verb means take unfair advantage of them: I don't want to impose on you. A gerund after on is rare, and there is no infinitive and no clause: ✗ impose on to stay another night.
Variants
Impose upon is the formal twin, standard in legal and academic prose (the duty imposed upon the trustee) and slightly old-fashioned in the apology sense (I fear we imposed upon your hospitality).
Pattern
impose [a tax, a rule, a deadline, a fine] on / upon [someone] · impose on / upon [someone] — trespass on their good nature · be imposed on [someone] · impose oneself on [a group] · an imposition on [someone] — the noun keeps on
Contrast
The two senses share a preposition and nothing else. The state imposed a fee on filers is authority; I hate to impose is etiquette, and in that second sense the verb has no object of its own at all. Against the neighbors: inflict on is worse (inflict a speech on the guests), foist on is sneakier (foist the bill on his brother), and encroach on is gradual rather than imposed at once. Note that with people the passive is fine — conditions were imposed on the defendant — but the thing imposed can never take to: ✗ imposed a fine to the company.

Place a burden, rule, or cost on somebody — or take advantage of their good nature.

  1. Congress imposed new reporting requirements on small lenders.
  2. The court imposed a six-month deadline on both parties.
  3. I don't want to impose on you two any longer than one night.
  4. Sanctions were imposed on the bank within a week of the ruling.
  5. She imposes her taste in music on everyone in the car.
  6. The tariff imposed upon imported steel raised the cost of the whole project.
See also
encroach on, encroach upon · impute to · devolve on, devolve upon · charge with, charge for · on

impute to#

Pronunciation
/ɪmˈpjut tu/ · im-PYOOT too · weak /ɪmˈpjut tə/
Русский
вменять кому-то (мотив, вину, стоимость) — приписывать незаявленное; юр./книжн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — law, accounting, and theology. An American meets it as a participle long before he meets the verb: imputed income on a tax return, imputed knowledge in an agency case, imputed negligence in a jury instruction.
Complement
the thing imputed is the direct object — a motive, a statement, a value, a fault — and to takes the person or entity it is charged to. A gerund after to is rare (imputed to having signed the form), and there is no infinitive (✗ impute the delay to be careless) and no clause. The object cannot be dropped: ✗ he imputed to the agency.
Pattern
impute [a motive, a fault, a value] to [someone] · be imputed to [someone] · imputed income / knowledge / negligence — the participle · imputation of [bad faith] — the noun takes of
Contrast
Impute to assigns something discreditable or artificial; attribute to is neutral. You attribute a quotation to Twain and impute a motive to an opponent — and ascribe to sits between them, more literary than either. Credit with is the flattering member of the family (credited her with the idea), and blame on the blunt one. Note that the law uses impute for something nobody actually did or knew: the agent's knowledge is imputed to the principal means the principal is treated as knowing it.

Charge somebody with a motive, a fault, or a value they did not actually declare.

  1. Counsel imputed bad faith to the other side without producing a document.
  2. The knowledge of the broker is imputed to the firm that hired him.
  3. The IRS imputes interest to below-market family loans.
  4. Do not impute to carelessness what a simple software bug explains.
  5. Critics imputed motives to her that the record does not support.
  6. Negligence was imputed to the parent company through the subsidiary.
See also
blame for, blame on · accuse of · impose on, impose upon · refer to · to

indemnify against#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈdɛmnəfaɪ əˈɡɛnst/ · in-DEM-nuh-fye uh-GENST · weak /ɪnˈdɛmnəfaɪ əɡənst/
Русский
гарантировать возмещение убытков от — договорное обязательство; юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
legal — contract drafting and insurance. An American meets it in exactly one place: the indemnification clause of a lease, a vendor agreement, or a settlement, usually in the fixed triple indemnify, defend, and hold harmless.
Complement
the party protected is the direct object, and against takes a noun phrase naming a loss, a claim, a liability, or a suit: indemnify the landlord against claims arising from the tenant's use. A gerund is rare, and there is no infinitive and no clause: ✗ indemnify the seller against to be sued. The protected party cannot be dropped: ✗ indemnify against all claims with nobody named is drafting malpractice, not grammar.
Variants
Indemnify from is equally standard in American clauses (indemnify and hold harmless from any liability), and indemnify for points at a loss already suffered rather than one that might happen: indemnified the owner for the cost of repairs. Practice varies firm to firm; all three are read the same way.
Pattern
indemnify [a party] against / from [claims, losses, liability] · indemnify [a party] for [an amount already lost] · indemnify, defend, and hold [a party] harmless against [X] · indemnity against [loss] — the noun keeps the preposition
Contrast
Indemnify against is a promise between parties; insure against is a policy you buy. A subcontractor indemnifies the general contractor against third-party claims, while the general contractor separately insures against fire. Both differ from compensate for, which is payment after the fact and takes for, and from protect from, which is the everyday word. Note that the object is the person protected, never the risk: ✗ indemnify the losses against the tenant.

Promise in a contract to cover somebody else's losses if a specified risk lands.

  1. The vendor agrees to indemnify the university against any third-party infringement claim.
  2. Directors are routinely indemnified against liability for good-faith decisions.
  3. The clause indemnifies the landlord against everything short of his own negligence.
  4. We would not sign until the contractor agreed to indemnify us from mechanics' liens.
  5. Nothing in the policy indemnifies the company against fines imposed by a regulator.
  6. She was indemnified for the legal fees but not for the lost year of income.
See also
insure against · protect from, protect against · relieve of · liable for, liable to · against

indict for#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈdaɪt fɔr/ · in-DITE for · weak /ɪnˈdaɪt fɚ/ — the c is silent
Русский
предъявлять обвинение в — через большое жюри; юр., США
Type
verb + preposition
Register
legal and news — grand juries, U.S. Attorneys, and the front page
Complement
the person is the direct object and for takes a noun phrase naming the offense, or a gerund naming the conduct: indicted for lying to investigators. Never a to-infinitive (✗ indicted for to lie) and never a clause: ✗ indicted for that he lied. The frame indict on takes counts or charges instead — indicted on three counts of wire fraud — and is at least as common in reporting.
Pattern
indict [someone] for [an offense, verb-ing] · indict [someone] on [counts, charges] · be indicted for / on [something] · return an indictment against [someone] · an indictment of [a policy] — the figurative noun takes of
Contrast
Four verbs run the same case and none of them shares a preposition. A prosecutor charges someone with a crime, a grand jury indicts him for it (or on counts of it), a jury convicts him of it, and a judge sentences him to something. Accuse of is the everyday word for the same act, and arraign on is the step in between. Note the figurative noun, which flips the preposition entirely: the report is an indictment of the whole program, never ✗ an indictment for.

Formally charge somebody with a crime by grand jury.

  1. A federal grand jury indicted him for mail fraud in November.
  2. Two officers were indicted for falsifying the incident report.
  3. She was indicted on seven counts, four of them dismissed before trial.
  4. The prosecutor declined to indict anyone for the leak.
  5. Being indicted for a crime is not the same as being convicted of one.
  6. The grand jury refused to indict, and the case ended there.
See also
charge with, charge for · accuse of · punish for · suspect of · for

infer from#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈfɚ frʌm/ · in-FUR from · weak /ɪnˈfɚ frəm/
Русский
делать вывод из — заключать по имеющимся данным
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — argument, science, and careful writing
Complement
from takes a noun phrase naming the evidence, or a gerund (inferred from watching the two of them) — never a to-infinitive (✗ inferred from to watch them). What is inferred is the direct object and may be a that-clause: inferred that the deal was off. The preposition never introduces the conclusion: ✗ infer from that the deal was off is a common slip for infer from her tone that the deal was off.
Pattern
infer [something] from [evidence] · infer from [evidence] that [clause] · [something] may be inferred from [evidence] · an inference from [the record] — the noun keeps from
Contrast
The usage everyone argues about: imply and infer are opposite ends of one act. The speaker implies; the listener infers. ✗ Are you inferring that I lied? means the opposite of what the speaker intends — say implying. Within the reasoning family, deduce from claims certainty, infer from claims likelihood, and extrapolate from claims a pattern will continue. All three take from; none takes of or out of: ✗ infer of his tone.

Draw a probable conclusion from evidence.

  1. From the tone of the email she inferred that the offer had been withdrawn.
  2. You can infer a great deal from what a company does not disclose.
  3. Nothing about intent can be inferred from a single missed payment.
  4. The jury was told not to infer guilt from the defendant's silence.
  5. He inferred from watching two shifts that the schedule was written by seniority.
  6. Readers are left to infer the ending from three lines of dialogue.
See also
deduce from · extrapolate from · derive from · follow from · from

infringe on, infringe upon#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈfrɪndʒ ɑn/ · in-FRINJ on · weak /ɪnˈfrɪndʒ ən/
Русский
нарушать, посягать на — на право, свободу или охраняемую собственность
Type
verb + preposition
Register
legal to neutral — constitutional argument, intellectual property, and op-eds
Complement
noun phrase naming a right, a freedom, or a protected interest. A gerund is unusual (infringing on being able to speak freely is possible but clumsy), and there is no infinitive and no clause: ✗ infringe on to speak freely.
Variants
Infringe upon is the formal twin and is common in judicial opinions. The important variant is the bare transitive: in intellectual-property law you infringe a patent, infringe a copyright, infringe the mark — with no preposition at all, and that is the professional usage.
Pattern
infringe on / upon [a right, a freedom, someone's autonomy] · infringe [a patent, a copyright, a trademark] — no preposition · an infringement of [copyright] · an infringement on [rights]
Contrast
Rights are infringed on; patents are infringed. The ordinance infringes on the right to assemble and the knockoff infringes the design patent are both standard, and swapping them marks a writer as an outsider to the field. Against the neighbors: encroach on is gradual and often physical, violate is blunt and transitive (violate the statute), impinge on is the most abstract, and interfere with is the broadest of all. The noun splits too — infringement of copyright but an infringement on civil liberties.

Intrude on a right, a freedom, or protected property.

  1. The plaintiffs say the curfew infringes on rights the state constitution guarantees.
  2. Requiring a permit for a two-person picket infringes upon speech, the court held.
  3. He felt the new metrics infringed on how he ran his own department.
  4. The company was found to have infringed three of the four patents.
  5. Nothing in the lease infringes on the tenant's right to quiet enjoyment.
  6. Streaming the whole film infringes the copyright whether or not you charge for it.
See also
encroach on, encroach upon · interfere with, interfere in · impose on, impose upon · comply with · on

inhere in#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈhɪr ɪn/ · in-HEER in · weak /ɪnˈhɪr ən/
Русский
быть неотъемлемо присущим чему-то — книжн./филос.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal and technical — philosophy, jurisprudence, and constitutional opinions (the power inheres in the office). It is rare enough in speech that most Americans know only its adjective, inherent in.
Complement
noun phrase naming the thing the quality belongs to — an office, a relationship, a system, a role. No gerund, no infinitive, no clause, and no passive: ✗ is inhered in. The subject is the quality, right, or power, never the possessor: ✗ the court inheres in that power.
Pattern
[a right, a power, a quality] inhere in [an office, a thing, a relationship] · inherent in [something] — the far commoner adjective · inhere — no other frame; the verb needs in
Contrast
Prefer the adjective. The risk inherent in the procedure is ordinary English; the risk inheres in the procedure is a philosopher's sentence. Against the neighbors: consist in names what something essentially is (happiness consists in useful work), reside in is where a power lives by design (sovereignty resides in the people), and vest in is the legal act of putting it there (the estate vests in the heir). Inhere in claims the quality could not be separated from the thing at all.

Belong to something as an inseparable part of what it is.

  1. Certain powers inhere in the office and do not depend on the statute.
  2. The dignity that inheres in the individual is not conferred by government.
  3. Whatever value the collection has inheres in keeping it together.
  4. Risk inheres in any procedure performed under general anesthesia.
  5. The authority to punish contempt inheres in the court itself.
  6. He argued that the flaw inheres in the design rather than the execution.
See also
devolve on, devolve upon · belong to · consist of · inherent in · in

insist on#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈsɪst ɑn/ · in-SIST on · weak /ɪnˈsɪst ən/
Русский
настаивать на — твёрдо требовать или упорно утверждать; после on герундий
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (insisted on paying), or possessive plus gerund (insisted on my paying) — never a to-infinitive (✗ insist to pay, ✗ insist on to pay). Before a clause the preposition drops, and American English uses the bare subjunctive: insisted that she be told, not that she is told.
Pattern
insist on [noun phrase] · insist on [verb-ing] · insist on [someone's] -ing · insist that [subject] [bare verb] · insist (alone)
Contrast
Insist to do is a fixed error — the verb has no infinitive frame at all. The two live patterns are insist on doing and insist that X do, and the second one keeps the American mandative subjunctive: He insisted that the door be locked, She insists that he take the earlier flight — bare verb, no -s, no should. (British English often inserts should.) Note the second sense, maintain in the face of doubt: she insisted that she'd sent it, where the clause is a plain indicative, not a subjunctive.

Demand something firmly, or maintain something in the face of doubt.

  1. He insisted on paying for everyone, which nobody had asked him to do.
  2. The landlord insisted on a second deposit.
  3. She insisted that the contract be reviewed before anyone signed.
  4. They insisted on our staying for dinner.
  5. He insisted he had mailed it, and the postmark said otherwise.
  6. The union insisted on binding arbitration.
See also
object to · agree to · decide on · comply with · on

insure against#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈʃʊr əˈɡɛnst/ · in-SHOOR uh-GENST · weak /ɪnˈʃʊr əɡənst/
Русский
страховать от — покупать полис на случай риска (не путать с ensure, assure)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral — insurance, banking, and risk talk
Complement
against takes a noun phrase naming a risk or peril — fire, flood, theft, loss, liability — or a gerund (insured against being sued by a former client). No to-infinitive (✗ insure against to be sued) and no clause. The party or property covered is the direct object: insure the building against flood.
Pattern
insure [property, a person] against [a risk] · be insured against [a risk] · insure [something] for [an amount] · insure [something] with [a carrier] · insurance against [loss] — the noun keeps against
Contrast
Three verbs Americans mix daily. Insure is about policies, ensure is about making certain (ensure that the door locks), and assure is about telling a person something confidently (assured her it was fine) — so ✗ insure that the door locks is the error, and it is everywhere. Within the risk family: insure against buys coverage, indemnify against is a contractual promise between two parties with no policy involved, and hedge against offsets a risk with an opposite position (hedge against inflation). Note the amount takes for, not against: insured for $400,000 against fire.

Buy or provide coverage that pays out if a particular risk occurs.

  1. The house is insured against fire but not against flood.
  2. Small clinics can rarely afford to insure against a judgment that size.
  3. The policy insures the fleet against theft and collision.
  4. Growers insure against a late frost the way other people buy lottery tickets in reverse.
  5. She was insured against being sued for work done before the merger.
  6. Nothing insures against bad luck entirely.
See also
indemnify against · protect from, protect against · provide with, provide for · against

interfere with, interfere in#

Pronunciation
/ˌɪntɚˈfɪr wɪθ/ · in-ter-FEER with · weak /ˌɪntɚˈfɪr wəθ/
Русский
мешать чему-то (with); вмешиваться во что-то не своё (in)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (interferes with getting any real work done) — never a to-infinitive. Interfere is intransitive and never takes a bare object: ✗ interfere the signal — say interfere with the signal.
Pattern
interfere with [a process, a signal, a plan] · interfere in [an affair, a dispute, a country] · interfere (alone)
Contrast
With disrupts; in meddles. The construction interferes with the Wi-Fi is about disruption of a thing; Don't interfere in their marriage is about intruding into a situation that isn't yours. In American political writing, interfere in is the standard collocation for meddling in another country's affairs or an election, while interfere with covers evidence, a witness, a signal, or a schedule. Legal usage keeps both: tortious interference with contract, interference in the proceeding.

Disrupt something, or get involved where you have no business.

  1. The new tower interferes with reception all along the ridge.
  2. She was told, more than once, not to interfere in her sister's divorce.
  3. Nothing should interfere with the vote count.
  4. The medication can interfere with several common blood thinners.
  5. Foreign governments interfered in the election in at least three documented ways.
  6. Long meetings interfere with getting anything actually finished.
See also

introduce to#

Pronunciation
/ˌɪntrəˈdus tu/ · in-truh-DOOSS too · weak /ˌɪntrəˈdus tə/
Русский
представлять кого-то кому-то; знакомить с чем-то — предлог to обязателен
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause. The person being introduced is a direct object and the person met takes to: introduce her to my parents. ✗ introduce her with my parents is an error carried over from other languages. Into is a separate pattern, used for putting something new inside a system.
Pattern
introduce [someone] to [someone] · introduce [someone] to [a subject, a food, a habit] · introduce [something] into [a system, a market] · introduce [a bill, a topic] · introduce [oneself]
Contrast
Introduce with is wrong. The two live prepositions are to and into, and they do different work: introduce her to sailing opens a person's acquaintance with something, while introduce a species into an ecosystem physically inserts it. Note that introduce belongs to the explain/describe/mention group — the person always needs to, never a bare indirect object: ✗ introduce me your friend.

Make one person known to another, or bring something in for the first time.

  1. Let me introduce you to my colleague from the Denver office.
  2. His grandmother introduced him to opera when he was six.
  3. The bill was introduced in March and died in committee.
  4. Zebra mussels were introduced into the Great Lakes by ballast water.
  5. She introduced herself to everyone in the row before the plane took off.
  6. Nobody introduced the new hire to anyone for a week.
See also
explain to · refer to · invite to

inure to#

Pronunciation
/ɪˈnjʊr tu/ · ih-NYOOR too · weak /ɪˈnjʊr tə/
Русский
доставаться в пользу кого-то; (в пассиве) быть закалённым к чему-то; юр./книжн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal and legal, in two separate lives. In a trust, a policy, or a contract you meet the fixed phrase inure to the benefit of; in essays and obituaries you meet the participle inured to meaning hardened by exposure. Neither is conversational.
Complement
in the legal sense, to takes a noun phrase naming the party who gets the benefit — almost always inside to the benefit of [someone]. In the participial sense, be inured to takes a noun phrase or a gerund: inured to being ignored, inured to the cold. Never a to-infinitive in either sense: ✗ inured to be ignored, and that trap is the reason the entry is here.
Variants
The spelling enure survives in older and British legal drafting and means exactly the same thing; American filings use inure.
Pattern
[a benefit, a right] inure to the benefit of [a party] · [something] inure to [someone's advantage] · be / become inured to [hardship, verb-ing] · this Agreement shall inure to the benefit of and be binding upon the parties — the boilerplate
Contrast
Two senses, one spelling, and neither is the everyday word. The proceeds inure to the beneficiary means they accrue to her — the plain verbs are go to and accrue to. He is inured to criticism means he has been hardened to it — the plain phrases are used to and hardened to. The second is where the gerund rule bites: like get used to and accustomed to, inured to is followed by -ing, never by an infinitive. Do not confuse either with in your interest or with ensure.

Accrue to somebody's benefit — or, in the passive, be hardened to something by long exposure.

  1. This warranty shall inure to the benefit of any subsequent owner.
  2. Any savings from the settlement inure to the members of the class.
  3. The rights granted here inure to the benefit of the licensee's successors.
  4. Twelve years on the night desk had inured her to bad news at three in the morning.
  5. He is thoroughly inured to being told the idea will never work.
  6. Nurses in that unit become inured to the alarms within a month.
See also
get used to, get accustomed to · adapt to · belong to · accustomed to · to

invest in#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈvɛst ɪn/ · in-VEST in · weak /ɪnˈvɛst ən/
Русский
вкладывать(ся) во что-то — деньги, время или силы в расчёте на отдачу
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (invest in retraining workers) — never a to-infinitive (✗ invest in to retrain them). A formal, unrelated pattern takes with: invest someone with authority.
Pattern
invest in [a company, a fund, a thing worth having] · invest [an amount] in [something] · invest in [verb-ing] · invest [someone] with [authority] — formal · be invested in [an outcome]
Contrast
Invest on and ✗ invest to are errors — the preposition is fixed at in. Two senses live in the same frame: the financial one (invest in bonds) and the everyday one meaning buy something worth the money (invest in a decent mattress). The passive be invested in has drifted to mean emotionally committed — she's heavily invested in the outcome — and is now more common in that sense than the literal one. Keep it apart from formal invest someone with a power or a title, which is the ceremonial sense behind investiture.

Put money, time, or effort into something you expect to pay off.

  1. They invested in index funds and left it alone for thirty years.
  2. The district is investing in early literacy instead of testing.
  3. Honestly, invest in a good chair before you invest in a standing desk.
  4. She invested four years in a degree she never used and doesn't regret.
  5. The state has invested heavily in broadband for rural counties.
  6. He's too invested in being right to hear the argument.

invite to#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈvaɪt tu/ · in-VITE too · weak /ɪnˈvaɪt tə/
Русский
приглашать на, приглашать к — звать прийти или сделать что-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to takes a noun phrase naming an event or a place — no gerund after the preposition (✗ invited us to having dinner). Invite someone to do something is a separate frame in which to is the infinitive marker, not the preposition: invited us to speak. Both are correct; what is impossible is mixing them.
Pattern
invite [someone] to [an event / a place] · invite [someone] to [verb] · invite [someone] for [dinner, drinks] · invite [someone] in / over / along / back · be invited to [something]
Contrast
One preposition, two structures, and the difference is what follows it. Invite her to the wedding (preposition + noun) against invite her to speak at the wedding (infinitive). Note the American directional particles that need no to at all: invite them over, invite him in, invite her along. Invite for names the occasion in a looser way (invited us for coffee), and invite also has a figurative transitive use meaning provoke: that kind of memo invites trouble.

Ask someone to come, or ask them to do something.

  1. They invited half the department to the housewarming.
  2. She was invited to speak at the conference in Portland.
  3. We invited them over for the game and they stayed until midnight.
  4. He wasn't invited to the wedding and has mentioned it four times.
  5. The library invites the public to comment on the renovation plan.
  6. Invite your neighbors for dinner once and you'll know within an hour.

involve in#

Pronunciation
/ɪnˈvɑlv ɪn/ · in-VOLV in · weak /ɪnˈvɑlv ən/
Русский
вовлекать(ся) во что-то — участвовать в чём-то или включать как часть
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (involved in raising the money) — never a to-infinitive (✗ involved in to raise the money). But note the other frame: transitive involve meaning entail takes a gerund with no preposition at all — the job involves traveling, and ✗ the job involves to travel is a common and serious error.
Pattern
involve [someone] in [something] · be involved in [something / verb-ing] · [a job, a process] involve [verb-ing] — no preposition · get involved with [a person, an organization]
Contrast
Two patterns that look alike and are not. She's involved in the campaign (prepositional, about participation) against The campaign involves knocking on doors (transitive, about what it entails). And involved with is different again: involved in a project means working on it, while involved with a person means romantically entangled, and involved with an organization means affiliated. He got involved with her and He got involved in her case are not remotely the same sentence.

Take part in something, or have something as a necessary part.

  1. Three agencies were involved in the response.
  2. She's been involved in local politics since she was nineteen.
  3. The renovation involves moving a load-bearing wall.
  4. Nobody wanted to get involved in a dispute between neighbors.
  5. He was involved in raising almost half the endowment.
  6. The role involves a fair amount of weekend work.

K–M#

keep up with, catch up with, catch up on#

Pronunciation
/ˈkip ʌp wɪθ/ · KEEP up with · weak /ˈkip əp wəθ/
Русский
не отставать от; догонять кого-то; наверстывать накопившееся
Type
verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional)
Register
informal to neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund — keep up with paying the invoices, catch up on grading. Never a to-infinitive (✗ keep up with to pay them) and never a clause. The three words are welded together and the object always follows the preposition: ✗ keep the news up with, ✗ catch my email up on.
Pattern
keep up with [the news, a person, inflation, the payments] · catch up with [someone ahead of you; also, meet socially] · catch up to [someone] — the physical sense, American variant · catch up on [sleep, email, the backlog] · keep up — intransitive (I couldn't keep up)
Contrast
One family, three jobs, and the preposition does the sorting. Keep up with means stay level with something moving — the reading, the rent, a faster walker. Catch up with means close a gap that has already opened, and it has a second, friendlier life meaning meet for coffee (let's catch up) and a third, grimmer one meaning consequences arrive (the drinking caught up with him). Catch up on takes the backlog itself as its object — sleep, laundry, three weeks of email — and never a person: ✗ catch up on my sister. Note that keep up alone is intransitive, and keep something up (keep the pressure up) is a separable particle verb that belongs with the phrasal verbs, not here.

Stay level with something moving, close a gap, or work through a backlog.

  1. I can't keep up with the reading in that seminar.
  2. Wages have not kept up with rent in this county since 2015.
  3. She walked ahead and we caught up with her at the light.
  4. Let's catch up next week when the quarter closes.
  5. He spent Sunday catching up on email and laundry.
  6. The paperwork he ignored in March finally caught up with him in July.
  7. Nobody keeps up with paying two mortgages for long.
See also
face up to, back out of, drop out of, walk out on · live up to · put up with · cope with · with

laugh at#

Pronunciation
/ˈlæf æt/ · LAF at · weak /ˈlæf ət/
Русский
смеяться над — at насмешка над кем-то, laugh with смеяться вместе с ним
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (laughed at being called sir) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. Laugh is intransitive: the thing amusing you always needs a preposition. ✗ laugh the joke.
Pattern
laugh at [someone / something] · laugh about [something, usually later] · laugh with [someone] · laugh off [criticism] · be laughed at
Contrast
Laugh at mocks; laugh about shares the joke; laugh with includes the person. They laughed at him is cruel and They laughed with him is not — the same event described from two sides, and the preposition is the whole difference. Laugh about is what you do afterward, when the thing has become funny (we laugh about it now). Note that laugh at can also mean dismiss as absurd: the bank laughed at his projections. The phrasal laugh off means brush aside.

Show amusement at someone or something.

  1. Nobody laughed at the joke and he told it twice more anyway.
  2. They laughed at her accent for a year and then hired her.
  3. We'll laugh about this eventually, but not this week.
  4. He laughed at the very idea of finishing by Friday.
  5. She laughed at being mistaken for the intern.
  6. The whole table was laughing with him, not at him.
See also
smile at · stare at · boast about · complain about · at

lead to#

Pronunciation
/ˈlid tu/ · LEED too · weak /ˈlid tə/
Русский
вести к, приводить к — быть причиной или дорогой куда-то; после to герундий
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (led to closing two branches) — never a to-infinitive after the preposition (✗ led to close two branches, which reads as purpose and means something else). A to-infinitive is correct in a different frame, after a person: led her to believe, led us to expect.
Pattern
[a cause] lead to [a result] · lead to [verb-ing] · lead [someone] to [verb] · lead [someone] to [a place] · [a road, a door] lead to [somewhere]
Contrast
The same three letters do two jobs. In the audit led to closing two branches, to is the fixed preposition and the verb must be -ing; in the audit led us to close two branches, to is the infinitive marker, licensed by the person in between. Both sentences are correct; ✗ led to close two branches is not. Compare result in, which is its close synonym (resulted in two closures), and result from, which points the other way. Lead to is also literal: that hallway leads to the loading dock.

Cause something, or be the route to somewhere.

  1. Deferred repairs led to a much larger bill.
  2. One conversation led to another and they were there until two.
  3. The investigation led to three indictments.
  4. That kind of shortcut leads to rewriting the whole thing later.
  5. The trail leads to an overlook about a mile up.
  6. His silence led everyone to assume the worst.
See also
result in · contribute to · add to · stem from · result from

learn about, learn from#

Pronunciation
/ˈlɜrn əˌbaʊt/ · LURN uh-bowt · weak /ˈlɜrn əbət/
Русский
узнавать о чём-то (about); учиться у кого-то или на чём-то (from)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
about takes a noun phrase, a gerund, or a wh-clause. from takes a person, an experience, or a source. Neither takes a to-infinitive — but learn itself does, with no preposition: learn to swim. Bare learn is also transitive (learn the routine) and takes a clause (learned that the office had moved). The past is learned in American English; learnt is British.
Pattern
learn about [a subject] · learn from [a person / an experience] · learn to [verb] · learn [something] · learn that [clause] · learn of [something] — formal, means hear about
Contrast
Learn about is the subject; learn from is the source; learn to is the skill. All three can appear in one sentence: she learned about soil chemistry from her father and learned to test it herself. The formal learn of means come to hear (we learned of his death from the paper) and sounds stiff in casual speech. Note the direction trap: learn is what the student does and teach is what the instructor does — ✗ she learned me how to drive is nonstandard for taught me.

Acquire knowledge or a skill.

  1. He spent a semester learning about watershed management.
  2. She learned everything she knows about wiring from her mother.
  3. You learn from a bad manager exactly what not to do.
  4. They learned about the closure from a sign on the door.
  5. It's never too late to learn to swim.
  6. We only learned of the change the week it took effect.
See also
hear about · explain to · think about · refer to · consult with

listen to#

Pronunciation
/ˈlɪsən tu/ · LISS-un too · weak /ˈlɪsən tə/
Русский
слушать — предлог to обязателен: listen to music, не ✗ listen music (ср. hear)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, or a person plus a bare infinitive or -ing (listen to her sing, listen to the rain falling). A gerund alone is fine (listen to arguing all day), but never a to-infinitive and never a that-clause. Listen is intransitive and the object always needs to: ✗ listen music, ✗ listen me.
Pattern
listen to [something / someone] · listen to [someone] do / doing · listen for [a sound you're waiting for] · listen (alone) · listen up · Listen.
Contrast
Listen music is one of the most common errors learners make, because hear is transitive and listen is not. Hear is the passive faculty and listen to is the deliberate act: I heard the alarm (it reached me) against I listened to the whole thing (I chose to). Listen for is different again — it means wait alertly for a particular sound: listen for the buzzer. And listen to someone often means heed them: nobody listened to the engineer.

Pay deliberate attention to a sound.

  1. She listens to the same three albums on every long drive.
  2. Nobody listened to the safety officer until after the accident.
  3. Listen to him tell it and you'd think he built the thing himself.
  4. I listened to about forty minutes of hold music.
  5. Listen for the click; that's how you know it's seated.
  6. You're not listening to me.
See also
hear about · hear of · look at · stare at · concentrate on

live up to#

Pronunciation
/ˈlɪv ʌp tu/ · LIV up too · weak /ˈlɪv əp tə/
Русский
оправдывать (ожидания, репутацию) — соответствовать заданной планке
Type
verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional)
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase naming a standard, a promise, a reputation, or the hype. A gerund is possible but rare (never lived up to being called a prodigy), and there is no to-infinitive (✗ live up to be a prodigy) and no clause. Nothing may be inserted: ✗ live it up to, which is a different idiom entirely.
Pattern
live up to [expectations, a promise, a name, the billing] · fail to live up to [something] · live up to [verb-ing] — rare · live it up — an unrelated idiom (enjoy yourself)
Contrast
The word order matters more here than almost anywhere else in the file. Live up to it means meet the standard; live it up means spend the weekend enjoying yourself. Against the neighbors: live up to a promise is meeting a standard someone set, comply with a rule is obeying an instruction, and keep up with something is staying level with a moving target. Live with is the resigned one — live with the consequences — and live off or live on names what supports you.

Meet the standard that a promise, a reputation, or an expectation set.

  1. The sequel never lives up to the first film.
  2. She has spent a decade living up to a nickname she got at nineteen.
  3. The district failed to live up to the settlement it signed in 2019.
  4. Few restaurants live up to that kind of advance press.
  5. He worries about living up to what his father built.
  6. The new hire lived up to every word in her references.
See also
keep up with, catch up with, catch up on · make up for · comply with · look up to, look down on · to

long for#

Pronunciation
/ˈlɔŋ fɔr/ · LAWNG for · weak /ˈlɔŋ fɚ/
Русский
тосковать по, страстно желать — обычно о недостижимом; книжн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
literary or heightened
Complement
for takes a noun phrase — a gerund is possible but unusual (longed for being left alone). To put an action after long, drop the preposition and use the infinitive: long to go home, which is the more natural form by far. Never a clause.
Pattern
long for [a noun phrase] · long to [verb] · be longing for [something] · a longing for [something]
Contrast
Register is the whole story here. Long for is heightened, close to poetry, and an American in ordinary conversation says miss, want, or can't wait for. Its neighbors line up by intensity: wish for (mild), hope for (expectant), long for and yearn for (aching), pine for (aching and slightly comic). Note that with a verb, long to go is preferred over long for going — the opposite of look forward to, where the gerund is compulsory.

Want something badly, usually something absent or out of reach.

  1. By February everyone in the office longs for daylight.
  2. He longed for the noise of a city and moved back within a year.
  3. She longed to see the house one more time.
  4. They long for a version of the town that never really existed.
  5. You could hear him longing for an argument.
  6. After a week of rain we were all longing for one clear morning.

look at, look for, look after, look into#

Pronunciation
/ˈlʊk æt/ · LOOK at · weak /ˈlʊk ət/
Русский
смотреть на; искать; присматривать за; разбираться в — предлог решает всё
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
All four take a noun phrase. look at and look into also take a gerund or a wh-clause (look at raising the fee, look into what went wrong). look for takes a noun phrase only — ✗ look for getting a refund. None of them takes a to-infinitive, and none takes a that-clause. Bare look takes an adjective, not an adverb: look tired, ✗ look tiredly.
Pattern
look at [something — direct your eyes, or examine] · look for [something — try to find] · look after [someone — take care of] · look into [something — investigate] · look over [something — review] · look through [something — scan or search]
Contrast
This is the family that makes look the most treacherous verb in the dictionary — six prepositions, six unrelated meanings, and no way to guess any of them. Look at the report is reading it; look for the report is hunting for it; look after the report is nonsense unless the report is a child; look into the report is investigating what it says; look over the report is reviewing it quickly. Three more sit in their own entries: look forward to (anticipate), look up to (admire), look down on (despise). And look up a word is a seventh thing again. When in doubt, name the meaning in plain words rather than guessing a particle.

Direct your eyes, search, tend, or investigate — the preposition decides which.

PatternWhat it meansExample
look atdirect your eyes; examineLook at this photo.
look fortry to findI'm looking for my keys.
look aftertake care ofShe looks after her nephew.
look intoinvestigateWe'll look into the complaint.
look overreview quicklyLook over the draft tonight.
look throughscan or rummageHe looked through the files.
look forward to + -inganticipate with pleasureI look forward to hearing from you.
look up toadmireHe looks up to his sister.
look down onconsider inferiorThey look down on outsiders.
look up [a word]find in a referenceLook it up.
  1. Look at the third column and tell me if that number makes sense.
  2. I've been looking for my other glove since Tuesday.
  3. Her neighbor looks after the dog whenever she travels.
  4. The state is looking into how the permits were issued.
  5. We're looking at raising the fee about eight percent.
  6. He looked through the whole drawer and found nothing.
  7. Can you look over the contract before I sign it?

look forward to#

Pronunciation
/ˈlʊk ˈfɔrwɚd tu/ · look FOR-werd too · weak /ˈlʊk ˈfɔrwɚd tə/
Русский
с нетерпением ждать — после to герундий: looking forward to hearing from you
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral; the standard closing of American business correspondence
Complement
noun phrase or gerund — to here is a preposition, not the infinitive marker, so the verb after it must be -ing: look forward to hearing from you, never ✗ look forward to hear from you. A wh-clause is possible (look forward to whatever comes next). Never a to-infinitive, never a that-clause.
Pattern
look forward to [noun phrase] · look forward to [verb-ing] · be looking forward to [something] · I look forward to hearing from you (fixed closing)
Contrast
This is the single most-cited gerund trap in English, and the reason is that the to looks exactly like the to of want to go. The test that settles it: substitute a plain noun. I look forward to the meeting works, so to is a preposition, so a verb after it must be -ing. The same test exposes the rest of the family — object to, be used to, get used to, be accustomed to, resort to, confess to, admit to, commit to, devote yourself to, be opposed to, all of them -ing. Register note: I look forward to is the formal closing and I'm looking forward to is the warmer one; both are correct.

Anticipate something with pleasure.

  1. I look forward to hearing from you.
  2. She's looking forward to three weeks with no email.
  3. We look forward to working with your team on this.
  4. He looks forward to the county fair the way children look forward to Christmas.
  5. I'm looking forward to seeing the finished kitchen.
  6. Nobody looks forward to open enrollment.
See also
object to · resort to · commit to · get used to · get around to · hope for · long for · look at · accustomed to · to

look up to, look down on#

Pronunciation
/ˈlʊk ˈʌp tu/ · look UP too · weak /ˈlʊk ˈʌp tə/
Русский
смотреть снизу вверх, уважать (up to); смотреть свысока, презирать (down on)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or pronoun naming a person or group — no gerund, no clause, no infinitive. Both are fixed three-word units and neither can be split: ✗ look him up to, ✗ look down them on. The object always follows the whole phrase.
Pattern
look up to [someone] · look down on [someone] · be looked up to · be looked down on
Contrast
A matched pair of opposites, and neither one is literal. Look up to means admire; look down on means consider beneath you. Do not confuse either with look up a word (consult a reference), which is a separate phrasal verb and is separable: look it up. The British look down one's nose at is the same idea with more scorn. Note that both of these passivize freely, which is unusual for prepositional verbs: he was looked up to by everyone in the shop.

Admire someone, or consider someone inferior.

  1. Every apprentice in that shop looked up to her.
  2. He looks down on anyone who didn't go to a four-year school.
  3. Kids look up to coaches more than coaches realize.
  4. The town has always been looked down on by the county seat.
  5. She looked up to her older brother until she was about fourteen.
  6. Nobody likes being looked down on by a twenty-four-year-old consultant.

major in, minor in#

Pronunciation
/ˈmeɪdʒɚ ɪn/ · MAY-jer in · weak /ˈmeɪdʒɚ ən/
Русский
специализироваться на (основной профиль); брать вторым профилем; амер. вуз
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral — standard American college vocabulary; British speakers say read [a subject] or do a degree in
Complement
noun phrase naming a field of study. No gerund (✗ major in studying biology), no infinitive, no clause. The verb has no other object: ✗ she majored biology, ✗ she majored to biology.
Pattern
major in [a subject] · minor in [a subject] · double major in [X] and [Y] · major in [X] with a minor in [Y] · her major is [a subject] · a major in [a subject] — the noun keeps in
Contrast
Major in is what a student does; specialize in is what a professional does. You major in economics in college and specialize in bankruptcy work afterward. The preposition never changes: ✗ major on chemistry is not American English, and ✗ graduate in chemistry is not either — Americans graduate with a degree in chemistry or simply graduate from a school. Note that the same in stays on the noun: a major in chemistry, a minor in Spanish.

Choose a field as the main, or the secondary, focus of a college degree.

  1. She majored in mechanical engineering and minored in Portuguese.
  2. Half the people I know who majored in philosophy ended up in software.
  3. He double majored in history and studio art, which took five years.
  4. You don't have to major in the subject to take the seminar.
  5. Nobody majors in that anymore; the department closed in 2018.
  6. A minor in statistics is worth more on the transcript than most electives.

make up for#

Pronunciation
/ˌmeɪk ˈʌp fɔr/ · make UP for · weak /ˌmeɪk əp fɚ/
Русский
компенсировать, возмещать — заглаживать нехватку, потерю или вину
Type
verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional)
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund — make up for the delay, make up for missing the deadline. Never a to-infinitive (✗ make up for to miss it) and never a clause. In this three-word frame nothing may be inserted: ✗ make the delay up for.
Pattern
make up for [a loss, a delay, a shortfall, verb-ing] · make up for lost time — the fixed phrase · make it up to [someone] — a separate frame with to · make up [a story, the bed, an exam] — separable, different verbs · make up with [someone] — reconcile
Contrast
Two frames, and the preposition tells them apart. Make up for takes the loss: nothing makes up for a lost season. Make it up to takes the person: I'll make it up to you. Both are idiomatic; mixing them is not: ✗ I'll make it up for you says something else. Against the neighbors, compensate for is the formal twin, offset against is the accounting version, and atone for is the moral one. Note the crowd of unrelated make up verbs — make up a story, make up the bed, make up an exam, make up with your brother — all of which take a movable object and belong with the phrasal verbs.

Compensate for a loss, a shortfall, or a failing.

  1. Two good quarters do not make up for a terrible year.
  2. He worked Saturdays to make up for the week he missed.
  3. Nothing makes up for missing your kid's last season.
  4. The rebate barely makes up for the increase in the premium.
  5. She tried to make up for forgetting the anniversary with a trip neither of them wanted.
  6. I know I let you down; I'll make it up to you.
See also
compensate for · offset against, set off against · pay for · live up to · for

militate against#

Pronunciation
/ˈmɪləteɪt əˈɡɛnst/ · MIL-uh-tayt uh-GENST · weak /ˈmɪləteɪt əɡənst/
Русский
говорить против, свидетельствовать не в пользу — книжн.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — academic writing, judicial opinions, and policy analysis. An American meets it in a law review or a peer-reviewed paper, almost never in speech.
Complement
noun phrase naming a conclusion, a finding, an outcome, or a course of action, or a gerund (militates against raising rates). No to-infinitive (✗ militates against to raise rates) and no clause. The subject is a fact or a consideration, never a person: ✗ the senator militates against the bill — a person argues against it.
Pattern
[a fact, a factor] militate against [a conclusion, a course] · militate against [verb-ing] · militate in favor of [something] — the much rarer positive
Contrast
Militate is not mitigate, and ✗ mitigate against is the single commonest error in this corner of the language. Mitigate is transitive and means lessen: mitigate the damage, mitigating circumstances. Militate against means count as a reason against something. The two are unrelated words, and an editor will catch the swap every time. If the sentence would work with argues against or weighs against, militate is the word you want; if it would work with reduces, you want mitigate.

Be a consideration that weighs against a conclusion or a course of action.

  1. Everything in the record militates against a finding of bad faith.
  2. The cost of relocation militates against moving the plant at all.
  3. Two factors militate against raising rates before the fall.
  4. His inexperience militates against giving him the district by himself.
  5. Nothing in the survey militates against the committee's reading of the data.
  6. The short timeline militates against a full environmental review.
See also
insure against · protect from, protect against · warn about, warn against · decide on, decide against · against

move for#

Pronunciation
/ˈmuv fɔr/ · MOOV for · weak /ˈmuv fɚ/
Русский
ходатайствовать о — вносить формальное предложение в суде или на собрании; юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — courtroom and parliamentary procedure. An American meets it on a docket (defendant moves for summary judgment), in a transcript, or at a meeting run under Robert's Rules.
Complement
for takes a noun phrase naming the relief or action requested: summary judgment, a mistrial, a continuance, dismissal, adjournment. No gerund (✗ move for dismissing the case) and no clause after for. The verb's other two frames use no preposition at all: move to [verb] is a to-infinitive (moved to dismiss), and move that [clause] takes a subjunctive (I move that the meeting be adjourned).
Pattern
move for [relief] · move to [verb] — infinitive, not a preposition · move that [clause] — subjunctive · a motion for [relief] · a motion to [verb] — the nouns split the same way
Contrast
Three frames, one verb, and the preposition is only in the first. Moved for summary judgment, moved to dismiss, and moved that the case be reassigned are all correct and all mean ask formally; ✗ moved for to dismiss and ✗ moved for that the case be reassigned are not. Against the neighbors: you move for relief inside a case that already exists, you petition for something to start a proceeding, and you apply for a permit or a job. And do not confuse any of this with everyday move to Denver, where to marks direction.

Ask a court or a meeting formally for a ruling or an action.

  1. The defense moved for a directed verdict at the close of the state's case.
  2. Counsel moved for a continuance, citing the witness's surgery.
  3. Both parties moved for summary judgment, and the judge denied both motions.
  4. I move for adjournment.
  5. The plaintiff moved to strike two paragraphs of the answer.
  6. She moved that the vote be taken by roll call.
See also
petition for · apply for, apply to · ask for, ask about · comply with · for

O–P#

object to#

Pronunciation
/əbˈdʒɛkt tu/ · ub-JEKT too · weak /əbˈdʒɛkt tə/
Русский
возражать против — после to герундий: object to being questioned
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase, gerund, or possessive plus gerund (objected to my being there) — to is a preposition here, so a to-infinitive is impossible: ✗ object to be questioned, ✗ object to answer. Before a clause the preposition drops: objected that the sample was too small.
Pattern
object to [noun phrase] · object to [verb-ing] · object to [someone's] -ing · object that [clause] · object (alone) · I object. · objection to [something]
Contrast
Same trap as look forward to, and the same test defuses it: you can say object to the delay, so to is a preposition, so it must be object to being delayed. Note the difference from oppose, which is transitive and takes no preposition at all — oppose the merger, ✗ oppose to the merger — although the adjective goes back to a preposition, opposed to the merger. In register, object to is stronger and more formal than mind (I don't mind waiting) and weaker than refuse.

State that you are against something.

  1. Two members objected to holding the vote without a quorum.
  2. She objects to being called by her first name in court.
  3. Nobody objected to the change until the invoices arrived.
  4. He objected to my parking in front of his house, which is a public street.
  5. The defense objected and was overruled.
  6. I don't object to the fee; I object to finding out about it afterward.
See also
look forward to · oppose · complain about · approve of · disapprove of · agree to · insist on

occur to#

Pronunciation
/əˈkɜr tu/ · uh-KUR too · weak /əˈkɜr tə/
Русский
приходить в голову кому-то — it never occurred to me; ср. occur происходить
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to takes a person only. The subject is nearly always the dummy it, followed by a that-clause or a to-infinitive: it occurred to me that she was right, it never occurred to him to ask. A noun phrase subject also works: the idea occurred to her in the shower. There is no gerund pattern here at all.
Pattern
it occur to [someone] that [clause] · it occur to [someone] to [verb] · [an idea] occur to [someone] · it never occurred to me · occur (alone — happen)
Contrast
Two verbs share one spelling. Plain occur means happen and takes no preposition: the outage occurred at 3 a.m., ✗ occurred to 3 a.m. Occur to means come into someone's mind, and always names a person. American speakers use the negative far more than the positive: It never occurred to me is the everyday form. Note the spelling of the inflections — occurred, occurring, with two r's.

Come into someone's mind.

  1. It never occurred to me that he might say no.
  2. Did it occur to anyone to check whether the room was booked?
  3. The solution occurred to her on the drive home.
  4. It occurs to me that we've been arguing about two different documents.
  5. It hadn't occurred to the committee to ask the tenants.
  6. The thought must have occurred to him at some point.
See also
remind of · think of · explain to

offset against, set off against#

Pronunciation
/ˌɔfˈsɛt əˈɡɛnst/ · awf-SET uh-GENST · weak /ˌɔfˈsɛt əɡənst/ — the verb stresses the second syllable, the noun the first (an ˈoffset)
Русский
зачитывать одно против другого — засчитывать в погашение; фин./юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — accounting, tax, and contract drafting
Complement
the amount applied is the direct object and against takes the amount it is applied to: offset the loss against this year's gains. No gerund (✗ offset against having paid twice), no infinitive, no clause. Both slots must name quantities of the same kind — money against money, hours against hours: ✗ offset the delay against the budget.
Variants
Set off against is the same operation in the language of contracts and litigation, and its noun is a setoff (or set-off): the buyer asserted a setoff against the unpaid balance. Note also the commoner everyday frame offset by, where the counterweight follows a passive: the gains were offset by higher fuel costs.
Pattern
offset [an amount] against [another amount] · set off [a claim] against [a debt] · [something] be offset by [something else] · a setoff against [a balance] · deduct [an amount] from [a total] — the neighboring frame with from
Contrast
Offset against subtracts one figure from another of the same kind; deduct from removes a figure from a total. You offset capital losses against capital gains, but you deduct mileage from taxable income. Offset by is the passive counterweight frame and needs no arithmetic at all (the raise was offset by the new premium), and make up for is the plain-English version of the whole idea. In British and legal drafting set off against dominates; American filings use both, and offset is the safer everyday choice.

Apply one amount against another so that the two cancel.

  1. Capital losses may be offset against capital gains in the same tax year.
  2. The contractor sought to set off the cost of repairs against the final invoice.
  3. Any advance is offset against the royalties the book eventually earns.
  4. Higher volume was offset by a thinner margin on every unit.
  5. The tenant set off three months of unmade repairs against the rent she owed.
  6. Overtime in December was offset against the hours he had banked in the fall.
See also
make up for · compensate for · charge with, charge for · pay for · against

participate in#

Pronunciation
/pɑrˈtɪsəˌpeɪt ɪn/ · par-TISS-uh-payt in · weak /pɑrˈtɪsəˌpeɪt ən/
Русский
участвовать в — принимать деятельное участие
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal to neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (participated in drafting the report) — never a to-infinitive. Participate is intransitive and cannot take a bare object: ✗ participate the study.
Pattern
participate in [an activity, a program, a study] · participate in [verb-ing] · participate (alone) · take part in [something] — the plainer synonym
Contrast
Participate to and ✗ participate at are errors; the preposition is fixed at in. Take part in means exactly the same thing in plainer English and is what Americans say out loud. Keep it apart from attend, which is transitive and takes no preposition at all — attend the meeting, ✗ attend to the meeting — so she attended the workshop (was present) and she participated in the workshop (did the exercises) are genuinely different claims.

Take an active part in something.

  1. Four hundred families participated in the pilot program.
  2. He participated in drafting the original charter.
  3. Students who participate in the survey get a gift card.
  4. Only two states declined to participate in the settlement.
  5. You can attend without participating in the breakout sessions.
  6. She participated in every rehearsal and missed the performance.
See also
involve in · attend · contribute to · compete with · interfere in

pay for#

Pronunciation
/ˈpeɪ fɔr/ · PAY for · weak /ˈpeɪ fɚ/
Русский
платить за — отдавать деньги в обмен на что-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for takes a noun phrase or a gerund (paid for having it towed) — never a to-infinitive after the preposition. A different frame takes the infinitive after a person: pay someone to do it. The bill, the fee, and the person are all direct objects with no preposition: pay the rent, pay the plumber, pay $40.
Pattern
pay for [a thing or service] · pay [someone] for [something] · pay [an amount] for [something] · pay [a bill, the rent, taxes] · pay [someone] to [verb] · pay for [a mistake] — suffer for it
Contrast
Pay the meal and ✗ pay for the bill are both wrong, and they are wrong in opposite directions. You pay a bill, an invoice, the rent, a fine, or a person — no preposition, because those are the money itself or its recipient. You pay for a meal, a haircut, a ticket, or a mistake — the thing you get in return. Note the idiom: he'll pay for that means suffer consequences, and pay off means both settle a debt and succeed (the gamble paid off).

Give money in exchange for something.

  1. Who's paying for the catering?
  2. She paid the contractor for the framing and withheld the rest.
  3. I paid almost nine hundred dollars for a repair that lasted a month.
  4. Pay the water bill before Friday or they add a fee.
  5. He paid for skipping the inspection about eighteen months later.
  6. They paid a neighbor to watch the house.
See also
charge for · invest in · provide for · punish for · for

petition for#

Pronunciation
/pəˈtɪʃən fɔr/ · puh-TISH-un for · weak /pəˈtɪʃən fɚ/
Русский
подавать ходатайство о — официально просить суд или орган; юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — courts, agencies, legislatures, and civic organizing. Americans meet the frame in the First Amendment itself: the right to petition the Government for a redress of grievances.
Complement
for takes a noun phrase naming what is asked for — review, custody, bankruptcy, clemency, a variance, a recount. No gerund (✗ petition for getting the fee waived) and no clause. The body petitioned is a direct object placed before for: petition the board for a hearing. A to-infinitive frame exists and is not this preposition: petitioned the council to rezone the parcel.
Pattern
petition for [relief, a status, a hearing] · petition [a body] for [something] · petition [a body] to [verb] — infinitive · file a petition for [something] · petition in bankruptcy
Contrast
Three verbs, three settings. You apply for a permit, a job, or a loan — routine, on a form. You petition for something that requires a body to exercise judgment, and the word implies a formal filing or a signed request. You move for relief inside a case already pending. Sue for is different again: it names the remedy you are demanding in litigation (sue for damages). Note that the noun keeps the preposition — a petition for review, a petition for certiorari — and that with a person the verb goes straight to the object: petition the mayor, not ✗ petition to the mayor.

Ask a court, an agency, or an official body formally for something.

  1. The company petitioned for Chapter 11 protection in March.
  2. Six hundred residents petitioned for a special election.
  3. She petitioned the court for sole custody after the second incident.
  4. Counsel petitioned for review, and the Supreme Court declined to hear it.
  5. The neighbors petitioned the zoning board for a variance and lost twice.
  6. He petitioned for clemency every year for a decade.
See also
move for · apply for, apply to · ask for, ask about · foreclose on · for

plan on#

Pronunciation
/ˈplæn ɑn/ · PLAN on · weak /ˈplæn ən/
Русский
рассчитывать, планировать — после on герундий: plan on staying; разг.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral American; more common in the U.S. than in Britain
Complement
gerund or noun phrase (plan on staying, plan on rain) — never a to-infinitive after the preposition (✗ plan on to stay). The infinitive belongs to the other frame: plan to stay, with no preposition. Both are correct and mean the same thing.
Pattern
plan on [verb-ing] · plan on [noun phrase] · plan to [verb] · plan for [a contingency] · plan [an event] · plan on [someone] -ing
Contrast
Plan on doing is characteristically American; British English leans harder on plan to do. Both are standard in the U.S., with plan on slightly more casual and slightly more provisional — I plan to retire in June sounds settled, I'm planning on retiring in June sounds like it could move. Plan for is different: it means prepare for a possibility (plan for a bad winter), not intend. And plan on someone doing something means count on it: don't plan on him being early.

Intend to do something.

  1. We're planning on driving up Thursday night.
  2. Don't plan on getting a seat if you show up at seven.
  3. She plans on finishing the certificate in the spring.
  4. Plan on about two hours with traffic.
  5. They planned for a shortfall and got a surplus instead.
  6. I hadn't planned on any of this.
See also

point at, point to#

Pronunciation
/ˈpɔɪnt æt/ · POYNT at · weak /ˈpɔɪnt ət/
Русский
показывать пальцем на (at); указывать на, свидетельствовать о (to)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause. Point out is a separate, separable phrasal verb that does take a that-clause: point out that the numbers don't match, and point something out to someone.
Pattern
point at [a person, a thing] · point to [a direction, a piece of evidence] · point [something] at [something] · point out [something] · point out to [someone] that [clause] · point toward [a conclusion]
Contrast
At is the physical gesture; to is the argument. She pointed at the map is a finger; the data point to a slowdown is an inference. Where a person is the target, point at is faintly rude — American children are told not to point at people — while point to someone as a model is a compliment. Point out is a third thing entirely: it means draw attention to a fact, takes an object or a clause, and is separable (point it out). ✗ point on is not English in any of these senses.

Indicate something with a finger, or indicate it with evidence.

  1. He pointed at the crack in the foundation and said nothing.
  2. Every indicator points to a soft landing, which is what they said last time too.
  3. Don't point at people.
  4. She pointed the flashlight at the ceiling to see the water stain.
  5. The report points to deferred maintenance as the underlying cause.
  6. I should point out that none of this was in the original scope.
See also
aim at · refer to · lead to · stare at · comment on

prefer to#

Pronunciation
/prɪˈfɜr tu/ · prih-FUR too · weak /prɪˈfɜr tə/
Русский
предпочитать что-то чему-то — здесь to значит чем, а не к
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
In the comparison frame, to is a preposition and both sides must be the same kind of thing — two noun phrases (prefer tea to coffee) or two gerunds (prefer walking to driving). ✗ prefer to walk to drive is impossible. In the other frame, prefer takes a bare to-infinitive with no comparison (I'd prefer to walk), and would prefer takes that plus a subjunctive (I'd prefer that he not come).
Pattern
prefer [A] to [B] · prefer [verb-ing] to [verb-ing] · prefer to [verb] · prefer [verb-ing] · would prefer [someone] to [verb] · would prefer that [clause]
Contrast
The trap is that prefer uses to for two completely different jobs. I prefer coffee to tea — preposition, comparison. I prefer to drink coffee — infinitive marker, no comparison. You cannot run both at once, which is why ✗ I prefer to walk to drive collapses; say I prefer walking to driving, or switch to I'd rather walk than drive, which is the form Americans actually use. Note that than is impossible with prefer in careful writing (✗ prefer coffee than tea), even though it is common in speech.

Like one thing better than another.

  1. She prefers mornings to evenings for anything requiring a brain.
  2. I prefer driving to flying for anything under six hours.
  3. Most of the staff prefer the older system.
  4. He'd prefer to handle it himself, which is the whole problem.
  5. They prefer email to phone calls, and they are correct.
  6. I'd prefer that we settle this before the meeting.
See also
choose between · compare to · decide on · long for · in preference to

prepare for#

Pronunciation
/prɪˈpɛr fɔr/ · prih-PAIR for · weak /prɪˈpɛr fɚ/
Русский
готовиться к — заранее приводить себя в готовность
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for takes a noun phrase or a gerund (prepare for having to move) — never a to-infinitive after the preposition (✗ prepare for to move). Prepare also takes a bare to-infinitive with no preposition: prepare to move, be prepared to negotiate — and that frame means readiness to act, not readiness for an event.
Pattern
prepare for [an event] · prepare for [verb-ing] · prepare [someone] for [something] · prepare to [verb] · be prepared for [something] · be prepared to [verb]
Contrast
Prepared for and prepared to are not the same claim. We're prepared for a strike means we have contingencies; We're prepared to strike means we're willing to do it. Reversing them in a negotiation would be expensive. Compare get ready for, the plain synonym, and provide for, which means support financially. Note also transitive prepare: you prepare a meal, prepare a brief, prepare the ground — no preposition.

Get ready for something in advance.

  1. The whole county spent a week preparing for the storm.
  2. She's been preparing for the boards since March.
  3. Nothing prepares you for how loud a newborn is.
  4. Prepare for having to explain this twice.
  5. They were prepared for a long negotiation and got a handshake in twenty minutes.
  6. He's prepared to walk away if the number doesn't move.
See also
