preside over#

Pronunciation
/prɪˈzaɪd ˈoʊvɚ/ · prih-ZIDE OH-ver · weak /prɪˈzaɪd əvɚ/
Русский
председательствовать на, возглавлять — руководить заседанием, органом, эпохой
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — courts, boards, ceremonies, and, with a twist of irony, institutional decline
Complement
noun phrase naming a proceeding, a body, an era, or a process: preside over the trial, over the committee, over the collapse of the company. No gerund (✗ preside over running the meeting), no infinitive, no clause. The verb needs the preposition — ✗ preside the meeting is not English, though chair the meeting is.
Variants
Preside at is used for a single ceremony or sitting — preside at the wedding, at the hearing — while preside over takes the whole proceeding, body, or period. Both are standard; over is far commoner.
Pattern
preside over [a trial, a meeting, an agency, an era] · preside at [a ceremony, a hearing] · preside — intransitive (Judge Alvarez presided) · presiding judge / presiding officer · the president of [an organization] — the noun takes of
Contrast
Preside over always takes a preposition; its synonyms usually do not. Chair the committee, run the meeting, and oversee the project are all transitive, and ✗ preside the committee is the error that follows from them. The ironic use is worth knowing because it is everywhere in American commentary: he presided over the loss of half the endowment attributes no action to the subject at all, only the office he held while it happened.

Be the person in charge of a proceeding, a body, or a period.

  1. Judge Alvarez presided over the trial for eleven weeks.
  2. She presided over the commission during its most productive stretch.
  3. He presided over a merger that cost four hundred people their jobs.
  4. The vice president presides over the Senate and votes only to break a tie.
  5. A retired bishop presided at the funeral.
  6. Whoever presides over that department inherits a decade of deferred maintenance.
See also
prevail over · devolve on, devolve upon · participate in · belong to · over

prevail over#

Pronunciation
/prɪˈveɪl ˈoʊvɚ/ · prih-VAYL OH-ver · weak /prɪˈveɪl əvɚ/
Русский
одержать верх над — победить соперника, притязание или препятствие
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — law, sports writing, and scripture-flavored prose
Complement
noun phrase naming an opponent, a rival claim, or an obstacle. No gerund (✗ prevailed over being outspent), no infinitive, no clause. The verb also stands alone (the plaintiff prevailed) and takes other prepositions in other senses, listed below.
Variants
Prevail against is the older, more literary form and survives in fixed phrases (the gates of hell shall not prevail against it); in legal writing prevail over a competing provision is the standard. Prevail in names the arena rather than the loser: prevailed in the arbitration.
Pattern
prevail over / against [an opponent, a competing claim] · prevail in [a suit, an election] · prevail — intransitive (the plaintiff prevailed) · prevail on / upon [someone] to [verb] — persuade, a different pattern · the prevailing wage, the prevailing wind — be widespread
Contrast
Two patterns, and confusing them changes the meaning completely. Prevail over someone is defeating them; prevail on someone to do something is talking them into it — and that second frame takes a person and then a to-infinitive: prevailed on her to stay another year. A third, intransitive sense means be widespread or hold sway (the practice still prevails in rural counties, the prevailing view). In drafting, this section shall prevail over any conflicting provision is the standard way to say which clause wins.

Win out over an opponent, a rival claim, or an obstacle.

  1. The plaintiffs prevailed over three motions to dismiss before reaching trial.
  2. Where the two provisions conflict, the later one prevails over the earlier.
  3. Nebraska prevailed over a heavily favored opponent in double overtime.
  4. Common sense finally prevailed over the committee's fondness for process.
  5. She prevailed in the arbitration but spent more than she recovered.
  6. His sister prevailed upon him to stay through the holidays.
See also
preside over · compete with, compete against, compete for · militate against · insist on · over

prevent from#

Pronunciation
/prɪˈvɛnt frʌm/ · prih-VENT from · weak /prɪˈvɛnt frəm/
Русский
не давать сделать, предотвращать — после from герундий: prevent him from leaving
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
gerund or noun phrase — never a to-infinitive (✗ prevent him to leave, one of the highest-frequency advanced errors in English). The only correct verb form after from is -ing: prevent him from leaving. Prevent also takes a plain object with no preposition: prevent an accident.
Pattern
prevent [someone / something] from [verb-ing] · prevent [something] · be prevented from [verb-ing] · prevent [someone] [verb-ing] — British; add from in American English
Contrast
Prevent someone to do something does not exist. The pattern is from plus -ing, and it is shared by a whole family: stop from, keep from, discourage from, prohibit from, ban from, bar from, deter from, hinder from, restrain from. Their positive counterparts take infinitives — allow to, permit to, enable to, encourage to — which is exactly why the error is so persistent. British English also permits prevent him leaving with no preposition; American editing puts the from back in.

Stop something from happening, or stop someone from acting.

  1. A bad hinge prevented the door from closing all the way.
  2. Nothing prevents you from filing an amended return.
  3. The guardrail prevented the car from going into the ravine.
  4. She was prevented from testifying by a scheduling order.
  5. Two inches of insulation prevents most of the heat loss.
  6. What's preventing us from starting today?
See also
discourage from · protect from · allow for · interfere with

protect from, protect against#

Pronunciation
/prəˈtɛkt frʌm/ · pruh-TEKT from · weak /prəˈtɛkt frəm/
Русский
защищать от — оберегать от опасности или вреда
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (protect against having to pay twice) — never a to-infinitive. Both prepositions take the same complements and are largely interchangeable; see the contrast for the tendency that separates them.
Pattern
protect [someone] from [harm] · protect [someone] against [a risk] · be protected from / against [something] · protect [something] — no preposition · protection from / against [something]
Contrast
From leans toward harm already present; against leans toward risk anticipated. You protect a child from the sun (it's shining now) and protect a portfolio against inflation (it might come). The overlap is enormous and no American will misunderstand either. One place against wins outright is inoculation and insurance: vaccinated against measles, insured against flood, hedge against a downturn — ✗ vaccinated from measles is wrong. Guard against takes only against, and shield from takes only from.

Keep someone or something safe.

  1. A good hat protects you from more sun than sunscreen does.
  2. The statute protects whistleblowers from retaliation.
  3. The vaccine protects against four strains.
  4. Nothing protects a small business against a bad landlord.
  5. The seawall protects the district from ordinary tides and nothing more.
  6. She protected her sources from everyone, including her editor.
See also
prevent from · save from · hide from · deprive of · against

provide with, provide for#

Pronunciation
/prəˈvaɪd wɪθ/ · pruh-VIDE with · weak /prəˈvaɪd wəθ/
Русский
обеспечивать — provide smb with smth = provide smth for smb; provide for также содержать
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal to neutral
Complement
with takes the thing supplied and the recipient is the direct object: provide students with laptops. for takes the beneficiary, or the contingency: provide for a family, the contract provides for arbitration. A gerund works after for (provides for extending the deadline); ✗ a to-infinitive does not.
Pattern
provide [someone] with [something] · provide [something] for [someone] · provide for [a family] · [a document] provide for [something] · provide that [clause] — legal · provide [something]
Two frames say the same thing with the arguments swapped
provide the crew with meals = provide meals for the crew. Mixing them (✗ provide the crew meals in formal writing, ✗ provide with meals to the crew) is the error. Then provide for on its own means support financially (he provided for four children on a machinist's wage), and in a contract it means stipulate (the lease provides for a five percent escalator). The legal provide that takes a full clause and no preposition.

Supply something, support someone, or stipulate something.

  1. The district provides every ninth grader with a laptop.
  2. They provided coffee and donuts for the volunteers.
  3. He provided for his mother for the last eleven years of her life.
  4. The bylaws provide for removing a director by two-thirds vote.
  5. The grant provides the lab with three years of funding.
  6. Nothing in the policy provides for a refund.
See also
cover with · pay for · prepare for · allow for · with

punish for#

Pronunciation
/ˈpʌnɪʃ fɔr/ · PUN-ish for · weak /ˈpʌnɪʃ fɚ/
Русский
наказывать за — налагать взыскание за содеянное
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (punished for lying) — never a to-infinitive (✗ punished for to lie) and never a clause. The person punished is a direct object: ✗ punish to him. The penalty itself takes with or by: punishable by a fine.
Pattern
punish [someone] for [something / verb-ing] · be punished for [something] · punish [someone] with [a penalty] · punishable by [a sentence] · punishment for [an offense]
Contrast
The offense takes for; the penalty takes by or with. He was punished for the leak names what he did; punishable by five years names what happens to him. ✗ punish someone of and ✗ punish someone about are errors. The noun keeps the same split: punishment for the offense, a sentence of five years. Compare blame for (assign fault, no penalty), charge with (formal accusation), and fine someone for (a specific monetary penalty, same shape as punish).

Impose a penalty on someone for something they did.

  1. Two students were punished for something a third one did.
  2. He was punished for taking the complaint over his manager's head.
  3. The rule punishes small filers for an honest mistake.
  4. Nobody should be punished for asking a question.
  5. Tampering is punishable by a fine of up to ten thousand dollars.
  6. She punished him for the lie rather than the thing he lied about.
See also
blame for · charge with · accuse of · forgive for · excuse for

put up with#

Pronunciation
/ˌpʊt ʌp ˈwɪθ/ · PUT up with · weak /ˌpʊt əp wəθ/
Русский
мириться с, терпеть — сносить неприятное, ничего не предпринимая
Type
verb + particle + preposition (phrasal-prepositional)
Register
informal to neutral — the everyday American way to say tolerate
Complement
noun phrase or gerund — put up with the noise, put up with being talked over. Never a to-infinitive (✗ put up with to be talked over) and never a clause. The string is fixed and the object always follows the preposition: ✗ put the noise up with, ✗ put it up with.
Pattern
put up with [something, someone, verb-ing] · not put up with [something] — the commonest frame · put up [a tent, a fight, a candidate] — separable, unrelated · put [someone] up — house them · put [someone] up to [something] — incite them
Contrast
Put up with is fixed; the other put up verbs are not. Put the guests up for the weekend and put the shelves up both allow the object to move, which is what makes their second word a particle; those belong with the phrasal verbs. This one never splits. The formal synonyms are transitive and take no preposition at all — tolerate the noise, endure the delay, stand it — so ✗ tolerate with the noise is the error that comes of translating from this pattern. Live with is its resigned cousin, and cope with is the version that implies effort rather than patience.

Tolerate something or someone annoying without doing anything about it.

  1. I'm not going to put up with another winter without a working furnace.
  2. She put up with that commute for six years.
  3. Nobody should have to put up with being spoken to that way at work.
  4. The neighbors put up with the construction noise until it started at six in the morning.
  5. He puts up with a lot from a client who pays that slowly.
  6. There is a limit to what a volunteer board will put up with.
See also

Q–R#

qualify for#

Pronunciation
/ˈkwɑləˌfaɪ fɔr/ · KWOL-uh-fye for · weak /ˈkwɑləˌfaɪ fɚ/
Русский
иметь право на, подходить под условия — соответствовать требованиям
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for takes a noun phrase — no gerund in ordinary use (✗ qualify for getting aid; say qualify for aid). To put a verb after qualify, use the infinitive with no preposition: qualify to vote, qualified to practice in three states. As takes a category: qualifies as a hardship.
Pattern
qualify for [a benefit, a program, a round] · qualify to [verb] · qualify as [a category] · be qualified for [a job] · qualify [a statement] — hedge it
Contrast
Qualify for is eligibility; qualify to is capacity; qualify as is classification. She qualifies for the subsidy (income), she's qualified to teach chemistry (credential), that qualifies as an emergency (definition). All three are live and none takes the others' preposition. A fourth, transitive sense means hedge or limit: he qualified the claim in a footnote — no preposition at all, and the opposite of endorsing it.

Meet the requirements for something.

  1. A family of four qualifies for assistance under about sixty thousand.
  2. Two of the four teams qualified for the regional round.
  3. He isn't qualified for the position and everyone in the room knew it.
  4. She's qualified to practice in Ohio and Michigan.
  5. Does a burst pipe qualify as an emergency repair?
  6. Very few of the applications actually qualified for review.
See also
apply for · compete for · prepare for · vote for · consist of

react to#

Pronunciation
/riˈækt tu/ · ree-AKT too · weak /riˈækt tə/
Русский
реагировать на — вести себя определённым образом в ответ; после to герундий
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (reacted to being asked) — never a to-infinitive (✗ react to be asked) and never a clause. React is intransitive: ✗ react the news. In chemistry the preposition is with: sodium reacts with water.
Pattern
react to [something] · react to [verb-ing] · [a substance] react with [another] · react against [a trend, an upbringing] · a reaction to [something]
Contrast
React on is an error in the ordinary sense; the preposition is to. Three related shapes: react to responds to a stimulus, react with is the chemical sense, and react against means push back at something you were formed by (a generation reacting against the earnestness of its parents). Note the medical use — react to a drug, and the noun an allergic reaction to, both with to — and compare respond to, which is interchangeable but implies more deliberation.

Behave in a particular way because of something that happened.

  1. Markets reacted to the announcement before anyone had read it.
  2. She reacted to the news better than I expected.
  3. About one patient in a thousand reacts to the dye.
  4. He reacted to being corrected in front of the class exactly as you'd guess.
  5. The whole neighborhood reacted against the rezoning.
  6. Chlorine reacts with ammonia to produce something you do not want in your bathroom.
See also
adapt to · object to · refer to · occur to

reconcile with#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛkənˌsaɪl wɪθ/ · REK-un-syle with · weak /ˈrɛkənˌsaɪl wəθ/
Русский
мирить(ся) с; согласовывать с — а reconcile oneself to смиряться с неизбежным
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral — the reconcile X with Y frame is standard in accounting and in argument
Complement
with takes a noun phrase — a person, a set of figures, a claim — or a gerund (reconcile that with claiming to be neutral); never a to-infinitive (✗ reconcile it with to claim neutrality). The separate reflexive pattern reconcile oneself to also takes a noun phrase or a gerund (reconciled herself to losing the house) and never an infinitive (✗ reconciled himself to lose it).
Pattern
reconcile [X] with [Y] · be reconciled with [person] · reconcile with [person] · reconcile oneself to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · be reconciled to [noun phrase / verb-ing]
Contrast
With joins two things that ought to match; to resigns you to one you cannot change. She reconciled with her father is a repaired relationship; she reconciled herself to her father's decision is acceptance without repair, and the second cannot use with. In accounting both prepositions are live: reconcile the statement with the ledger is the general idiom, reconcile the statement to the ledger the bookkeeper's. Note that the intransitive the couple reconciled needs no preposition at all.

Make two things agree, make peace with someone, or accept what cannot be changed.

  1. The two brothers reconciled with each other after eleven years.
  2. I can't reconcile his account with what the timestamps show.
  3. Accounting reconciles the daily receipts with the deposit slips every Monday.
  4. She eventually reconciled herself to living on half the income.
  5. How do you reconcile a hiring freeze with opening two new offices?
  6. They were reconciled about a month before he died.
See also
agree with · comply with · compare with · differ from · with

recover from#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈkʌvɚ frʌm/ · rih-KUV-er from · weak /rɪˈkʌvɚ frəm/
Русский
оправляться после (болезни, беды); а recover без предлога возвращать себе
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (recovering from having the flu) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The transitive verb is a different pattern with no preposition: recover the data, recover damages — and there from names the source instead (recover the money from the insurer).
Pattern
recover from [an illness, a setback] · recover from [verb-ing] · recover [something lost] · recover [something] from [a source] · make a recovery from [something]
Contrast
Intransitive recover from is about getting better; transitive recover is about getting something back. She recovered from the surgery and They recovered the stolen equipment share no structure at all. Watch the ambiguity in recover from: the firm recovered from the loss means it bounced back, while the firm recovered from the insurer means it collected. Compare get over, the everyday spoken synonym of the first sense, and heal from, which is newer and common in American therapeutic usage.

Get better after illness or difficulty, or get something back.

  1. He's still recovering from the knee replacement.
  2. The town never really recovered from the plant closing.
  3. It took her a semester to recover from a bad first year.
  4. Divers recovered the black box from about ninety feet of water.
  5. Nobody recovers from that kind of press in one news cycle.
  6. Recovering from being publicly wrong is a skill worth having.
See also
suffer from · benefit from · escape from · die of · cope with

recuse from#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈkjuz frʌm/ · rih-KYOOZ frum · weak /rɪˈkjuz frəm/
Русский
брать самоотвод в — устраняться из-за конфликта интересов; юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
legal — an American meets this word in a judge's order, an ethics memo, or a news story about a justice stepping aside; in conversation you would say step aside or sit this one out
Complement
the verb normally takes a reflexive object first — recuse himself from — and from then takes a noun phrase or a gerund (recused herself from hearing the appeal); never a to-infinitive (✗ recused himself from to hear it). American journalism now also drops the reflexive (the judge recused from the case) and even the object (the justice recused), but a brief will keep himself.
Pattern
recuse [oneself] from [a case, a vote, a decision] · recuse [oneself] from [verb-ing] · recuse from [noun phrase] · be recused from [a panel]
Contrast
Four verbs, four distances. Recuse from steps out of one matter because of a conflict and keeps the job; resign from leaves the job; abstain from keeps the seat and declines to vote; withdraw from says nothing about why. A judge who recuses herself from the case is still on the bench the next morning. The outsider's version is disqualify: a party moves to disqualify the judge, the judge recuses herself. ✗ Recuse of and ✗ recuse out of are errors.

Step out of a case or a decision because you have a conflict of interest.

  1. The judge recused herself from the case because her nephew worked for the defendant.
  2. Two board members recused themselves from voting on the contract.
  3. He should have recused himself from the review of his own department.
  4. The attorney general recused from the investigation last spring.
  5. She recuses herself from anything involving her former firm.
  6. If the conflict is disclosed early, a member can recuse and the meeting goes on.
See also
refrain from · retire from · separate from · testify to · from

refer to#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈfɜr tu/ · rih-FUR too · weak /rɪˈfɜr tə/
Русский
ссылаться на; обращаться к (источнику); направлять к (специалисту); обозначать
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (referred to being underpaid) — never a to-infinitive (✗ refer to be underpaid). In the send-someone-onward frame the person is a direct object: refer the patient to a specialist. Note the doubled r in referred, referring, referral.
Pattern
refer to [something — mention] · refer to [a source — consult] · refer [someone] to [someone else] · [a word] refer to [a thing — denote] · refer to [something] as [a name]
Contrast
Four senses, one preposition, and they are told apart by what surrounds it. He referred to the memo (mentioned it), refer to the appendix (consult it), her doctor referred her to a cardiologist (sent her onward), "quorum" refers to the minimum number (denotes it). The compound referred to as is the standard formula for naming: the area referred to as the Loop. ✗ refer about is not English. Compare mention, which is transitive and takes no preposition — ✗ mention about it is a common error.

Mention something, consult a source, or send someone onward.

  1. She never referred to the incident again.
  2. Refer to the chart on page nine for the full schedule.
  3. The clinic referred him to a specialist in Cleveland.
  4. The term refers to any structure built before the 1968 code.
  5. Everyone at the plant refers to it as the annex, though it isn't attached to anything.
  6. The footnote refers to a study that has since been retracted.
See also
point to · comment on · consult with · introduce to · subscribe to

refrain from#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈfreɪn frʌm/ · rih-FRAYN frum · weak /rɪˈfreɪn frəm/
Русский
воздерживаться от — сознательно не делать; после from герундий
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — the language of signs, announcements, and policies (please refrain from smoking); in conversation Americans say don't or lay off
Complement
gerund, or a noun phrase naming an activity or substance (refrain from comment, refrain from alcohol) — never a to-infinitive (✗ refrain from to smoke, ✗ refrain to comment) and never a that-clause. The one who refrains is always the subject: you cannot ✗ refrain him from talking — that verb is prevent from or keep from.
Pattern
refrain from [verb-ing] · refrain from [noun phrase] · please refrain from [verb-ing]
Contrast
Four ways of not doing something. Refrain from is a deliberate, usually temporary holding back; abstain from is a standing policy — from alcohol, from meat, from a vote; desist from is what a cease-and-desist letter demands; stop takes a bare gerund and no preposition (stop talking, ✗ stop from talking). And unlike prevent from and discourage from, refrain never takes an object of its own. ✗ Refrain of is an error.

Deliberately hold back from doing something.

  1. Please refrain from using flash photography in the gallery.
  2. The judge asked both attorneys to refrain from interrupting each other.
  3. He refrained from saying what everyone in the room was thinking.
  4. Employees should refrain from discussing the settlement publicly.
  5. She refrained from alcohol for the whole year of her residency.
  6. I refrained from pointing out that the same thing happened last spring.
See also
prevent from · discourage from · recuse from · object to · from

relate to#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈleɪt tu/ · rih-LAYT too · weak /rɪˈleɪt tə/
Русский
касаться чего-то; понимать, сочувствовать кому-то; излагать что-то кому-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral; the empathy sense (I can relate to that) is informal and thoroughly American
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (I can relate to being the new person) — never a to-infinitive (✗ relate to be the new person). A wh-clause is fine (everything relating to what happened that night). In the transitive relate X to Y frame both slots are noun phrases.
Pattern
[something] relate to [something] · relate to [someone / an experience] · relate to [verb-ing] · relate [X] to [Y] · be related to [someone] · relating to [topic]
Contrast
Three verbs wear one spelling. This relates to the second complaint means it concerns it; I can relate to her means I identify with her — informal, and the object may be a person, a feeling, or a whole situation; he related the story to us means he told it. Family is a fourth thing again and needs the passive participle: she's related to the governor, never ✗ she relates to the governor. The participle relating to is a stock connector in contracts and subpoenas, interchangeable with concerning and in relation to.

Concern something, identify with someone, or connect one thing to another.

  1. Nothing in the memo relates to the layoffs.
  2. I can relate to wanting to quit halfway through a shift.
  3. He has trouble relating to people he didn't grow up with.
  4. The professor related the data to an earlier study out of Michigan.
  5. All documents relating to the merger have to be preserved.
  6. She's related to half the families in the county.

relieve of#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈliv əv/ · rih-LEEV uv · weak /rɪˈliv ə/
Русский
освобождать от (обязанности, ноши) — снимать бремя с человека
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal, and usually a euphemism — the wording of a personnel notice or a military order (relieved of command); its joking use (relieved me of forty dollars) is a joke about the euphemism
Complement
the person always sits between the verb and of — relieve someone of something — and of takes a noun phrase or a gerund (relieved of having to file the report); never a to-infinitive (✗ relieved of to file the report). There is no ✗ relieve of with no person named, and the passive (was relieved of his duties) is far commoner than the active.
Pattern
relieve [someone] of [duties / a burden / a possession] · [someone] be relieved of [noun phrase / verb-ing]
Contrast
Do not confuse the verb with the feeling. He was relieved of his post means he was removed from it; he was relieved at the news, relieved by the verdict, relieved to hear it means he felt relief — a different word, an adjective, with its own prepositions. Only the first is this pattern. Among neighbors, relieve someone of takes a burden away, free someone from removes a constraint, absolve someone of clears the blame, and fire says plainly what relieved of his duties is written to avoid.

Take a duty, a burden, or a possession away from someone.

  1. The colonel was relieved of command within a week.
  2. A settlement would relieve the company of any further obligation.
  3. She was relieved of the paperwork so she could spend the hour with patients.
  4. The new system relieves the front desk of having to re-key every order.
  5. Two administrators were quietly relieved of their responsibilities in June.
  6. Somebody in the crowd relieved him of his wallet.
See also
deprive of · rob of · rescue from · retire from · of

rely on#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈlaɪ ɑn/ · rih-LIE on · weak /rɪˈlaɪ ən/
Русский
полагаться на — доверять, что сделает нужное
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase, pronoun, or gerund (relying on getting reimbursed) — never a to-infinitive after the preposition (✗ rely on to get reimbursed). A to-infinitive is correct after a person: rely on her to check it. A wh-clause is fine. Rely never appears without its preposition: ✗ you can rely me.
Pattern
rely on [someone / something] · rely on [someone] to [verb] · rely on [verb-ing] · rely on [someone's] -ing · reliance on [something]
Variants
rely upon — identical in meaning, more formal, common in legal and academic writing
Contrast
Rely to, ✗ rely in, and ✗ rely of are all errors. Within the family, rely on is the neutral written form, count on the warm spoken one, and depend on the one that also carries the be-determined-by sense that neither of the others has. Rely upon is the formal variant, standard in legal and academic prose. Note the noun: reliance on — and the legal phrase detrimental reliance, which is this same preposition frozen.

Trust something or someone to do what is needed.

  1. The whole valley relies on one two-lane bridge.
  2. You can rely on her to catch anything I miss.
  3. They relied on volunteer labor for the first three seasons.
  4. Don't rely on the automatic backup; check it once a month.
  5. The finding relies on a sample of forty-one people.
  6. Rural clinics rely upon federal reimbursement for most of their revenue.
See also
depend on · count on · believe in · on

remind of, remind about#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈmaɪnd əv/ · rih-MIND uv · weak /rɪˈmaɪnd ə/
Русский
напоминать — of о сходстве или о факте, about о деле, о котором забыли
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
of takes a noun phrase or a gerund and covers both resemblance and recollection. about takes a noun phrase or a gerund and covers upcoming obligations. The person reminded is always a direct object: ✗ remind to me. Remind also takes a to-infinitive after the person (remind me to call) and a that-clause (remind him that it's Tuesday) — neither with a preposition.
Pattern
remind [someone] of [something / someone] · remind [someone] about [an obligation] · remind [someone] to [verb] · remind [someone] that [clause] · That reminds me.
Contrast
Four patterns, and of versus about is the one that carries meaning. You remind me of my father is resemblance — it says you are like him. Remind me about the deposit is a prompt about a task. And remind me to make the deposit is a prompt to act, using the infinitive rather than any preposition. Mixing them produces sentences that are grammatical and wrong: ✗ remind me of making the deposit says the act of depositing calls something else to mind.

Cause someone to remember something, or make you think of something else.

  1. This street reminds me of the town my mother grew up in.
  2. Remind me about the parking pass before we leave.
  3. She reminded him to bring the second set of keys.
  4. Your handwriting reminds me of my grandmother's.
  5. Remind the committee that the deadline is a statutory one.
  6. Nothing reminds you of how old a house is like a January heating bill.
See also
forget about · occur to · think of · warn about · explain to

rescue from#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛskju frʌm/ · RESS-kyoo from · weak /ˈrɛskju frəm/
Русский
спасать из, спасать от — вытаскивать из опасности или беды
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (rescued from having to speak) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The person or thing rescued is a direct object and the danger takes from: ✗ rescue to someone.
Pattern
rescue [someone] from [danger] · be rescued from [something] · rescue [someone] · a rescue from [something] · rescue [an animal] — as a noun, an adopted animal
Contrast
Rescue takes only from. Its neighbors keep their own: save someone from (interchangeable, broader), free someone from or of, deliver someone from (biblical or formal), bail someone out of trouble (informal, and note the particle). In American usage rescue has also become a noun for an adopted shelter animal — she's a rescue — with no preposition at all.

Get someone out of danger or difficulty.

  1. The crew rescued four people from the roof of a submerged car.
  2. A neighbor rescued the dog from a storm drain.
  3. She rescued the whole presentation from a dead projector and a lost cable.
  4. Two hikers were rescued from the ridge after dark.

reside in#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈzaɪd ɪn/ · rih-ZYDE in · weak /rɪˈzaɪd ən/
Русский
проживать где-то (юр.); быть заключённым в — о свойстве или власти
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — a lease, a tax form, a voter-registration rule, a court opinion, or a civics sentence about where power lies; in speech Americans say live
Complement
noun phrase naming a place, a body, or an abstraction (the difficulty resides in the wording). A gerund is possible in the abstract sense but stilted (its value resides in being reproducible) — prefer a noun. Never a to-infinitive (✗ resides in to be reproducible) and never a that-clause.
Pattern
reside in [a city, a state, a district] · reside at [a street address] · reside with [a person, or a body that holds authority] · [a quality / a problem] reside in [something]
Contrast
The three prepositions divide by scale and by kind. You reside in a county, reside at 400 East Sixth Street, and reside with your grandmother — or, for authority, with the body that holds it (final approval resides with the board), which is the same frame as rest with. Against plain live: reside is what the form asks (Where do you currently reside?) and live is what people answer. The abstract sense — the problem resides in the design — is the formal twin of lie in, and is standard in academic prose.

Have your legal home somewhere — or, of a quality or a power, be located in something.

  1. Both parties resided in Cook County at the time of filing.
  2. Sovereignty resides in the people, not in the office.
  3. She resides at 400 East Sixth Street.
  4. Custody resides with the mother until the hearing.
  5. The real difficulty resides in the definition of "employee."
  6. Anyone residing in the district for thirty days may register to vote.
See also

resort to#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈzɔrt tu/ · rih-ZORT too · weak /rɪˈzɔrt tə/
Русский
прибегать к — обращаться к нежелательному средству; после to герундий
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund — to is a preposition here, so a verb after it must be -ing: resorted to lying, never ✗ resorted to lie. Never a clause. Resort has no other verb frame at all: ✗ he resorted the lie.
Pattern
resort to [something] · resort to [verb-ing] · as a last resort · without resorting to [verb-ing]
Contrast
Another member of the look forward to family, and the same test settles it: you can say resort to violence, so to is a preposition, so it is resort to shouting, not ✗ resort to shout. The word carries a built-in judgment — whatever you resort to is a fallback you would rather not have used, which is why resort to force and resort to bribery sound natural and ✗ resorted to a sensible compromise sounds odd. Compare the noun last resort, and the unrelated homonym resort meaning a vacation property.

Turn to something undesirable because nothing better is available.

  1. They resorted to hauling water by hand for most of August.
  2. Nobody wants to resort to layoffs, which is what everyone says before layoffs.
  3. He resorted to checking the spreadsheet line by line.
  4. The department resorted to a hiring freeze rather than cut programs.
  5. You can usually settle it without resorting to lawyers.
  6. As a last resort, we can push the launch to October.

respond to#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈspɑnd tu/ · rih-SPOND too · weak /rɪˈspɑnd tə/
Русский
отвечать на, реагировать на — на вопрос, письмо или ситуацию
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral, a step more formal than answer
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (responded to being passed over by quitting) — never a to-infinitive (✗ respond to be asked). A clause attaches to bare respond with no preposition at all: he responded that the file was closed. The medical and technical sense takes a plain noun: the tumor responded to radiation.
Pattern
respond to [a question, a letter, a crisis] · respond to [verb-ing] · respond with [an action, a counteroffer] · respond by [verb-ing] · respond that [clause]
Contrast
Answer takes no preposition and respond requires one — answer the question but respond to the question; ✗ answer to the question is an error, and answer to with a person means something else entirely (be accountable to). Reply to is the near-twin but is limited to messages, while respond to also covers situations, treatments, and stimuli (responded to the medication, responded to the alarm). React to is involuntary and immediate; respond to implies a considered move.

Say or do something in answer to a question, a message, or a situation.

  1. Nobody responded to my email for four days.
  2. The department responded to the complaint with a two-page denial.
  3. Her blood pressure responded to the lower dose almost immediately.
  4. Three engines responded to the alarm at two in the morning.
  5. He responded to being left off the invitation by showing up anyway.
  6. The company has yet to respond to questions about the recall.

rest on#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛst ɑn/ · REST on · weak /ˈrɛst ən/
Русский
опираться на — держаться на чём-то физически или логически
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral; rest upon is formal and literary
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (the whole case rests on proving intent) — never a to-infinitive (✗ rests on to prove intent). A wh-clause also works: it rests on whether the email was sent.
Pattern
[an argument, a claim, an outcome] rest on [an assumption] · [a physical thing] rest on [a support] · [someone's eyes] rest on [something] · rest [something] on [something]
Variants
rest upon — identical in meaning, more formal and more literary; standard in appellate opinions and in descriptions of a gaze
Contrast
Rest on names what holds something up; rest with names who is responsible. The two never swap: the decision rests with the dean assigns the choice to a person, while the decision rests on the dean would make the dean the supporting evidence. Against depend on: rest on is static support — the argument is built on it — whereas depend on is contingency, varying with what the other thing does. Bare rest in a courtroom (the state rests) is a fixed idiom with no complement, and rest on your laurels is frozen.

Be supported by something, physically or logically.

  1. The entire argument rests on a single unnamed source.
  2. The porch roof rests on four cedar posts.
  3. Their case rests on proving he knew about the transfer.
  4. Much rests upon the wording of the second clause.
  5. Her gaze rested on the photograph a moment too long.
  6. He rested his elbows on the counter and waited for an answer.
See also
rest with · depend on · rely on · stem from · turn on · on · upon

rest with#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɛst wɪθ/ · REST with · weak /ˈrɛst wəθ/
Русский
быть на чьей-то ответственности — решение остаётся за кем-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal — bylaws, contracts, editorials, and committee minutes; in speech Americans say is up to
Complement
noun phrase naming a person or a body that holds the authority. No gerund (✗ the decision rests with deciding), no infinitive, no clause. The subject is the thing being decided — a decision, the final say, responsibility, blame.
Pattern
[a decision / responsibility / blame] rest with [a person or body] · the choice rests with [someone] · ultimately rest with [someone]
Contrast
With assigns responsibility, on assigns support — the decision rests with the board (they decide) against the decision rests on the audit (the audit is what it is built on). Lie with is the near-synonym and a shade more literary (the fault lies with the vendor); be up to is the everyday version (it's up to you); reside with is the same frame in stiffer clothes. ✗ Rest to and ✗ rest in are not this pattern.

Be the responsibility or the decision of a particular person or body.

  1. Final approval rests with the state board, not the county.
  2. The decision rests with her, and always did.
  3. Responsibility for the delay rests with the contractor.
  4. Where the burden of proof rests with the plaintiff, an ambiguity favors the defense.
  5. Whatever the doctors recommend, the choice rests with the family.
  6. Authority to waive the fee rests with the dean's office.
See also
rest on · reside in · vest in · belong to · with

result in, result from#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈzʌlt ɪn/ · rih-ZULT in · weak /rɪˈzʌlt ən/
Русский
in приводить к следствию; from быть следствием причины — направление противоположно
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund — resulted in closing the plant, results from having no backup — never a to-infinitive (✗ resulted in to close the plant). Never a clause. Result is intransitive and takes no object: ✗ result a delay.
Pattern
[a cause] result in [an effect] · [an effect] result from [a cause] · result in [verb-ing] · result from [verb-ing] · as a result of [something]
Contrast
The two prepositions point in opposite directions, and swapping them inverts the sentence. The storm resulted in three outages — cause first. Three outages resulted from the storm — effect first. Both are true; ✗ the storm resulted from three outages is false. Result in is the formal twin of lead to and cause; result from is the formal twin of be caused by and stem from. ✗ result to is not English in either direction.

Produce an outcome, or be produced by a cause.

  1. The audit resulted in four policy changes and one resignation.
  2. Most of the delays resulted from a single misfiled permit.
  3. Cutting the route resulted in stranding about two hundred riders.
  4. The infection resulted from a lapse in sterilization procedure.
  5. Higher rates usually result in fewer refinancings.
  6. Nothing good has ever resulted from sending that email at midnight.
See also
lead to · stem from · contribute to · die from · as a result of

retire from#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈtaɪr frʌm/ · rih-TIRE from · weak /rɪˈtaɪr frəm/
Русский
уходить на пенсию, оставлять (пост, службу, спорт)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase naming a job, a field, or an organization — no gerund, no clause (✗ retire from working; say retire from teaching, where teaching is a noun, or just retire). To names where you go; as names the title you held.
Pattern
retire from [a job, a field, an institution] · retire to [a place] · retire as [a title] · retire (alone) · be retired · retire [a number, a debt, a piece of equipment]
Contrast
Retire of is an error. The three live prepositions divide the sentence: she retired from the district as an assistant principal and moved to Tucson. Note the transitive senses, which take no preposition: a team retires a number, a company retires debt, an airline retires a fleet. And note the older, unrelated sense of retire meaning withdraw for the evening (he retired at ten), which survives mostly in period fiction.

Stop working permanently — and the preposition names what you leave.

  1. He retired from the fire department after twenty-eight years.
  2. She retired from competitive swimming at twenty-three.
  3. Two of the three founders have retired from the board.
  4. They retired to a town with one traffic light and no winter.
  5. He retired as chief of staff and stayed on as a consultant.
  6. The Yankees retired his number the following spring.
See also
escape from · recover from · separate from · from

rob of#

Pronunciation
/ˈrɑb əv/ · ROB uv · weak /ˈrɑb ə/
Русский
лишать кого-то чего-то — отнимать силой или несправедливо
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (robbed of seeing her graduate) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The victim or the place is the direct object and the loss takes of: robbed him of his savings, ✗ robbed his savings from him is clumsy at best.
Pattern
rob [someone / a place] · rob [someone] of [something] · be robbed of [something] · rob [a bank, a store] · steal [something] from [someone] — the mirror verb
Contrast
Rob takes the victim; steal takes the loot. They robbed the bank and they stole the money are both correct, and ✗ they robbed the money and ✗ they stole the bank are both wrong. When you want to name both, rob uses of (robbed the bank of four thousand dollars) and steal uses from (stole four thousand dollars from the bank). The figurative sense is common and always takes of: an injury robbed him of a season, robbed of the chance to say goodbye.

Take something from someone by force or by unfairness.

  1. Two men robbed the branch on Ellis Street at closing time.
  2. The injury robbed him of an entire season.
  3. She was robbed of a promotion by a reorganization nobody explained.
  4. They robbed the till and left the safe alone.
  5. A late frost robbed the orchard of about half its crop.
  6. He felt robbed of the chance to make his case.
See also

root for#

Pronunciation
/ˈrut fɔr/ (also /ˈrʊt/) · ROOT for · weak /ˈrut fər/
Русский
болеть за — открыто желать успеха; разг., амер.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
informal and American — sports, elections, and encouragement; the British equivalent is support
Complement
noun phrase — a team, a person, an outcome. No gerund (✗ rooting for winning), and no that-clause (✗ root that they win). An infinitive is licensed only by the person sitting between the preposition and the verb, exactly as with wait for: rooting for the underdog to win.
Pattern
root for [a team / a person] · root for [someone] to [verb] · be rooting for [someone]
Contrast
Root for is cheering from outside, with nothing at stake but sympathy. Support can mean money or votes as well as good wishes (support the team, support the bill); back puts resources behind something; cheer for is louder and more literal. Keep the separable particles apart from this fixed preposition: root out moves its object (root out corruption, root it out) and root around in means rummage — neither is this verb.

Openly hope that someone or something succeeds.

  1. Half the bar was rooting for the visiting team out of spite.
  2. We're all rooting for you on Thursday.
  3. She was quietly rooting for the amendment to fail.
  4. He roots for whichever team his father hated.
  5. Nobody roots for the insurance company in a movie.
  6. I'm rooting for her to get the fellowship.

run out of#

Pronunciation
/ˈrʌn ˈaʊt əv/ · RUN OWT uv · weak /ˈrʌn aʊt ə/
Русский
у кого-то кончается что-то — израсходовать весь запас
Type
verb + particle + preposition
Register
neutral, everyday
Complement
noun phrase naming a supply — time, money, gas, patience, excuses. No gerund (✗ ran out of trying), no infinitive, no clause. The of is a fixed preposition and its object cannot move: ✗ run the gas out of.
Pattern
run out of [a supply] · be running out of [time] · [a supply] run out — intransitive, no object
Contrast
The particle and the preposition do different jobs. Run out alone is intransitive and needs nothing after it (the milk ran out, the clock ran out); adding of names what was exhausted. Do not confuse run out on someone, which means abandon them (he ran out on his family), or transitive run someone out of town, where out of is literally directional. And be out of is the state where run out of is the event: we're out of coffee describes this morning, we ran out of coffee describes what happened.

Use up the whole supply of something.

  1. We ran out of gas about six miles short of the exit.
  2. The department is running out of money before the fiscal year ends.
  3. She ran out of patience halfway through the second meeting.
  4. They ran out of excuses years ago.
  5. If this appeal fails, he runs out of options.
  6. The store had run out of the small sizes by ten in the morning.

save from#

Pronunciation
/ˈseɪv frʌm/ · SAYV from · weak /ˈseɪv frəm/
Русский
спасать от — а save for откладывать на, save on экономить на
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (saved us from having to reprint it) — never a to-infinitive. The thing saved is a direct object; from names the danger. Other frames take for (save it for later), on (save on shipping), and a bare object (save money, save a seat).
Pattern
save [someone] from [danger / verb-ing] · be saved from [something] · save [something] for [later] · save on [a cost] · save [someone] [trouble] · save up for [something]
Contrast
Save from is rescue; save for is postponement; save on is economy. Three prepositions, three unrelated meanings, and the sentence collapses if you swap them: save the file for Monday and save the file from Monday are not variants of each other. Note the double-object frame that skips the preposition entirely — that saved me an hour, it'll save you a trip — which is the most common form in American speech. Save up for is the money sense with a particle.

Keep someone or something out of danger, or set something aside.

  1. Her seatbelt saved her from far worse.
  2. That template saved us from rewriting the same memo forty times.
  3. Save the good tomatoes for the sandwiches.
  4. You'd save about eleven dollars on shipping by ordering both together.
  5. A neighbor saved the cat from the storm drain and adopted it a week later.
  6. Calling ahead saves you a trip.

search for#

Pronunciation
/ˈsɜrtʃ fɔr/ · SURCH for · weak /ˈsɜrtʃ fɚ/
Русский
искать что-то — а search a place значит обыскивать место
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for takes a noun phrase naming what is missing — no gerund (✗ search for getting a signal). The transitive frame takes the place or person examined, with no preposition: search the house, search him. Both can appear together: search the house for weapons.
Pattern
search for [something missing] · search [a place, a person] · search [a place] for [something] · search through [a container] · in search of [something]
Contrast
You search a place, and you search for a thing. They searched the car means they went through it; they searched for the car means they couldn't find it. Both are correct and they describe opposite situations, which makes this one of the most consequential preposition contrasts in the whole dictionary — it is the difference between a police report and a missing-persons report. Look for is the everyday synonym of search for; search through emphasizes rummaging.

Try to find something, or examine a place thoroughly.

  1. Volunteers searched for her along the river for three days.
  2. Customs searched every bag on the flight.
  3. Deputies searched the property for the missing equipment.
  4. He spent an hour searching for a file that was open in another window.
  5. She searched through the box and came up with two photographs.
  6. They set out in search of somewhere with parking.
See also
look for · ask for · hope for · wait for · hide from

sentence to#

Pronunciation
/ˈsɛntəns tu/ · SEN-tunss too · weak /ˈsɛntəns tə/
Русский
приговаривать к — назначать наказание осуждённому; юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
legal, and the standard register of court reporting; the passive (was sentenced to) is far commoner than the active
Complement
the person sentenced sits between the verb and to, and to takes a noun phrase naming the punishment — a term, a fine, probation, death. No gerund (✗ sentenced to serving time). A to-infinitive does appear, but only in the courtroom formula sentenced to serve 36 months, sentenced to pay restitution, where to serve is the infinitive marker and not this preposition. The test settles it: sentenced to five years takes a plain noun, so that to is the preposition.
Pattern
sentence [someone] to [a term / a fine / death] · [someone] be sentenced to [noun phrase] · be sentenced to [a term] for [the offense] · sentence [someone] to [verb — courtroom formula]
Contrast
The offense takes for and the punishment takes to, and they never swap: sentenced to three years for wire fraud. Around it stand the other stages, each with its own preposition: charge someone with an offense, convict someone of it, sentence them to a term, fine them (no preposition at all). Condemn to is now figurative or historical (condemned to death, condemned to a life of paperwork) where sentence to is the live legal verb.

Impose a punishment on someone who has been convicted.

  1. He was sentenced to eighteen months in federal prison.
  2. The judge sentenced her to probation and two hundred hours of community service.
  3. Three defendants were sentenced to time served and released that afternoon.
  4. She was sentenced to five years for the fraud and two more for obstruction.
  5. The court sentenced him to pay $40,000 in restitution.
  6. Nobody in the state has been sentenced to death since 2011.
See also
charge with · accuse of · punish for · testify to · sue for · to

separate from#

Pronunciation
/ˈsɛpəˌreɪt frʌm/ · SEP-uh-rayt from · weak /ˈsɛpəˌreɪt frəm/
Русский
отделять(ся) от — разъединять или держать порознь
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause. Into takes the resulting parts (separate into groups), and the intransitive separate takes no complement at all (the couple separated). Note the pronunciation split: the verb ends /eɪt/, the adjective /ət/.
Pattern
separate [A] from [B] · be separated from [something] · separate into [parts] · separate (intransitive) · separation from [something]
Contrast
Separate from divides two things; separate into divides one thing up. Separate the yolks from the whites against separate the class into four groups — different structures, and using from for the second (✗ separate the class from four groups) is nonsense. Compare divide into, which behaves like separate into, and distinguish between / distinguish from, which is about telling apart rather than pulling apart. The figurative use is common in American business prose: what separates a good manager from a bad one.

Move or keep two things apart.

  1. Separate the yolks from the whites while the eggs are cold.
  2. A chain-link fence separates the playground from the parking lot.
  3. What separates this camera from the cheaper one is the sensor.
  4. He was separated from his unit for two days.
  5. Separate the invoices into paid and unpaid.
  6. They separated in April and reconciled by August.

share with#

Pronunciation
/ˈʃɛr wɪθ/ · SHAIR with · weak /ˈʃɛr wəθ/
Русский
делиться с — отдавать часть своего или иметь что-то общее
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
with takes the person you share with; the thing shared is a direct object: share the file with the team. A gerund can be the thing shared (share being on call), but no to-infinitive and no clause. In takes something you partake of: share in the profits.
Pattern
share [something] with [someone] · share [something] · share in [profits, credit, blame] · share [a trait] with [someone] · have [something] in common with [someone]
Contrast
Share with gives a portion away; share in takes a portion of something already collective. She shared her notes with me (I got a copy) against she shared in the credit (she got a piece of it). ✗ share to someone is an error, though it has spread through social-media interfaces. Note the third sense, hold in common: the two languages share a root, which takes a direct object or with, never in.

Give someone part of what you have, or have something in common.

  1. She shared her notes with anyone who asked.
  2. Would you share that with the rest of us?
  3. The two houses share a driveway with no written agreement of any kind.
  4. Junior partners share in the profits but not the losses.
  5. He shares a birthday with his grandfather.
  6. They shared one bathroom with six other tenants.

sign up for#

Pronunciation
/ˌsaɪn ˈʌp fɔr/ · syne-UP for · weak /saɪn ˈʌp fər/
Русский
записываться на — регистрироваться или подписываться на что-то
Type
verb + particle + preposition
Register
neutral to informal — everyday American English for classes, shifts, plans, and mailing lists
Complement
for takes a noun phrase — a class, a service, a shift, a plan. A gerund is unusual and marked (?signed up for cleaning the garage); to put a verb after sign up, drop for and use the infinitive: signed up to volunteer. No that-clause.
Pattern
sign up for [a class, a service, a shift] · sign up to [verb — infinitive] · sign [someone] up for [something] · sign up with [an organization]
Contrast
The particle moves and the preposition does not. Up slides around an object — sign me up, signed the whole family up — while for never leaves its noun: ✗ sign up the class for. Then the prepositions divide the meanings: sign up for is enrolling in something, sign up with is joining an outfit (signed up with a new agency), sign for is signing a receipt (sign for the package), and sign on to is endorsing (signed on to the letter). The idiom I didn't sign up for this means this is not what I agreed to.

Enroll in something, or put your name down for it.

  1. Forty people signed up for the Saturday session in the first hour.
  2. I signed up for the two-year plan and regretted it by March.
  3. Sign your kids up for swim lessons before the list fills.
  4. She signed up to work the overnight shift on Christmas.
  5. He signed up for a gym membership he has never once used.
  6. Long hours are one thing, but nobody signed up for this.

smile at#

Pronunciation
/ˈsmaɪl æt/ · SMILE at · weak /ˈsmaɪl ət/
Русский
улыбаться кому-то — направлять улыбку на кого-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (smiled at being remembered) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. Smile is intransitive and the target always needs a preposition: ✗ smile me. A rare transitive survives in smile a thin smile.
Pattern
smile at [someone / something] · smile about [something] · smile on [someone] — favor · smile (alone) · a smile for [someone]
Contrast
At aims the smile; about names the cause; on is fortune. She smiled at the baby directs it; she smiled about the whole thing later explains it; fortune smiled on them is a fixed literary idiom meaning they got lucky. ✗ smile to someone is not idiomatic American English. Compare laugh at, which is usually hostile, with smile at, which almost never is — the same preposition, opposite social temperature.

Turn a smile toward someone or something.

  1. He smiled at everyone in line and meant it, which was unsettling.
  2. She smiled at the photograph for a long moment before putting it back.
  3. Smile at the camera, not at me.
  4. They smiled about it for weeks afterward.
  5. The weather smiled on the whole weekend for once.
  6. He smiled at being recognized after all those years.

specialize in#

Pronunciation
/ˈspɛʃəˌlaɪz ɪn/ · SPESH-uh-lize in · weak /ˈspɛʃəˌlaɪz ən/
Русский

verb + preposition
Register

Complement
noun phrase or gerund (specializes in restoring old plaster) — never a to-infinitive (✗ specializes in to restore plaster). Specialize is intransitive: ✗ specialize immigration law. American spelling is -ize; specialise is British.
Pattern
specialize in [a field / verb-ing] · be specialized in — avoid; use the active · a specialist in [a field] · specialize (alone)
Contrast
Specialize on and ✗ specialize at are errors — the preposition is fixed at in, and so is the noun's: a specialist in labor law. Note that the passive is specialized in sounds translated; American writers use the active.

Concentrate your work or study on one narrow field.

  1. The firm specializes in municipal bond work.
  2. She specializes in restoring plaster ceilings, which is a smaller field than you'd think.

stand up for#

Pronunciation
/ˌstænd ˈʌp fɔr/ · stand-UP for · weak /stænd ˈʌp fər/
Русский
заступаться за, отстаивать — публично защищать человека или принцип
Type
verb + particle + preposition
Register
neutral and warm; common in speech and in political writing
Complement
noun phrase — a person, a group, a principle — or a reflexive (stand up for yourself). A gerund is awkward here (?stood up for keeping the library open; say stood up for the library), and there is no infinitive (✗ stand up for to defend her).
Pattern
stand up for [someone / a principle] · stand up for [oneself] · stand up for [what is right]
Contrast
Three stand patterns, three meanings. Stand up for is defending someone; stand up to is confronting the stronger party (stood up to her boss); stand for is representing (What does the acronym stand for?) or, in the negative, tolerating (I won't stand for it). The particle does not move around the object here — ✗ stand him up for — and stand someone up means failing to show for a date, which is a separable particle and a different verb entirely. The colloquial twin is stick up for; plain defend takes no preposition.

Publicly defend a person or a principle under attack.

  1. Nobody stood up for the new hire when the manager went after her.
  2. She stood up for herself in a room where that cost something.
  3. The union stood up for the night crew all the way through arbitration.
  4. He talks a great deal about standing up for the little guy.
  5. If you won't stand up for your own numbers, leave them out of the deck.
  6. Two council members stood up for the ordinance and the rest went quiet.

stare at#

Pronunciation
/ˈstɛr æt/ · STAIR at · weak /ˈstɛr ət/
Русский
пристально смотреть на — глядеть подолгу, не отрывая взгляда
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause. Stare is intransitive and always needs at before its target: ✗ stare the screen. The only bare use is absolute: don't stare.
Pattern
stare at [someone / something] · stare (alone) · stare into [space, the distance] · stare down [someone] · be staring [someone] in the face
Contrast
Stare at is fixed — ✗ stare to and ✗ stare on are errors. The family sorts by intensity and politeness: look at is neutral, watch is transitive with no preposition (watch the game), stare at is rude, glare at is hostile, gaze at is fond or dreamy, and glance at is brief. Only watch drops the preposition, which is why ✗ watch at the game and ✗ stare the game are both wrong.

Look at something fixedly for a long time.

  1. He stared at the ceiling until about four in the morning.
  2. Two people on the platform were openly staring at her.
  3. She stared at the invoice as though it might revise itself.
  4. Don't stare.
  5. He stared into the middle distance and said he'd think about it.
  6. The answer had been staring us in the face for a week.

stem from#

Pronunciation
/ˈstɛm frʌm/ · STEM from · weak /ˈstɛm frəm/
Русский
происходить из, восходить к — иметь своим источником
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal — common in journalism and analysis
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (stems from having no oversight) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. Stem in this sense is intransitive and never takes an object: ✗ stem the problem is a different verb entirely, meaning stop the flow.
Pattern
[a problem, a belief, a case] stem from [a cause] · stem from [verb-ing] · stem [the tide, the bleeding, the flow] — a different verb, no preposition
Contrast
Stem from points from effect back to cause, exactly like result from and arise from, and the opposite of result in and lead to. ✗ stem to does not exist. Watch the homonym: stem the losses means stanch them, takes a direct object, and has nothing to do with this pattern — the charges stem from the losses and the charges stemmed the losses are unrelated sentences. Journalists lean on stem from heavily because it asserts a cause without asserting blame.

Have its origin in something.

  1. The charges stem from a 2019 procurement contract.
  2. Most of the friction stems from a job description nobody has updated since 2011.
  3. Her interest in soils stemmed from a summer on a research plot.
  4. The delays stem from having only one inspector for the whole county.
  5. The dispute stems from a boundary survey done in 1948.
  6. Almost every complaint stems from the same two forms.

stick to#

Pronunciation
/ˈstɪk tu/ · STIK too · weak /ˈstɪk tə/
Русский
придерживаться чего-то — не отступать от; разг. вариант adhere to
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral, and very common in speech
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (stick to telling the truth) — never a to-infinitive (✗ stick to tell the truth) and never a that-clause. The literal sense takes a surface (the label won't stick to the jar).
Pattern
stick to [a plan, a story, a diet, the facts] · stick to [verb-ing] · [something] stick to [a surface] · stick with [a plan / a person]
Contrast
Stick to and stick with overlap on plans and diverge everywhere else. Both work in stick to the plan / stick with the plan, but only stick with takes a person you stay alongside (stick with me), and only stick to takes a physical surface. Stick at it is British where Americans say stick with it. Keep the separable particles out of this entry: stick around (wait), stick out (protrude), stick it out (endure), stick up for (defend) — their objects move, this to never does.

Keep to something and not depart from it.

  1. Stick to the script and we'll be out of there in ten minutes.
  2. He stuck to his story through three separate interviews.
  3. She's sticking to decaf until the baby comes.
  4. If we stick to replacing one system a quarter, it stays affordable.
  5. The batter won't stick to a pan that hot.
  6. I'd stick to what you actually know in front of that committee.

stipulate to#

Pronunciation
/ˈstɪpjəˌleɪt tu/ · STIP-yuh-layt too · weak /ˈstɪpjəˌleɪt tə/
Русский
признавать факт по соглашению сторон — юр., в процессе
Type
verb + preposition
Register
legal — an American meets stipulate to in a courtroom, a deposition transcript, or a settlement agreement; outside law the verb is used without to (the lease stipulates a thirty-day notice)
Complement
noun phrase naming a fact, an exhibit, or an element (stipulated to the authenticity of the recording), or a gerund (stipulated to entering the records without a custodian) — never a to-infinitive (✗ stipulated to admit the exhibit). For a full proposition, drop the preposition: stipulate that the report was authentic.
Pattern
stipulate to [a fact / an exhibit / an element] · stipulate to [verb-ing] · stipulate that [clause — no preposition] · stipulate [a condition] · enter into a stipulation as to [something]
Contrast
The two senses take opposite kinds of act. Plain transitive stipulate means require as a condition — the grant stipulates that funds be spent within a year. With to, it means agreeing with the other side that something need not be proved at all: the defense stipulated to the chain of custody, and that issue leaves the trial. Neighbors: concede and admit to give away a point in argument, agree to accepts a proposal, consent to authorizes — none of them has the technical effect of removing an element from proof.

Agree formally, on the record, that a fact may be taken as established.

  1. Both sides stipulated to the authenticity of the recordings.
  2. The defense would not stipulate to the value of the stolen property.
  3. Counsel, will you stipulate to the witness's qualifications?
  4. They stipulated to liability and tried the damages question alone.
  5. The parties stipulated to entering the medical records without a custodian.
  6. If we stipulate to the timeline, the hearing takes an afternoon instead of a week.
See also
agree to · admit to · testify to · submit to · to

submit to#

Pronunciation
/səbˈmɪt tu/ · sub-MIT too · weak /səbˈmɪt tə/
Русский
подчиняться чему-то, подвергаться; подавать что-то куда-то — два разных глагола
Type
verb + preposition
Register
formal in the yielding sense; the hand-it-in sense is everyday office and academic English
Complement
in the yielding sense, to takes a noun phrase or a gerund (submitted to being searched) — never a to-infinitive (✗ submit to be searched). In the hand-it-in sense the thing submitted is the direct object and to names the recipient, always a noun phrase: submit the form to HR.
Pattern
submit to [authority, a test, an examination] · submit to [verb-ing] · submit [something] to [someone] · be submitted to [a committee] · submit that [clause]
Contrast
Two verbs share one spelling, and the direct object tells them apart. He submitted to a drug test is undergoing something; he submitted the form to HR is handing it in — only the second has an object before to. Among the yielding verbs: surrender to is giving up in a contest or to the police, yield to is giving way to traffic or pressure, defer to is bowing to someone's judgment while keeping your own position. And the lawyer's we submit that the statute is clear takes a clause and no preposition at all.

Yield to something, or hand something in to someone.

  1. He refused to submit to a search without a warrant.
  2. All bids must be submitted to the purchasing office by Friday.
  3. She submitted to two rounds of interviews before anyone mentioned salary.
  4. The paper was submitted to three journals and rejected by all three.
  5. They would rather close than submit to being audited every quarter.
  6. Members submit to the ethics rules when they join.
See also
subscribe to · stipulate to · comply with · object to · to

subscribe to#

Pronunciation
/səbˈskraɪb tu/ · sub-SKRIBE too · weak /səbˈskraɪb tə/
Русский
подписываться на; разделять (взгляд, мнение)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause (✗ subscribe to getting the paper). Both senses share the frame: a publication or service, and a view or doctrine.
Pattern
subscribe to [a publication, a service] · subscribe to [a view, a theory] · subscribe [an amount] — formal, rare · a subscription to [something]
Contrast
Subscribe of and ✗ subscribe for are errors in ordinary use (subscribe for shares survives only in securities language). The second sense is the useful one for writers: I don't subscribe to that theory means I don't accept it, and it is a shade more formal and more distancing than I don't believe it. The noun keeps to: a subscription to the Times. Compare sign up for, the everyday American phrasal equivalent of the service sense.

Pay to receive something regularly, or accept a view.

  1. They still subscribe to the print edition on Sundays.
  2. I don't subscribe to the theory that everything was better in 1994.
  3. The lab subscribes to three databases nobody uses.
  4. She subscribes to the view that meetings should have an agenda or a cancellation.
  5. He subscribed to four streaming services and watched one.
  6. A subscription to the journal runs about ninety dollars a year.
See also
belong to · agree with · believe in · refer to · apply for

substitute for#

Pronunciation
/ˈsʌbstəˌtut fɔr/ · SUB-stuh-toot for · weak /ˈsʌbstəˌtut fɚ/
Русский
заменять собой — ставить одно вместо другого
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase only — no gerund, no clause. The new item is the direct object and the old one takes for: substitute margarine for butter means use margarine instead of butter. The intransitive frame reverses the feel: substitute for someone means fill in for them.
Pattern
substitute [new] for [old] · substitute for [someone] — fill in · be substituted for [something] · a substitute for [something] · replace [old] with [new] — the mirror verb
Contrast
This is the most commonly reversed pattern in the dictionary. Substitute X for Y puts X in and takes Y out. Replace X with Y takes X out and puts Y in. So substitute honey for sugar and replace sugar with honey describe the same recipe, and getting either backwards inverts it. Because so many people reverse it, substitute X with Y has spread in speech; it is still widely criticized in edited American prose, and the safe move is to use replace ... with whenever you want the with-frame.

Put one thing in the place of another.

  1. Substitute buttermilk for the whole milk and add a little baking soda.
  2. You can substitute almond flour for about a third of the wheat flour.
  3. She substituted for the regular teacher three days a week.
  4. There's no substitute for actually reading the contract.
  5. The committee substituted a milder resolution for the one on the floor.
  6. Replace the gasket with the one in the kit — same job, opposite wording.
See also
prefer to · compare with · provide with · separate from

succeed in#

Pronunciation
/səkˈsid ɪn/ · suk-SEED in · weak /səkˈsid ən/
Русский
суметь, добиться — после in герундий; а succeed smb сменить на посту
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
gerund or noun phrase — succeeded in raising the money — never a to-infinitive (✗ succeeded to raise the money, which is a different and mostly obsolete pattern about inheriting a throne). Never a clause.
Pattern
succeed in [verb-ing] · succeed in [a field, an effort] · succeed [someone] — follow in office, no preposition · succeed to [a title, a throne] — formal, archaic · succeed (alone)
Contrast
Succeed to do something is wrong. The verb has no infinitive frame: use succeed in doing, or switch to manage to do, which is the ordinary American way of saying it. The bare transitive is a different verb altogether — she succeeded him as chair means she took over the job — and the archaic succeed to the throne is the only surviving to pattern. Compare fail, which behaves in the opposite way and takes the infinitive: failed to raise the money.

Manage to do something, or take over someone's position.

  1. They succeeded in raising almost twice the target.
  2. He never quite succeeded in explaining why the numbers changed.
  3. Two of the eight succeeded in passing on the first attempt.
  4. The campaign succeeded in registering four thousand new voters.
  5. She succeeded him as chair in the spring.
  6. Nobody succeeds in that job without a very good deputy.
See also

sue for#

Pronunciation
/ˈsu fɔr/ · SOO for · weak /ˈsu fər/
Русский
подавать в суд, требуя чего-то — for называет иск или сумму; юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral, legal-adjacent; sue for peace is a frozen diplomatic idiom
Complement
noun phrase naming either the relief sought (sue for damages, sue for $2 million, sue for divorce) or the ground (sue for breach of contract, sue for negligence). A gerund works for the ground (sued the paper for printing the photo) but never for the relief, and there is no to-infinitive (✗ sued for to recover the deposit).
Pattern
sue [someone] for [damages / an amount] · sue [someone] for [a wrong] · sue for [verb-ing] · sue over [an episode] · sue [someone] in [a court]
Contrast
For does double duty and only context separates the two jobs: sued for defamation names the claim, sued for $3 million names the money. Sue over names the episode that prompted the suit (sued over the layoffs) and is the journalist's preposition. Around it: a prosecutor charges someone with a crime, a private party sues, and prosecute takes no preposition. Sue for peace is fixed — an army sues for peace, and nobody sues for a truce.

Bring a lawsuit seeking something, or on a particular ground.

  1. The contractor sued for the balance of the contract plus interest.
  2. She sued the hospital for negligence and settled a year later.
  3. Two shareholders are suing for $4 million in damages.
  4. He sued the magazine for running the photograph without permission.
  5. They filed to sue for divorce in a state neither had lived in.
  6. Cut off from resupply, the garrison sued for peace.
See also
charge with · accuse of · sentence to · testify to · apply for · for

suffer from#

Pronunciation
/ˈsʌfɚ frʌm/ · SUFF-er from · weak /ˈsʌfɚ frəm/
Русский
страдать от — быть подверженным болезни, недостатку или тяготе
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (suffers from having no natural light) — never a to-infinitive, never a clause. The transitive frame takes a single event with no preposition: suffer a stroke, suffer a defeat — and from would be wrong there.
Pattern
[someone] suffer from [a chronic condition] · [something] suffer from [a flaw] · suffer [an injury, a loss, a defeat] · suffer (alone) · suffer for [one's art]
Contrast
Suffer from is ongoing; bare suffer is a single event. He suffers from migraines is a condition he lives with; he suffered a concussion happened once, and ✗ suffered from a concussion on Tuesday is odd. The pattern extends past medicine: the argument suffers from a missing premise, the neighborhood suffers from decades of disinvestment. Note that careful American writing increasingly avoids suffers from for disabilities in favor of has, on the grounds that suffer editorializes.

Be affected by an illness, a flaw, or a hardship.

  1. About one adult in eight suffers from some form of chronic pain.
  2. The building suffers from a roof that was never right to begin with.
  3. The report suffers from relying on a single source.
  4. He suffered a stroke in March and was back at the shop by June.
  5. Small towns along that corridor have suffered from forty years of decline.
  6. The whole argument suffers from not defining its terms.
See also
recover from · benefit from · die of · cope with · complain about

suspect of#

Pronunciation
/səˈspɛkt əv/ · suh-SPEKT uv · weak /səˈspɛkt ə/
Русский
подозревать в — считать виновным без доказательств
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (suspected him of skimming) — never a to-infinitive after of (✗ suspected him of to skim) and never a that-clause after the preposition. The active ✗ suspected him to be skimming is unidiomatic too; the only live infinitive frame is the formal passive the fire is suspected to have been deliberate, and even there suspected of being is the commoner form. Suspect does take a that-clause with no preposition: I suspect that she already knew. Note the stress shift: the verb is /səˈspɛkt/, the noun /ˈsʌspɛkt/.
Pattern
suspect [someone] of [something / verb-ing] · be suspected of [something] · suspect that [clause] · suspect [something] · a suspect in [a case]
Contrast
Suspect of is the private version of accuse of. Suspicion is held; accusation is voiced; a charge is filed — suspected of, accused of, charged with, and the preposition changes only at the last step. ✗ suspect someone for something is an error. Note the noun frame, which uses a different preposition again: a suspect in the robbery, never ✗ a suspect of the robbery.

Believe, without proof, that someone has done something.

  1. Investigators suspected him of moving money through the parts business.
  2. She was suspected of leaking the schedule and never asked about it directly.
  3. I suspect the thermostat, not the furnace.
  4. Two employees were suspected of falsifying the counts.
  5. He suspected that the offer would be withdrawn by Monday.
  6. Nobody suspected the quietest person in the office of anything.
