take care of#

Pronunciation
/ˈteɪk ˈkɛr əv/ · tayk KAIR uv · weak /ˈteɪk ˈkɛr ə/
Русский
заботиться о; заниматься (делом) — после of герундий
Type
verb + noun + preposition (fixed idiom)
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (take care of getting it notarized) — never a to-infinitive after of (✗ take care of to get it notarized). A separate frame, take care to do something, uses the infinitive and means be careful to: take care to date it. The two are not interchangeable.
Pattern
take care of [someone] · take care of [a task / verb-ing] · take care to [verb] · take care (farewell) · be taken care of
Contrast
Take care of it handles it; take care to do it is cautious about it. Take care of the paperwork means do it; take care to sign the paperwork means don't forget the signature. The idiom covers both people and jobs, which care for does not — care for is only about people and only about tending. Note the euphemism, alive and well in American crime fiction: we'll take care of him. And Take care on its own is a goodbye.

Look after someone, or deal with a task.

  1. Her neighbor takes care of the plants whenever she travels.
  2. I'll take care of the deposit on my way in.
  3. Somebody needs to take care of getting the permit amended.
  4. He took care of his father for the last three years of his life.
  5. The warranty takes care of everything except the labor.
  6. Take care to keep the original; they'll ask for it.

take precedence over#

Pronunciation
/ˌteɪk ˈprɛsɪdəns ˈoʊvər/ · tayk PRESS-ih-dunss OH-ver · weak /ˌteɪk ˈprɛsɪdns ˌoʊvɚ/
Русский
иметь приоритет перед — стоять выше при столкновении интересов
Type
verb + noun + preposition — a fixed idiom rather than a simple verb + preposition
Register
formal — statutes, contracts, policies, and workplace prose
Complement
over takes a noun phrase or a gerund (safety takes precedence over meeting the deadline) — never a to-infinitive (✗ takes precedence over to meet the deadline) and never a clause. The noun is frozen: no article (✗ take a precedence over), no plural, and no modifier beyond clear or absolute. The complement can be dropped when the other claim is understood: the more specific provision takes precedence.
Pattern
[X] take precedence over [Y] · [X] take precedence · take absolute precedence over [Y] · in order of precedence
Contrast
Over is the only preposition here — ✗ take precedence to, and take precedence of is archaic British. The idiom ranks two real claims: the earlier lien takes precedence over the later one, and both exist. Its neighbors do other things: supersede replaces one instrument with another and takes no preposition (this agreement supersedes all prior agreements), override cancels (the federal rule overrides the state rule), outweigh compares weight rather than rank. Note the spelling trap: precedence is rank, precedent is a prior case — you set a precedent but take precedence.

Rank ahead of something else when the two conflict.

  1. Patient safety takes precedence over the schedule, every time.
  2. A federal statute takes precedence over a conflicting state rule.
  3. In this house, homework takes precedence over screens.
  4. The earlier filing takes precedence over the amended one.
  5. Nothing takes precedence over getting the water back on.
  6. Where two provisions conflict, the more specific one takes precedence.
See also
comply with · rest with · over · subject to

talk to, talk with, talk about#

Pronunciation
/ˈtɔk tu/ · TAWK too · weak /ˈtɔk tə/
Русский
говорить — to/with с кем-то (with на равных), about о чём-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
to and with take a person. about takes a noun phrase, a gerund, or a wh-clause (talked about moving, talked about where to live). None takes a to-infinitive. Talk is intransitive in all of these — the exceptions are the fixed transitive uses talk sense, talk shop, talk business, and talk someone into / out of something.
Pattern
talk to [someone] · talk with [someone] · talk about [a topic] · talk to [someone] about [a topic] · talk of [something] — literary · talk over [a decision] · talk [someone] into / out of [verb-ing]
Contrast
Talk with is far more common in American English than in British English, and Americans reach for it when the exchange is mutual — I talked with the family for an hour implies both sides spoke. Talk to is the default and can tilt one-directional, which is why I'm going to talk to him often means a reprimand is coming and I'm going to talk with him rarely does. Neither is wrong; the difference is tone. Talk about names the subject, talk over means discuss thoroughly to a decision, and the literary talk of is now mostly a fixed phrase (the talk of the town). Note that ✗ discuss about is an error — discuss takes a direct object.

Speak with someone, or speak about something.

PatternWhat it meansNote
talk to [person]speak to them — the defaultCan imply a one-way talk or a reprimand
talk with [person]speak with them, mutuallyCommon and neutral in American English
talk about [topic]discuss the subjecttalk about the budget
talk over [thing]discuss it through to a decisionlet's talk it over
talk of [thing]literary or fixedthe talk of the town
talk [person] into / out of [-ing]persuade / dissuadetalked her into staying
discuss [topic]no preposition — ✗ discuss aboutdiscuss the budget
  1. I need to talk to you about Thursday.
  2. She talked with each family individually before the hearing.
  3. They talked about moving for two years and then did it in a weekend.
  4. He talked me out of buying it, which he was right about.
  5. Let's talk over the numbers before we send anything.
  6. Nobody wants to talk about the parking, and the parking is the whole problem.
  7. Talk to your doctor before you change the dose.
See also
discuss · argue with · complain to · explain to · think about · hear from

testify to#

Pronunciation
/ˈtɛstəˌfaɪ tu/ · TESS-tuh-fye too · weak /ˈtɛstəˌfaɪ tə/
Русский
давать показания о; служить свидетельством чего-то; юр.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
legal in the literal sense — a deposition, a hearing, a trial; the figurative sense (the ruins testify to a wealthy past) belongs to formal written English
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (testified to having seen him leave), or a wh-clause (testifies to how much has changed) — never a to-infinitive (✗ testified to have seen him). A full proposition drops the preposition: testified that he had seen him leave.
Pattern
testify to [a fact / a quality] · testify to [verb-ing] · testify that [clause] · testify against / for [a party] · testify about [a topic] · testify before [a body] · testify in [a proceeding]
Contrast
Each preposition takes a different piece of the courtroom. To takes the fact you vouch for, about the subject matter, against and for the side, before the body you appear in front of, in the case: she testified before the committee, for the government, about the contracts, to the authenticity of the ledgers. Among the vouching verbs, attest to is the near-twin in formal writing and on signed documents, vouch for is the personal and spoken version, swear to stresses the oath, and bear witness to is the literary one.

State something formally as a witness — or, of a thing, be evidence of something.

  1. Two employees testified to the accuracy of the time sheets.
  2. He testified to having been in the building that night.
  3. The condition of the roof testifies to years of deferred maintenance.
  4. She testified before the committee for two hours without notes.
  5. Neighbors testified to the family's character at sentencing.
  6. The turnout testifies to how badly people wanted this.
See also
vouch for · stipulate to · sentence to · recuse from · to

thank for#

Pronunciation
/ˈθæŋk fɔr/ · THANK for · weak /ˈθæŋk fɚ/
Русский
благодарить за — человек без предлога: thank you for the ride
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase or gerund (thanked her for coming) — never a to-infinitive (✗ thank you for to come). Never a clause. The person thanked is a direct object and never takes a preposition: ✗ thank to you.
Pattern
thank [someone] for [something / verb-ing] · Thank you for [verb-ing] · thanks to [someone] — because of · be thankful for [something] · no thanks to [someone]
Contrast
Thanks to is not the plural of thank for. Thank you for the ride is gratitude; Thanks to the ride, I made it means because of it — and thanks to is often sarcastic (thanks to him, we started forty minutes late). ✗ Thank to you is an error in every sense. Note that thank, like forgive and excuse, takes the person directly, while apologize needs to — a distinction that catches learners of every background.

Express gratitude for something.

  1. Thank you for covering my shift.
  2. She thanked every volunteer for showing up in that weather.
  3. Thanks for the ride — I owe you one.
  4. He never thanked anyone for anything, which people noticed.
  5. Thanks to a rescheduled flight, we got an extra day.
  6. I'm thankful for a landlord who answers the phone.

think about, think of#

Pronunciation
/ˈθɪŋk əˌbaʊt/ · THINK uh-bowt · weak /ˈθɪŋk əbət/
Русский
думать — about обдумывать, of вспоминать или приходить на ум
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
Both take a noun phrase, a gerund (thinking about moving, thought of selling), or a wh-clause. Neither takes a to-infinitive (✗ think about to move) and neither takes a that-clause — for a clause, drop the preposition: think that it's a mistake. Bare think also takes an adjective complement in fixed frames: think it unwise.
Pattern
think about [something / verb-ing] · think of [something / verb-ing] · think of [a name, an idea] — recall or invent · think [something] of [someone] — hold an opinion · think that [clause] · think it over · think highly of [someone]
Contrast
Think about is the process; think of is the flash. I've been thinking about it all week is deliberation; I can't think of her name is retrieval; who thought of that? is invention. They overlap freely for plans (thinking about / of moving), but they part ways completely in the opinion frames: What do you think of it? asks for a verdict, and think highly of, think little of, and think the world of take only of. ✗ Think to do something is not English except in the narrow he never thought to ask, meaning it didn't occur to him.

Consider something, or have something come to mind.

PatternWhat it meansExample

What do you think of it?asking for a verdictWhat did you think of the film?
think highly / little ofhold an opinion of someoneShe thinks highly of him.
think that [clause]state a belief — no prepositionI think it's a mistake.
think to doonly: it didn't occur to himNobody thought to ask.
  1. I've been thinking about the offer all week.
  2. I can't think of her married name to save my life.
  3. Who thought of putting the printer in the stairwell?
  4. What did you think of the second half?
  5. They're thinking about selling and moving closer to the grandkids.
  6. She thinks highly of him, which is more than he deserves.
  7. He never thought to check whether the office was open.

touch on#

Pronunciation
/ˈtʌtʃ ɑn/ · TUCH on · weak /ˈtʌtʃ ən/
Русский
затрагивать (тему) — упоминать вскользь
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral; touch upon is formal and academic
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (touched on selling the building), or a wh-clause (touched on why the numbers moved) — never a to-infinitive (✗ touched on to sell the building).
Pattern
touch on [a topic] · touch briefly on [a topic] · touch on [verb-ing] · touch on [wh-clause]
Variants
touch upon — identical in meaning, more formal; standard in academic and legal prose
Contrast
This on is fixed, not a movable particle: ✗ touch the subject on. And plain transitive touch is physical and takes no preposition, so he touched the subject and he touched on the subject are different sentences entirely. What varies among the neighbors is depth: touch on is a passing mention, get into and go into are thorough, cover is complete, dwell on stays too long. In a second sense touch on means come close to a quality (a politeness touching on contempt), where the neighbor is verge on.

Mention a subject briefly in passing.

  1. The report touches on staffing but never gives a number.
  2. She touched on the lawsuit for about thirty seconds and moved on.
  3. His remarks touched upon the constitutional question without resolving it.
  4. Nobody at the meeting touched on what happens if the grant lapses.
  5. The chapter touches on being raised by grandparents.
  6. Her politeness touched on the theatrical.

translate into#

Pronunciation
/trænzˈleɪt ˈɪntu/ · tranz-LATE in-too · weak /trænzˈleɪt ˈɪntə/
Русский
переводить на (язык); выливаться во что-то — предлог into, не ✗ to
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase naming the target language or result — no gerund, no clause. The source takes from: translated from the Portuguese. The figurative sense takes the same into and a noun phrase: translates into savings.
Pattern
translate [something] into [a language] · translate [something] from [a language] into [another] · [something] translate into [a result] · translate as [a rendering] · be translated by [a translator]
Contrast
Into names the target, from the source, and neither can be swapped for to: ✗ translated to Spanish is an error in careful writing, though it is heard. Note the very common figurative use in American business and sports writing — higher volume translates into lower margins, speed doesn't always translate into wins — which is a full-fledged sense of the verb, not a metaphor anyone notices anymore. Translate as introduces the rendering itself: the phrase translates roughly as "leave it alone."

Put something into another language, or turn into another form.

  1. The book was translated into eleven languages before it sold well in English.
  2. She translates technical manuals from German into English.
  3. None of that translates into actual policy.
  4. The idiom translates roughly as "don't borrow trouble."
  5. A four percent bump in traffic translated into almost nothing on the bottom line.
  6. His stage presence never quite translated into film.

turn on#

Pronunciation
/ˈtɜrn ɑn/ · TURN on · weak /ˈtɜrn ən/
Русский
зависеть от (решающего обстоятельства); наброситься на кого-то
Type
verb + preposition — in the two senses below only
Register
the turn on a question sense is formal and analytic, at home in legal and editorial writing; the turn on someone sense is neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (it all turns on proving intent), or a wh-clause (it turns on whether the email was sent) — never a to-infinitive (✗ turns on to prove intent).
Pattern
[a case, an argument, an outcome] turn on [a fact or a question] · [a person or animal] turn on [someone] · turn on [verb-ing] · turn on [wh-clause]
Contrast
Two different constructions share these two words, and the test is whether the object can move. Turn on the light → turn the light on → turn it on: the object slides, so on is a particle, the pair is a phrasal verb, and it belongs in the discussion of phrasal verbs rather than in this file. The case turns on one question → ✗ turns one question on: the object cannot move, so on is a fixed preposition and the pattern belongs here. The same test separates two more: turned on him means attacked him, turned him on means aroused him — word order carries the whole difference. In the analytic sense the near-synonyms are hinge on, depend on, and rest on, of which turn on is the most legal.

Depend on one decisive point, or suddenly attack someone.

  1. The whole appeal turns on a single sentence in the contract.
  2. It turns on whether she knew the account was overdrawn.
  3. The dog turned on its handler without warning.
  4. Half the neighborhood turned on him after the vote.
  5. Everything turns on getting the inspection done this week.
  6. Elections in that district turn on turnout, not persuasion.
See also
depend on · rest on · rely on · turn to · on

turn to#

Pronunciation
/ˈtɜrn tu/ · TURN too · weak /ˈtɜrn tə/
Русский
обращаться к (за помощью); приниматься за что-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase — a person, an occupation, a substance, a page, a topic — or a gerund (turned to selling insurance). Never a to-infinitive in this pattern: he turned to write is a different sentence, in which to write is a purpose infinitive and he physically turned around.
Pattern
turn to [someone] for [help] · turn to [an occupation, a habit, a substance] · turn to [verb-ing] · [a conversation] turn to [a topic] · turn to [page 214]
Contrast
Turn to someone seeks help; turn on someone attacks them — one letter, opposite meanings. Around them: turn into is transformation (turned into a decent manager), turn against is a change of allegiance (the party turned against him), and turn down is a separable particle (turned the offer down, turned it down), which is why it is a phrasal verb and this is not. Watch the purpose-infinitive trap: he turned to writing made writing his work, he turned to write only says he swiveled in his chair.

Go to someone or something for help, or take something up.

  1. When the second round of layoffs hit, she turned to her sister.
  2. He turned to teaching after fifteen years in restaurants.
  3. The conversation turned to money about ten minutes in.
  4. Turn to page 214 and read the second paragraph aloud.
  5. A lot of people in that plant turned to painkillers.
  6. Who do you turn to when the person you'd normally ask is the problem?
See also
turn on · resort to · refer to · rely on · to

vary with#

Pronunciation
/ˈvɛri wɪθ/ · VAIR-ee with · weak /ˈvɛri wəθ/
Русский
меняться в зависимости от — изменяться вместе с чем-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral; heavily used in technical, scientific, and consumer writing (results may vary)
Complement
noun phrase naming what the thing moves in step with — a season, a temperature, an income. No gerund (✗ varies with raising the temperature; say varies with temperature), no infinitive, no clause.
Pattern
[X] vary with [Y] · vary by [a category] · vary from [X] to [Y] · vary in [a dimension] · vary from [a norm]
Contrast
Five prepositions carve five different claims. With names what it moves in step with (demand varies with the weather); by names the categories it differs across (prices vary by state); in names the dimension of difference (the panels vary in thickness); from … to gives the range (varies from ten to forty minutes); and vary from alone means depart from a standard (varies from the specification). Against neighbors, depend on claims determination rather than covariation, correlate with is the statistical and symmetric version, and fluctuate takes no complement at all.

Change in step with something else.

  1. The dosage varies with body weight.
  2. Turnout varies with the weather more than anyone likes to admit.
  3. Shipping cost varies with distance and by carrier.
  4. Interest on the note varies with the prime rate.
  5. Yields vary with soil temperature at planting.
  6. How long an appeal takes varies with the circuit.
See also
depend on · differ from · result from · with

vest in#

Pronunciation
/ˈvɛst ɪn/ · VEST in · weak /ˈvɛst ən/
Русский
закреплять (власть, право, титул) за кем-то — юр./офиц.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
legal — constitutions, trusts, corporate charters, and equity grants; an American meets it in the phrase all legislative powers herein granted shall be vested in a Congress, not in conversation
Complement
noun phrase naming the person or body that receives the power, the title, or the right. No gerund (✗ vested in managing the fund), no infinitive, no clause. The passive is the standard form: authority is vested in the board.
Pattern
[a power / a right / title] be vested in [someone] · vest [a power] in [someone] · vest [someone] with [a power] · [a right] vest — intransitive
Two frames say the same thing with the arguments swapped
vest authority in the board and vest the board with authority, exactly like provide X to Y against provide Y with X. Watch the intransitive: in the options vest in four years, in is the ordinary time preposition and nothing is receiving anything — that sentence answers when, not who, and is not this pattern. Among neighbors, confer on grants, devolve on is power passing downward or by default, and reside in and rest with say where authority already sits rather than how it got there. Do not read across to invest in, which in ordinary American English means putting money into something.

Place a power, a right, or a title formally in someone's hands.

  1. All legislative powers herein granted are vested in a Congress of the United States.
  2. Title to the property vested in the trust on the date of the transfer.
  3. The charter vests final authority in the board of trustees.
  4. Discretion to waive the penalty is vested in the commissioner.
  5. The statute vests the agency with rulemaking power.
  6. On his death, the remainder vested in his two daughters.
See also
rest with · reside in · invest in · belong to · in

vote for, vote against#

Pronunciation
/ˈvoʊt fɔr/ · VOHT for · weak /ˈvoʊt fɚ/
Русский
голосовать за (for) или против (against)
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase naming a candidate or measure — no gerund (✗ vote for raising the fee; say vote to raise the fee or vote for the increase). Vote also takes a bare to-infinitive with no preposition (voted to adjourn) and a that-clause in formal minutes (voted that the matter be tabled).
Pattern
vote for [a candidate, a measure] · vote against [something] · vote on [a question] · vote to [verb] · vote in [an election] · vote [someone] in / out
Contrast
Four prepositions and an infinitive, each with its own job. Vote for and vote against take sides; vote on names the question being decided, not the side (the council votes on the ordinance Tuesday); vote in names the election; and vote to do names the action decided. ✗ vote to a candidate is an error. Note the American particles: vote someone in, vote someone out, vote down a measure — all separable and all preposition-free.

Express a choice in an election or a formal decision.

  1. She voted for the bond measure and against everything else on the ballot.
  2. Two members voted against the settlement and one abstained.
  3. The board votes on the budget at the March meeting.
  4. They voted to adjourn before anyone could raise it again.
  5. He's never once voted in a primary.
  6. The district voted him out after a single term.
See also
decide on · agree on · choose between · qualify for · object to

vouch for#

Pronunciation
/ˈvaʊtʃ fɔr/ · VOWCH for · weak /ˈvaʊtʃ fər/
Русский
ручаться за — подтверждать надёжность по личному знанию
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral — everyday spoken English for people, and ordinary formal English for facts
Complement
noun phrase — a person, a fact, a quality (vouch for the accuracy of the transcript). A gerund is grammatical but stiff (vouch for his having been there; most speakers say vouch for that), and there is no to-infinitive (✗ vouch for to be accurate). The preposition is obligatory: ✗ vouch someone. A that-clause is marginal — say I can vouch for the fact that… or switch to attest or swear.
Pattern
vouch for [a person] · vouch for [a fact / a quality] · can't vouch for [something]
Contrast
Vouch for puts your own credit behind someone or something. Testify to does the same under oath, attest to does it in writing and often with a signature, and guarantee promises a result and takes no preposition at all (guarantee the work, ✗ guarantee for the work). Answer for is what comes afterward, when the thing you vouched for goes wrong.

Say from your own knowledge that someone or something can be trusted.

  1. I've known her twenty years and I'll vouch for her.
  2. Nobody at the agency would vouch for the numbers in the draft.
  3. I can vouch for the coffee; I can't vouch for the parking.
  4. Two colleagues vouched for his whereabouts that evening.
  5. The notary vouched for the identity of both signers.
  6. He vouched for the contractor, and then spent a year regretting it.
See also
testify to · stand up for · count on · rely on · for

wait for, wait on#

Pronunciation
/ˈweɪt fɔr/ · WAYT for · weak /ˈweɪt fɚ/
Русский
ждать (for); обслуживать (on); wait on в значении ждать — разг., амер.
Type
verb + preposition
Register
wait for is neutral; wait on meaning await is informal and regional in American English
Complement
for takes a noun phrase, or a person plus a to-infinitive (wait for her to finish — this is the one place an infinitive is correct in this pattern). A gerund after for is impossible: ✗ wait for finishing. On takes a person: wait on tables in the serving sense. Await is transitive and takes no preposition at all.
Pattern
wait for [someone / something] · wait for [someone] to [verb] · wait to [verb] · wait on [a customer, a table] — serve · wait on [something] — informal for wait for · await [something] — formal, no preposition · can't wait to [verb]
Contrast
Wait on has two lives in American English. The standard sense is serving: she waited on tables through college, the clerk will wait on you next. The other — I'm waiting on the bus meaning waiting for it — began as Southern and Midland American usage, has spread nationally in speech and in business writing (waiting on approval), and is still flagged in formal edited prose, where wait for is safe. Note await, which takes a direct object and no preposition: await further instructions, ✗ await for instructions. And wait for someone to do something is one of the very few prepositional patterns that takes an infinitive, licensed by the person in between.

Stay where you are, or delay acting, until something happens.

PatternWhat it meansRegister
wait for [thing]await itStandard everywhere
wait for [person] to [verb]await their action — infinitiveStandard
wait on [person]serve themStandard
wait on [thing]wait for itOriginally Southern/Midland U.S.; common in speech, avoided in formal prose
wait to dodelay actingStandard
await [thing]no prepositionFormal
  1. We waited for almost an hour and nobody ever came out.
  2. She's waiting for the inspector to sign off.
  3. He waited on tables at a diner on Route 9 for six years.
  4. They're still waiting on approval from the county.
  5. Don't wait for perfect; send the draft.
  6. I can't wait to see the finished porch.
  7. The committee awaits the final report.
See also
look forward to · hope for · count on · search for · depend on

warn about, warn against#

Pronunciation
/ˈwɔrn əˌbaʊt/ · WORN uh-bowt · weak /ˈwɔrn əbət/
Русский
предупреждать — about об опасности, against чтобы чего-то не делали
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
about and of take a noun phrase naming a danger — of is the more formal of the two. against takes a noun phrase or a gerund and means advise not to (warned against signing anything) — never a to-infinitive after against. Warn separately takes a person plus a to-infinitive (warned her to leave, warned him not to sign) and a that-clause (warned that the road was flooded), neither with a preposition.
Pattern
warn [someone] about [a danger] · warn [someone] of [a danger] — formal · warn [someone] against [something / verb-ing] · warn [someone] to / not to [verb] · warn that [clause] · warn [someone] off
Contrast
About and of describe the danger; against prescribes avoiding it. He warned us about the ice tells you the ice is there; he warned us against driving tells you not to. The two often coexist: warned residents about the surge and against staying. Note that warn takes the person directly — ✗ warn to someone — and that the negative infinitive is warned her not to sign, never ✗ warned her to not sign in careful prose. The noun keeps about / of: a warning about the ice, a warning of severe weather.

Tell someone about a danger, or tell them not to do something.

  1. The ranger warned us about the washout on the lower trail.
  2. Her attorney warned her against saying anything on the record.
  3. The label warns of a risk that affects about one user in fifty thousand.
  4. He warned everyone to leave before the tide turned.
  5. Two inspectors had warned about the wiring the previous spring.
  6. The forecast warns of freezing rain after midnight.
See also
remind of · discourage from · protect from · explain to

wish for#

Pronunciation
/ˈwɪʃ fɔr/ · WISH for · weak /ˈwɪʃ fɚ/
Русский
желать чего-то — хотеть, часто маловероятного
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
for takes a noun phrase — no gerund and no to-infinitive after the preposition (✗ wish for going, ✗ wish for to go). Wish separately takes a bare to-infinitive (wish to speak with the manager — formal) and a past-tense clause for unreal situations: I wish I had more time, I wish she were here — note the subjunctive were.
Pattern
wish for [a noun phrase] · wish to [verb] — formal · wish [someone] [something] · wish [clause with past tense] · wish [someone] well · as you wish
Contrast
The three frames are not interchangeable. Wish for a raise is longing; wish to speak is a formal way of saying want; I wish I had a raise is counterfactual, and the past tense is doing the work rather than any preposition. ✗ I wish that I have more time is wrong — it must be had. The double-object frame is fixed and preposition-free: wish you luck, wish him well, wish them a good trip. And note the proverb, which is the pattern's most quoted use: be careful what you wish for.

Want something, often something unlikely.

  1. Everyone in the district was wishing for one more snow day.
  2. Be careful what you wish for.
  3. She wished for a quiet Christmas and got four houseguests.
  4. I wish to register a complaint about the fee schedule.
  5. I wish I had asked more questions before signing.
  6. They wished him well and never called again.
See also
hope for · long for · dream of · ask for

work on, work for, work with#

Pronunciation
/ˈwɜrk ɑn/ · WURK on · weak /ˈwɜrk ən/
Русский
работать — on над чем-то, for на кого-то, with с кем-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
on takes a noun phrase or a gerund (working on getting the permit) — never a to-infinitive. for takes an employer, a client, or a cause. with takes a colleague, a client, or a material. at takes a place of work or an effort; in takes a field or an industry. None of them takes a clause.
Pattern
work on [a project / verb-ing] · work for [an employer / a cause] · work with [a person / a material] · work at [a place / something requiring effort] · work in [a field, an industry] · work as [a role] · work out [a solution]
Contrast
Six prepositions, and each answers a different question. Work on = what you are building or improving (working on the second draft). Work for = who pays you (works for the county). Work with = who or what is alongside you (works with kids, works with mahogany). Work at = where, or how hard (works at the hospital, has to work at it). Work in = which field (works in public health). Work as = which role (works as a paralegal). Note that work with a person and work for a person are very different claims about a hierarchy, and people are sensitive about which one you use.

Do a job, or apply effort to something.

  1. She's been working on the same manuscript for six years.
  2. He works for a firm that does nothing but municipal water.
  3. I've worked with her on two projects and would again.
  4. They're working on getting the variance before the weather turns.
  5. She works in public health and at a clinic three days a week.
  6. Marriage takes work, and he was willing to work at it.
See also
concentrate on · specialize in · consult with · apply for · participate in · with

worry about#

Pronunciation
/ˈwɜri əˌbaʊt/ · WUR-ee uh-bowt · weak /ˈwɜri əbət/
Русский
беспокоиться о — тревожиться из-за чего-то
Type
verb + preposition
Register
neutral
Complement
noun phrase, gerund (worried about missing the flight), or wh-clause — never a to-infinitive (✗ worry about to miss it). Worry also takes a that-clause with no preposition: worried that the check would bounce. Reflexive worry yourself and transitive it worries me need no preposition either.
Pattern
worry about [something / verb-ing / wh-clause] · be worried about [something] · be worried by [something] · worry that [clause] · [something] worry [someone] · Don't worry about it.
Contrast
Worried about is your state; worried by is the thing's effect on you. I'm worried about the numbers describes ongoing anxiety you carry; I'm worried by the numbers names the numbers as what disturbed you, and is closer to troubled by or alarmed by. Both are correct and about is far more common. Worry for is the marginal third member — it survives in I worry for her, which sounds concerned on someone's behalf rather than anxious, and it will not substitute for about anywhere else: ✗ I worry for the deadline. ✗ Worry of is not English at all. The fixed dismissal is don't worry about it.

Feel anxious about something.

PatternWhat it meansExample
worry about [thing]carry ongoing anxiety about itShe worries about money.
be worried about [thing]be in that state nowI'm worried about the numbers.
be worried by [thing]the thing is what disturbed youI'm worried by how fast it changed.
worry for [person]be concerned on their behalf — marginalI worry for her.
worry that [clause]no prepositionThey worried that it would rain.
[thing] worry [person]transitive — no prepositionThe cough worries me.
  1. She worries about her mother driving at night.
  2. Nobody at that level worries about the parking.
  3. He's worried about missing the connection in Atlanta.
  4. I'm worried by how quickly the estimate changed.
  5. They worried that the inspection would turn up something expensive.
  6. Don't worry about the dishes; I'll get them.
See also
care about · think about · complain about · hope for · forget about

Verbs that take no preposition at all#

The other half of this file. Every verb below takes a direct object with nothing in front of it, and every one of them attracts a preposition that does not belong there — usually because the corresponding verb in Spanish, French, Portuguese, Italian, Russian, Hindi, or Arabic does take one, or because the English noun takes one and the verb gets tarred with it. A discussion about the budget is correct and ✗ discuss about the budget is not; an approach to the problem is correct and ✗ approach to the problem is not. Learn these as a set, because they fail as a set.

Several of them do have a real prepositional twin with a different meaning — enter into an agreement, attend to a patient, answer for a decision, reach for a glass. Those are noted in each entry, and they are the reason the errors survive: the wrong version sounds like something you have heard.

answer#

Pronunciation
/ˈænsɚ/ · AN-ser · weak /ˈænsɚ/
Русский
отвечать на что-то — прямое дополнение без предлога: answer the question
Type
verb + direct object (no preposition)
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct object naming the question, the phone, the door, or the person — no preposition in that sense: ✗ answer to the question, ✗ answer to my email. The genuine answer to means be accountable to a person, and answer for means take responsibility for a thing — both are real, and both mean something else.
Pattern
answer [a question, the phone, the door, a letter] · answer [someone] · answer to [a boss, a board] — be accountable · answer for [one's conduct] · answer back · the answer to [a question] — the noun takes to
Contrast
Answer to the question is wrong for replying, and right for something else. He answered the question is a reply; He answers to the board is a reporting line; He'll answer for that decision is accountability. Meanwhile the noun takes to — the answer to question 4 — which is exactly why the verb attracts it. Also correct: reply to and respond to both take the preposition, so reply to my email and respond to my email are right while ✗ answer to my email is not.

Reply to something, or be accountable to someone.

  1. Nobody answered my question, so I asked it again.
  2. Answer the phone; it's been ringing all morning.
  3. She answered every email the same day, which nobody asked her to do.
  4. The inspector general answers to Congress, not to the agency.
  5. Somebody is going to have to answer for the missing invoices.
  6. The answer to the third question is on the back page.
See also
react to · explain to · refer to

approach#

Pronunciation
/əˈproʊtʃ/ · uh-PROHCH · weak /əˈproʊtʃ/
Русский
подходить к; приступать к — без предлога: approach the house
Type
verb + direct object (no preposition)
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct object naming a place, a person, a number, or a problem — no preposition: ✗ approach to the intersection, ✗ approach to the problem as a verb. The noun takes to (a new approach to the problem), and that is where the error comes from.
Pattern
approach [a place, a person, a figure, a problem] · approach [someone] about [a matter] · an approach to [a problem] — the noun · approach (intransitive): winter is approaching
Contrast
The noun takes to; the verb takes nothing. Her approach to the problem was unusual is correct, and ✗ she approached to the problem is not. The only preposition the verb takes is about, naming the matter raised with a person: he approached her about the vacancy. Note the numeric use, which is common in American reporting — costs approaching two million dollars — and the intransitive one, which needs no object at all: as the deadline approached.

Come near to something, or begin to deal with it.

  1. Two deputies approached the vehicle from opposite sides.
  2. Enrollment is approaching nine hundred for the first time since 2009.
  3. She approached the problem the way an engineer would.
  4. He approached the dean about a leave and was told to put it in writing.
  5. The storm approached faster than the forecast had allowed for.
  6. Her approach to teaching writing has almost no rules in it.
See also
arrive at · reach · enter · react to · ask about

attend#

Pronunciation
/əˈtɛnd/ · uh-TEND · weak /əˈtɛnd/
Русский
посещать, присутствовать на — без предлога: attend the meeting
Type
verb + direct object (no preposition)
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a direct object naming an event, an institution, or a service — no preposition: ✗ attend to the meeting, ✗ attend at the conference. The genuine attend to means deal with or take care of, and attend on means wait on — both are real and neither means being present.
Pattern
attend [a meeting, a school, a funeral] · attend to [a matter, a patient] — deal with · attend on / upon [someone] — formal, wait on · attendance at [an event] — the noun takes at
Contrast
Attend the meeting is being there; attend to the meeting is handling the arrangements — and one of those is almost never what a learner means. The noun goes its own way again: attendance at the meeting, an attendee at the conference. Compare participate in, which asserts more than presence, and be present at, which asserts exactly presence. In American English attend is also the standard verb for enrollment: she attended Oberlin, no preposition.

Be present at something.

  1. About forty people attended the hearing and nine spoke.
  2. He attended three different high schools in four years.
  3. She couldn't attend the funeral and has never forgiven the airline.
  4. The nurse attended to him before anyone had filled out a form.
  5. Attendance at the workshop is optional but counted.
  6. Please attend to the roof before the freeze.
See also
participate in · arrive at · listen to · take care of · reach

contact#

Pronunciation
/ˈkɑntækt/ · KON-takt · weak /ˈkɑntækt/
Русский
связываться с — без предлога: contact me, не ✗ contact with me
Type
verb + direct object (no preposition)
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct object naming the person or office — no preposition: ✗ contact with the office, ✗ contact to me. The same is true of call, phone, email, text, and write with a person: ✗ phone to her, ✗ email to me. The noun takes with (in contact with the family), which is where the error starts.
Pattern
contact [someone] · call / phone / email / text [someone] · write [someone] — American; British adds to · be in contact with [someone] · get in touch with [someone] · reach out to [someone]
Contrast
Contact with someone is a noun phrase, not a verb phrase. I contacted her is right; ✗ I contacted with her is not. The whole group behaves the same way: call me, email me, text me, phone the clinic — all bare objects. The one genuine American-British split is write: Americans say write me when you get there, and British English requires write to me. Both are correct in their own varieties, and write to is safe everywhere. When you want a preposition, use the noun frames: in contact with, in touch with.

Get in touch with someone.

  1. Contact the registrar's office before the deadline, not after.
  2. She contacted three references and heard back from one.
  3. Call me when you land.
  4. He emailed everyone on the list twice in one afternoon.
  5. Write me when you get settled.
  6. They've been in contact with the family since Tuesday.
See also
reach · explain to · consult with · talk to · hear from

discuss#

Pronunciation
/dɪˈskʌs/ · dih-SKUSS · weak /dɪˈskəs/
Русский
обсуждать что-то — без предлога: discuss the plan, не ✗ discuss about
Type
verb + direct object (no preposition)
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct object, a gerund (discussed selling the building), or a wh-clause (discussed how to pay for it) — and no preposition before any of them: ✗ discuss about the budget, ✗ discuss on the budget, ✗ discuss of the budget. There is no that-clause: ✗ discuss that we should move.
Pattern
discuss [a topic] · discuss [verb-ing] · discuss [wh-clause] · discuss [something] with [someone] · a discussion about / of [something] — the noun does take a preposition
Contrast
Discuss about is the single most common preposition error in English, and it comes from the noun: a discussion about the merger is perfectly correct, so the preposition leaks back into the verb where it has no business. The only preposition discuss takes is with, and it names the other person, not the topic: discuss the merger with counsel. If you want a preposition on the topic, change the verb — talk about it, speak about it, comment on it, or have a discussion about it.

Talk something over with someone.

  1. We discussed the budget for two hours and settled nothing.
  2. They discussed selling the building and voted to refinance instead.
  3. Let's discuss it with the whole team on Monday.
  4. The board never discussed how the shortfall would be covered.
  5. She discussed her options with an attorney before responding.
  6. Nothing in that email is worth discussing.
See also
talk about · comment on · consult with · argue about

emphasize#

Pronunciation
/ˈɛmfəˌsaɪz/ · EM-fuh-size · weak /ˈɛmfəˌsaɪz/
Русский
подчёркивать, акцентировать — без предлога, не ✗ emphasize on
Type
verb + direct object (no preposition)
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a direct object or a that-clause — no preposition: ✗ emphasize on the deadline, ✗ emphasize about safety. The noun takes on (emphasis on safety) and the phrasal frame is place / put emphasis on, which is where the error comes from. Stress behaves identically: stress the deadline, ✗ stress on the deadline.
Pattern
emphasize [something] · emphasize that [clause] · stress [something] · stress that [clause] · place / put emphasis on [something] · with an emphasis on [something]
Contrast
Emphasize on is wrong, and an emphasis on is right, which is the whole trap in one sentence. The same split governs stress (verb, no preposition; noun, on), highlight, underscore, and underline, all transitive. If you want the preposition, use the noun: the training places heavy emphasis on de-escalation. Note that emphasize also takes a clause directly — she emphasized that attendance was voluntary — with no on anywhere in sight.

Give special importance to something.

  1. The memo emphasized the deadline three separate times.
  2. She emphasized that nothing had been decided yet.
  3. The program emphasizes fieldwork over coursework.
  4. He stressed the difference between a recommendation and a requirement.
  5. The curriculum places heavy emphasis on writing.
  6. I want to emphasize how narrow the finding actually is.
See also
concentrate on · point to · comment on · insist on · explain to

enter#

Pronunciation
/ˈɛntɚ/ · EN-ter · weak /ˈɛntɚ/
Русский
входить в — без предлога: enter the room; enter into о договоре
Type
verb + direct object (no preposition)
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct object naming a place, a state, or a stage — no preposition in the literal sense: ✗ enter into the room, ✗ enter to the building. The figurative enter into is a genuine and separate pattern for agreements and processes: enter into a contract, enter into negotiations.
Pattern
enter [a room, a building, a country] · enter [a contest, a phase, one's thirties] · enter into [an agreement, negotiations, a discussion] · enter [data] into [a system] · enter (intransitive, stage direction)
Contrast
Enter into the room is wrong; enter the room is right. The confusion is fed by the fact that enter into is correct for abstractions — you enter into a contract, an alliance, a partnership, or a debate. The rule of thumb: if you could physically walk in, drop the preposition. Note also enter meaning key in, which takes into or in: enter the figures into the spreadsheet. And the intransitive stage direction Enter Hamlet is the fossil that gives the verb its theatrical feel.

Go into a place, or become party to an agreement.

  1. He entered the building through the loading dock.
  2. Three hundred people entered the contest and two read the rules.
  3. The company entered into a consent decree rather than go to trial.
  4. She entered her thirties without any of the alarm she'd been promised.
  5. Enter the confirmation number into the second field.
  6. The negotiations the union entered into lasted eleven weeks.
See also
arrive at · reach · approach · participate in · into

lack#

Pronunciation
/ˈlæk/ · LAK · weak /ˈlæk/
Русский
не иметь, недоставать — без предлога: lacks detail, не ✗ lacks of detail
Type
verb + direct object (no preposition)
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a direct object naming what is missing — no preposition: ✗ lack of experience as a verb, ✗ lack in experience. The noun takes of (a lack of experience) and the adjective takes in (lacking in experience), and those two are the source of every error made with the verb.
Pattern
lack [something] · be lacking · be lacking in [a quality] · a lack of [something] · for lack of [something]
Contrast
Three parts of speech, three different constructions, and only one of them takes of. The proposal lacks detail (verb, bare object), the proposal shows a lack of detail (noun, of), the proposal is lacking in detail (participial adjective, in). ✗ The proposal lacks of detail mixes the first two and is wrong. Note that lack is stative and resists the progressive: ✗ is lacking experience is odd where lacks experience is right, though is lacking on its own (meaning absent) is fine: what's lacking is a budget.

Not have something that is needed or expected.

  1. The plan lacks any mechanism for enforcement.
  2. She has never lacked confidence.
  3. The building lacks a second exit, which is why it can't be permitted.
  4. What the argument lacks is a single piece of evidence.
  5. It's a careful report, though lacking in imagination.
  6. The case was dropped for lack of evidence.
See also
consist of · deprive of · suffer from · provide with · for lack of

marry#

Pronunciation
/ˈmɛri/ · MAIR-ee · weak /ˈmɛri/
Русский
жениться на, выходить замуж за — без предлога: marry her, не ✗ marry with
Type
verb + direct object (no preposition)
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct object naming the person — no preposition: ✗ marry with her, ✗ marry to him. The adjectival frame is where the preposition lives, and it is to, never with: married to a surgeon, ✗ married with a surgeon. Get married to follows the same rule.
Pattern
marry [someone] · marry (intransitive) · get married · get married to [someone] · be married to [someone] · marry [a couple] — what the officiant does · married with children — a different with entirely
Contrast
Married with her is one of the most persistent errors in English, because Spanish casarse con and French se marier avec both use the equivalent of with. English uses no preposition for the verb and to for the state: He married her in 1998 and He's been married to her since 1998. The one place with is correct is a completely different structure — married with two kids means married and having two kids, where with attaches to the children, not the spouse. Note also the officiant's transitive use: a justice of the peace married them.

Become someone's husband or wife.

  1. She married a man she met in a bus station in Lincoln.
  2. They got married in a courthouse and had the party a year later.
  3. He's been married to the same woman for thirty-four years.
  4. Her sister married young and stayed married.
  5. The couple were married by a friend who got ordained online.
  6. They're married with three kids and a dog nobody planned on.
See also
introduce to · belong to · separate from · share with · to

oppose#

Pronunciation
/əˈpoʊz/ · uh-POHZ · weak /əˈpoʊz/
Русский
выступать против, противостоять — без предлога, не ✗ oppose to
Type
verb + direct object (no preposition)
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a direct object naming what you are against — no preposition: ✗ oppose to the merger, ✗ oppose against the bill. A gerund can be that object, still with no preposition: opposed extending the deadline. The preposition appears only once the verb has become the adjective opposed, and then it is to plus a noun or a gerund: opposed to the merger, opposed to extending the deadline, never ✗ opposed to extend the deadline.
Pattern
oppose [a plan, a bill, a nominee] · oppose [verb-ing] · be opposed to [noun phrase / verb-ing] · as opposed to [noun phrase] — a fixed comparative phrase
Contrast
Oppose to is wrong and opposed to is right — one letter apart, and the difference is a part of speech. The verb is transitive like support, its opposite: she supports the levy, she opposes the levy, neither with a preposition. Once you reach for be, you have switched to the adjective and the to becomes obligatory — and it is a preposition, so a verb after it takes -ing, which puts opposed to in the look forward to family. Compare object to, which is a verb that does take to, so object to the merger and oppose the merger say the same thing with opposite structures. Note the fixed phrase as opposed to, which means rather than and behaves as a complex preposition of its own.

Be against something, and act against it.

  1. Every county commissioner opposed the rezoning.
  2. She opposes raising the retirement age.
  3. The union is opposed to reopening the contract midterm.
  4. He opposed the nomination for reasons he never explained.
  5. Two of the three trustees are opposed to the sale.
  6. They want a flat fee as opposed to an hourly rate.
See also
object to · disapprove of · protest · agree with · argue against · opposed to

protest#

Pronunciation
/prəˈtɛst/ · pruh-TEST · weak /prəˈtɛst/
Русский
протестовать против — в амер. англ. без предлога: protest the ruling
Type
verb + direct object (no preposition)
Register
neutral — the transitive form is characteristically American
Complement
a direct object naming what you object to, or a that-clause (protested that he had never been told) — in American English, no preposition: protest the ruling, protest the war. British English requires protest against the ruling. Protest to names the person you complain to, and is correct in both varieties.
Pattern
protest [a decision, a policy, a verdict] — American · protest against [something] — British, and also used in the U.S. · protest to [an official] · protest that [clause] · protest (intransitive) · under protest
Contrast
This is a genuine transatlantic split, and the American form is the bare transitive. Thousands protested the ruling is standard in the United States and reads as an error to some British ears, where protested against the ruling is required. Both are current in American writing, with the bare form dominant in news copy. Note the third pattern, which is neither: protest to the commissioner names the person you complain to. And protest that introduces a denial (he protested that nobody had told him), which is where protest too much comes from.

Object publicly to something.

  1. Several hundred people protested the closure outside the county building.
  2. Students protested the tuition increase for most of the spring.
  3. She protested the charge in writing and got half of it reversed.
  4. In Britain they would say the crowd protested against the bill.
  5. He protested to the commissioner and was told to file a form.
  6. He protested that nobody had ever explained the deadline to him.
  7. She signed it under protest.
See also
complain to · object to · argue with · comply with · vote against

reach#

Pronunciation
/ˈritʃ/ · REECH · weak /ˈritʃ/
Русский
достигать, добираться до; дозвониться до — без предлога: reach Denver
Type
verb + direct object (no preposition)
Register
neutral
Complement
a direct object naming a place, a person, a level, or a decision — no preposition: ✗ reach to the summit, ✗ reach at the office. The genuine reach for means extend a hand toward something, and reach out to means make contact — both are real and both mean something else.
Pattern
reach [a place, a person, a figure, an agreement] · reach for [something] · reach out to [someone] · reach into [a bag, a pocket] · within reach of [something] — the noun takes of
Contrast
Reach the airport, not ✗ reach to the airport. The verb is transitive for arriving, contacting, and attaining: reach Denver, reach me at this number, reach a verdict, reach ninety degrees. Reach for is the physical gesture (reached for the salt), and reach out to is the American business idiom for making contact — now so common that it has largely displaced contact in email. Compare arrive at and arrive in, which do take prepositions, and get to, which takes to: three ways of saying the same thing with three different requirements.

Arrive at something, get in touch with someone, or attain a level.

  1. We reached the trailhead about twenty minutes before dark.
  2. You can reach me at the office until five.
  3. The jury reached a verdict on the second day.
  4. Temperatures reached a hundred and four in the valley.
  5. He reached for the railing and missed it.
  6. Somebody from the office will reach out to you this week.
See also
arrive at · approach · contact · enter

resemble#

Pronunciation
/rɪˈzɛmbəl/ · rih-ZEM-bul · weak /rɪˈzɛmbəl/
Русский
быть похожим на — без предлога: resemble his father, не ✗ resemble to
Type
verb + direct object (no preposition)
Register
neutral to formal
Complement
a direct object — no preposition: ✗ resemble to her mother, ✗ resemble with her mother. Resemble is stative and does not take the progressive: ✗ is resembling her mother. The noun takes to: a resemblance to her mother.
Pattern
resemble [someone / something] · bear a resemblance to [someone] · take after [a relative] — the idiomatic alternative · look like [someone] — the everyday one
Contrast
Resemble to is an error imported from French ressembler à and Spanish parecerse a. The verb takes a bare object; only the noun takes to — a striking resemblance to her mother. In American speech the verb is rare: people say she looks like her mother or she takes after her mother, and resemble is reserved for writing. Note the useful passive-free construction X and Y resemble each other, which avoids choosing a direction.

Look or be like something else.

  1. She resembles her mother more every year.
  2. The finished building resembled the rendering in almost no respect.
  3. His second novel resembles the first one a little too closely.
  4. Nothing on the plate resembled what the menu had described.
  5. The two dialects resemble each other more than either resembles the standard.
  6. The child bears a strong resemblance to his grandfather.
See also
compare to · differ from · separate from
